The formation of gradient-driven singular structures of codimension one and two in two-dimensions: The case study of ferronematics Part II: Refined structure of the energy-concentration set
Giacomo Canevari, Federico Luigi Dipasquale, Bianca Stroffolini

Abstract
In this paper, we continue our study, started in [ 9 ] , of a two-dimensional variational model for ferronematics — composite materials formed by dispersing magnetic nanoparticles into a liquid crystal matrix. The model features two coupled order parameters: a Landau-de Gennes 𝐐 \mathbf{Q} -tensor for the liquid crystal component and a magnetisation vector field 𝐌 \mathbf{M} , both of them governed by a Ginzburg-Landau-type energy. The energy includes a singular coupling term favouring alignment between 𝐐 \mathbf{Q} and 𝐌 \mathbf{M} . We analyse the asymptotic behaviour of (not necessarily minimizing) critical points as a small parameter ε \varepsilon tends to zero. While in [ 9 ] we showed that the (rescaled) energy density for the 𝐐 \mathbf{Q} -component concentrates, to leading order, on a finite number of singular points, in this paper we prove the energy density for the 𝐌 \mathbf{M} -component concentrates along a one-dimensional rectifiable set. Moreover, we prove that the curvature of the singular set for the 𝐌 \mathbf{M} -component (technically, the first variation of the associated varifold) is concentrated on a finite number of points, i.e. the singular set for the 𝐐 \mathbf{Q} -component. Crucial to our arguments will be the energy estimates and compactness results proved in [ 9 ] . Keywords: Ginzburg-Landau functional, Allen-Cahn equation, vectorial problems, topological singularities, rectifiable sets. 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification: 35Q56 ⋅ \cdot 76A15 ⋅ \cdot 49Q15 ⋅ \cdot 26B30



In this paper, we continue our study, started in
[
9
]
, of
the asymptotic behaviour of critical points of the energy functional

\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, defined in (
1
) below, that
has been proposed in the physical literature
[
7
]
as a simplified model for
two-dimensional ferronematics. Ferronematics are
a class of composite materials obtained as suspensions
of magnetic nanoparticles in a nematic liquid crystal host
[
8
,
18
]
.
According to the approach proposed in
[
7
]
, ferronematics are described by two order parameters.
The orientation of the liquid crystal molecules
is described by the Landau-de Gennes
\mathbf{Q}
-tensor,
which is a map from the physical domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}

to the space
\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}
of
2\times 2
, symmetric, real matrices
with trace equal to zero. Nonzero values of
\mathbf{Q}
correspond
to liquid crystal configurations with a well-defined direction of
molecular alignment, represented by the
eigenspace of
\mathbf{Q}
associated with the positive eigenvalue,
while
\mathbf{Q}=0
indicates an isotropic state,
where all the directions of molecular alignment are equally likely.
The distribution of magnetic nanoparticles is
described by the average magnetisation vector,

\mathbf{M}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The system is governed by a
free energy functional which depends on both
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{M}
:

(1)

\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{M}):=\int_{\Omega}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{M})\right\}{\mathrm{d}}x,

where
\varepsilon
is a non-dimensional parameter.
The interaction between the liquid crystal host and
the magnetic inclusions is mediated by the potential
f_{\varepsilon}
,
which takes the form

(2)

f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{M}):=\frac{1}{4}\left(1-\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left(1-\left|\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}-\varepsilon\beta\,\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{M}\cdot\mathbf{M}+\kappa_{\varepsilon}.

Here
\beta>0
is given and
\kappa_{\varepsilon}
is an additive
constant, depending on
\varepsilon
and
\beta
only,
uniquely determined by imposing that
\inf f_{\varepsilon}=0
.
For positive values of
\beta
, the potential
f_{\varepsilon}

promotes alignment between the liquid crystal molecules
and the magnetisation vector.
As in
[
9
]
, we consider two alternative sets of boundary conditions
for
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
.
One option is to impose
Dirichlet boundary conditions for both
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
:

(3)

\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}},\quad\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{bd}}\qquad\textrm{on }\partial\Omega.

In this case, the boundary data
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\in C^{1}(\partial\Omega,\,\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2})
,

\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{bd}}\in C^{1}(\partial\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
are
\varepsilon
-independent
maps that satisfy

(4)

\left|\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{bd}}\right|=\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right)^{1/2},\qquad\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}=\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{bd}}\otimes\mathbf{M}_{\mathrm{bd}}}{\sqrt{2}\beta+1}-\frac{\mathbf{I}}{2}\right)

at any point of
\partial\Omega
.

Alternatively to (
3
)-(
4
), we consider
‘mixed’ boundary conditions, i.e. Dirichlet boundary
conditions for
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and homogeneous Neumann boundary
conditions for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
:

(5)

\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}},\quad\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}=0\qquad\textrm{on }\partial\Omega,

where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is the exterior unit normal to
\partial\Omega
.
We assume the boundary datum
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}

(independent from
\varepsilon
and) of the form

(6)

\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}=\sqrt{2}\left(\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{bd}}\otimes\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{bd}}-\frac{\mathbf{I}}{2}\right)

on
\partial\Omega
, for some
map
\mathbf{n}_{\mathrm{bd}}\in C^{1}(\partial\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Regardless of our choice of boundary conditions, we always
assume
that there
exists a constant
{\rm C}_{\rm pot}>0
, independent of
\varepsilon
, such that

(7)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\leq{\rm C}_{\rm pot}

for any
\varepsilon
small enough.
One can provide sufficient conditions for (
7
) in terms of the domain and the boundary datum, see e.g.
[
9
, Remark 1]
.

By a
critical point
of the energy functional
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, we mean
a finite-energy solution
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
of the
Euler-Lagrange system of equations

(8)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-1)\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}-\dfrac{\beta}{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\otimes\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\dfrac{\left|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}}{2}\mathbf{I}\right)=0

(9)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Delta\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}+\dfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(\left|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-1)\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\dfrac{2\beta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}=0.

As show in
[
9
]
, under boundary conditions as in (
3
)-(
4
) or as in (
5
)-(
6
) and upon assuming (
7
), for any critical pair
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
we have the fundamental energy bounds

(10)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\leq C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|,

(11)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left\{\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C,

where the constant
C
does not depend on
\varepsilon
.
We consider the functions

(12)

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right),

(13)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}).

In view of (
10
), (
11
), the families
(\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
,
(\nu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
are bounded in
L^{1}(\Omega)
.

The following convergence theorem has been obtained in
[
9
]
.

Theorem A

(
[
9
]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a bounded, simply connected domain of class
C^{2}
.
Let
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}\subset W^{1,2}(\Omega,\,\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2})\times W^{1,2}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a sequence of critical
points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
subject to either (
3
)–(
4
)
or to (
5
)–(
6
).
Assume that the condition (
7
)
is satisfied. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

1.

\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
strongly in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
for any
p<2

and
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{M}_{\star}
strongly in
L^{p}(\Omega)
for any
p<+\infty
.

2.

The limiting maps
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
,
\mathbf{M}_{\star}

satisfy

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\star}(x)\right|=1,\quad\left|\mathbf{M}_{\star}(x)\right|=\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right)^{1/2},\quad\mathbf{Q}_{\star}(x)=\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}_{\star}(x)\otimes\mathbf{M}_{\star}(x)}{\sqrt{2}\beta+1}-\frac{\mathbf{I}}{2}\right)

for a.e.
x\in\Omega
and

(14)

\div(\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\times\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\star})=0

in the sense of distributions in
\Omega
.

3.

\mu_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\mu_{\star}
and

\nu_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup\nu_{\star}
weakly*
as measures in
\Omega
.

4.

\spt\mu_{\star}
is a finite set of points.

5.

\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
strongly in

W^{1,p}_{\rm loc}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star})
for any
p<+\infty
.

We recall that, by
5
and (
14
),

\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
is a smooth harmonic map in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

While Theorem
A
provides detailed information on the asymptotic
convergence of the
\mathbf{Q}
-component of a sequence of critical pairs and on the structure of the limiting energy concentration set
\spt\mu_{\star}
, it gives much weaker information on the behaviour of the
\mathbf{M}
-component and no information on the structure of the limiting set
\spt\nu_{\star}
.
In this paper, we characterise precisely the set
\spt\nu_{\star}
, we clarify
its relationship with the set
\spt\nu_{\star}
, and we obtain stronger convergence
properties for the
\mathbf{M}
-component outside the union of
\spt\mu_{\star}
and
\spt\nu_{\star}
.

As the coupling term promotes
alignment between
\mathbf{M}
and the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{Q}
, the
energy of
\mathbf{M}
should concentrate on singular lines, corresponding
to jumps in the eigenvector frame. Furthermore, keeping into account the
results of
[
12
,
11
]
, where the
asymptotic behaviour of minimiser under boundary conditions
as in (
3
)-(
4
) or as in (
5
)-(
6
) has been studied,
we expect that the endpoints of these singular lines should be either points of

\spt\mu_{\star}
or, possibly, points of
\partial\Omega
.

Main results

At first sight, both (
8
) and (
9
) look like
perturbed Ginzburg-Landau systems. In
[
9
]
, we have
largely exploited the Ginzburg-Landau-type structure of the
system (
8
) in order to study the asymptotic behaviour of
the
\mathbf{Q}
-component. In the present paper, we focus more closely
on the system (
9
).

The starting point of our analysis is the fact that (
9
)
can be regarded as the Euler-Lagrange equation of
a ‘perturbed’ vectorial Allen-Cahn-type energy functional, i.e.
the functional
E_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
17
) below.
This observation allows us to pursue the approach
introduced in the recent paper
[
6
]
for
non-minimising solutions of the Allen-Cahn system.
As we are going to see, a major difficulty is provided by
the coupling term. To deal with this issue, we adapt
the energy methods developed in
[
6
]

and we combine them with
the refined energy estimates and convergence results for the

\mathbf{Q}
-component obtained in
[
9
]
.

In order to make explicit the Allen-Cahn-type structure
of the system (
9
), we start by introducing the function

\ell\colon\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

(15)

\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}):=\frac{1}{4}\left(\left|\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}-1\right)^{2}-\beta\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{M}\cdot\mathbf{M}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\beta^{2}+\sqrt{2}\beta\right).

for any
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
It is easily checked (see Lemma
1.1
)
that for every
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\setminus\{0\}
,
the function
\mathbf{M}\mapsto\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
has only two minimisers, given by

\mathbf{M}_{\pm}=\pm\left(\sqrt{2}\beta\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|+1\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n},

where
\mathbf{n}
is a unit eigenvector of
\mathbf{Q}
relative to
its positive eigenvalue.
Now, given any sequence
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}

of critical points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, we define

(16)

V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}):=\ell(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ell(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,y),

and

(17)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,G):=\int_{G}\left\{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

for any measurable set
G\subseteq\Omega
.
Although not explicitly stressed in the notation,
the function
V
and the functional
E_{\varepsilon}
actually depend on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
, too.
In particular, the wells of
V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
are not fixed,
but
move
, dependingly on both
x
and
\varepsilon
.

If
G\subset\Omega
is a subset where
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
, say, for any
sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, then for any such
\varepsilon
the maps

(18)

(\mathbf{M}_{\pm})_{\varepsilon}=\pm\left(\sqrt{2}\beta\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|+1\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\varepsilon}

provide the only zeroes of
V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
in
G
. Note that, whenever

(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon}
,
(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}
are defined, there holds

\left|(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon}-(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}\right|>2
.
We also notice that, basically by (
7
) and (
11
) (see Proposition
2.2
for more details), the
\varepsilon
-independent bound

(19)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,G)\leq\mathcal{E}_{0}

is in force.

With the notation introduced above,
the Euler-Lagrange equation (
9
) can be recast in the form

(20)

-\varepsilon\Delta\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})=0,

which is exactly the Euler-Lagrange equation of the functional
E_{\varepsilon}
.
Formally, the Equation (
20
) looks like an Allen-Cahn system.
Although the fact the wells are not fixed prevents us
from applying directly the results of
[
6
]
, we can
still follow the same line of thought.
In particular, exactly as in
[
6
]
,
it turns out that, besides the energy densities
\nu_{\varepsilon}

and their limit
\nu_{\star}
, another fundamental object is involved in the study for obtaining the structure properties of the set
\spt\nu_{\star}
:
the
limiting potential energy density
,
\zeta_{\star}
.

Proceeding as in
[
6
]
,
we define the
potential energy densities

(21)

\zeta_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}),

As a consequence of the uniform energy bound (
19
),
the sequence
(\zeta_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded in
L^{1}(\Omega)
,
and thus
we can extract a subsequence and find
\zeta_{\star}
,
a non-negative Radon measure in
\Omega
, so that

\zeta_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\zeta_{\star}

weakly* as measures in
\Omega
in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
As we discuss in more detail in the last part of this
introduction, it turns out that

\zeta_{\star}\leq\nu_{\star}\lesssim\zeta_{\star}

in the sense of measures away from
\spt\mu_{\star}
, so that, loosely speaking,

\nu_{\star}
and
\zeta_{\star}
retain the same amount
of information. However, exactly as in
[
6
]
,

\zeta_{\star}
is a much more convenient object to study than

\nu_{\star}
.

We are now ready to state our main theorems, which are best
expressed using the language of the theory of varifolds
(see, e.g.,
[
20
]
for a detailed account —
a brief review of basic terminology, as needed in this paper,
is included in Section
4
.)

Theorem B
.

The set
\spt\nu_{\star}
is
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, with locally
finite measure. Upon setting

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}:=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star},

the following holds.

1.

The limiting potential energy measure
\zeta_{\star}
is absolutely
continuous with respect to the measure
\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.
In other words, there exists a function
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}:\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
locally integrable with respect to the measure
\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
,
such that

\zeta_{\star}=\mathfrak{v}_{\star}\,\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}.

The function
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
is locally bounded both from above and
from below in any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

2.

The measure
\zeta_{\star}
is the weight measure of the
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable varifold

\mathbb{V}_{\star}
carried by
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
with density function
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
.

3.

The varifold
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
is stationary in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and
its first variation as a varifold in
\Omega
is concentrated on
\spt\mu_{\star}
.

4.

The set
\spt\nu_{\star}
is locally a union of segments, open relative to
\Omega
,
each of which having constant density, given by the value of
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
at any
point of the segment. In addition, apart from an exceptional,
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-null set, around any point
x_{0}
of

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
, the singular set
\spt\nu_{\star}
consists of exactly one segment,
with constant density
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})
.

5.

As
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{M}_{\star}
in
L^{\infty}_{\rm loc}(\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star}))
.

Theorem
B
provides detailed information on the
structure of the set
\spt\nu_{\star}
, obtained through the properties
of the limiting measure
\zeta_{\star}
.

Statement
3
of Theorem
B
is an abridged
version of our second main theorem, Theorem
C
below,
which establishes the precise form of the relationship between
\spt\mu_{\star}
and
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
(and thus,
\spt\nu_{\star}
).
Before stating Theorem
C
, we
need to introduce another bit of notation.

Given distinct points
a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}
in
\Omega
,
we denote by
\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n})
their Ginzburg-Landau renormalised energy, defined exactly as in
[
4
]
(we recall its characterising property in (
4.5
) below) and representing
their interaction energy.
In particular, we will be interested in the renormalised
energy
\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n_{\star}})

of the (distinct) points
a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n_{\star}}
in

\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\Omega
.

Theorem C
.

The first variation
\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}
of the
varifold
\mathbb{V}_{\star}:=\mathbf{v}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})

satisfies

(22)

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\star}}z_{j}\cdot\mathbf{X}(a_{j})

for any
\mathbf{X}\in C^{1}_{c}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where

(23)

z_{j}=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}\left(a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}}\right)

for any
j\in\{1,\dots,n_{\star}\}
. Here

n_{\star}
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}}
denote, respectively,
the cardinality and the points of
\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\Omega
.

We can look at (
22
)–(
23
)
as a
balance law
which sheds light on the
relationship between
\spt\mu_{\star}
and
\spt\nu_{\star}
:
the interaction energy between the point singularities

a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}}
, encoded in the Ginzburg-Landau
renormalised energy
\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}})
,
is compensated by the first variation of
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
.
In particular, we see from (
22
) that
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
is
stationary in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
(Notice that this implies that the ‘boundary’ of
\spt\nu_{\star}

must consist of points of
\partial\Omega
or points of
\spt\mu_{\star}
.)
In the pure Allen-Cahn
setting (both in the scalar case
[
16
]
and in the
vectorial case on two-dimensional domains
[
6
]
),
the stationarity of the limiting varifold is inherited from
Equation (
29
), whose validity ultimately
goes back to the fact that
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
solves the Allen-Cahn equation,
so that

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}=0
can be seen as a criticality condition
for
\spt\nu_{\star}
. Similarly, in
the pure Ginzburg-Landau theory, the condition

\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}})=0
for any

j\in{1,\dots,n_{\star}}
represents the criticality condition for

\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\Omega

(cf. e.g.
[
4
, Theorem VII.4]
and
[
5
, Theorem IX.1]
).
It is therefore natural that the interaction between
the two components
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

induces a balance between the first variation of
\mathbb{V}
and the gradient of the renormalised energy, rather than separate criticality of these two objects.

Structure of the proofs

The proofs of the Theorem
B
and Theorem
C
are substantially more complex than the analogous results for minimising solutions. Many of the arguments of
[
12
,
11
]

do not carry over to this context, because they are based on energy minimality.
Studying the properties of the limiting measure
\nu_{\star}

— in particular, proving rectifiability of its support —
is a much more delicate task. A substantial difficulty is the lack of relevant monotonicity formulae for
\nu_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed, since we expect concentration of
\nu_{\varepsilon}
on a
1
-dimensional set, we seek bounds on quantities of the form
r^{-1}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\nu_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}x
. However, a direct computation (based on a Pohozaev identity, see Lemma
1.6
below) gives

(24)

\begin{split}r^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{1}{r}\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\nu_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)&=\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x\\
&\qquad+r\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left(\varepsilon\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1},\end{split}

where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
denote, respectively, the normal and the tangent field to

\partial B_{r}(x_{0})
.

Leaving aside the terms that depend on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
for the moment, the first integral in the right-hand side of (
24
) is problematic, because it does not have a definite sign. While in the scalar case similar “discrepancy” terms can be bounded from below based on the maximum principle
[
19
,
16
]
, there is no reason why such bound should extend to the vectorial case.
(For instance, an example in
[
21
]
shows that in the vectorial case
the pointwise discrepancy associated with entire solutions may not have a sign, in
sharp contrast with the scalar case
[
19
]
.)
To circumvent this issue, we follow the strategy of the recent paper
[
6
]
, which is ultimately based on refined energy estimates. Once again, we have to adapt this approach to make it compatible with the coupling terms.

The key ingredients to this purpose are the following facts proved in
[
9
]
:

1.

The clearing-out theorem for
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
, which ensures that,
for every compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
there holds
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
uniformly on
K
, for every
\varepsilon

small enough, depending only on
K
.
In fact, for every compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, we have also

(25)

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\to 1\qquad\mbox{ uniformly on }K\mbox{ as }\varepsilon\to 0.

2.

For every compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and every

p
with
1\leq p<+\infty
, there holds

(26)

\int_{K}\left\{\left|\nabla\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\right|^{p}+\left(\frac{\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1}{\varepsilon}-\kappa_{\star}\right)^{p}\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\to 0

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, where
\kappa_{\star}=\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right)
is a constant that could be seen as the ground level of the ‘modified’ Ginzburg-Landau potential (the term in round brackets above) arising because of the interaction between the two order parameters.

3.

The strong convergence
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
in
W^{1,p}_{\rm loc}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
) from item
5
of
Theorem
A
.

4.

\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
is a smooth harmonic map in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

The first part of
1
is a straightforward consequence
of
[
9
, Proposition 2.5]
and (
10
). It ensures that, away
from
\spt\mu_{\star}
, the Allen-Cahn structure is well defined, making our approach
viable. The uniform convergence (
25
) comes as a consequence
of uniform bounds on the
p
-Ginzburg-Landau energy of
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
, for every
p

with
1\leq p<+\infty
(see
[
9
, Proposition 2.6 and Lemma 2.8]
).

Item
2
follows from Lemma 3.7 and Remark 3.6
in
[
9
]
.
It is actually crucial to the proof of the strong convergence in
3
and it also entails that

(27)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\to 0,

strongly in
L^{p}(K)
, for every compact
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and any
p
with
1\leq p<+\infty
, see Lemma
3.2
below.

Concerning
3
, it implies that,
if
B(x_{0},\,r)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{1}\to\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{1}\to\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{1}

for almost every radius.
Finally, the harmonicity of
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
away from
\spt\mu_{\star}
implies that

\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{1}=\int_{\partial B_{r}(x_{0})}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{1}.

Back to (
24
), we observe that, by the above,
the terms depending
on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
on the second line actually
vanish in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
, whenever

B_{r}(x_{0})
is well-contained in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
Furthermore, by (
27
), as far as we are concerned with the limit

\varepsilon\to 0
, we may replace the potential

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
with the
simpler

potential
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
in (
24
) and,
instead of the energy densities
\nu_{\varepsilon}
, we may consider the

simpler
energy densities

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}).

In view of the uniform energy bound (
19
),
the sequence
(\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}

is bounded in
L^{1}(\Omega)
and so, up to extraction of a subsequence,

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
convergence weakly
∗
in the sense of measures
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
By (
27
), there holds

(28)

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\nu_{\star}\qquad{\textrm{weakly${}^{*}$ as measures in }K,}

for every compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
We stress that here
\nu_{\star}
is
exactly
the limit of the measures
\nu_{\varepsilon}

that appears in Theorem
A
. The convergence
in (
28
) says essentially
that, at first order,
all the relevant information on the limiting measure
\nu_{\star}

is already contained in the simpler energy measures

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
. What is more, on the account of the results
in
[
6
]
, we may expect “clearing-out” properties
and energy decay estimates for
\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}

(see Section
2.3
for the precise statements), leading to lower density bounds on
\nu_{\star}
(see Section
3.1
).

As already mentioned,
one major consequence of the presence of
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
in the definition of
V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

is the
fact that the wells of
V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
are not fixed but
move
,
both with
x
and
\varepsilon
, as shown by (
18
).
Most arguments
in
[
6
]
are based on testing (
20
)
against maps involving the difference
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-(\mathbf{M}_{\pm})_{\varepsilon}
, so that
the fact that
(\mathbf{M}_{\pm})_{\varepsilon}
is not constant gives rise to terms
depending on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and its derivatives.
We manage them by using the above ingredients.
Combined with the
the strategy in
[
6
]
and through a
rather delicate analysis, they allow us
to obtain the estimates and the compactness
results for the
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
-component
in Section
2

and Section
3
, respectively.
The main result at the
\varepsilon
-level is the clearing-out
property for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
provided by Theorem
2.12
,
which, passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
, yields a corresponding
clearing-out property for
\nu_{\star}
, item
2
of
Theorem
3.3
below.

As in
[
6
]
,
the clearing-out theorem for
\nu_{\star}
is a fundamental tool towards
the analysis of the structure properties of the set
\spt\nu_{\star}
. First, it
yields density lower bounds for
\nu_{\star}
and the fact that the
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure
of
\spt\nu_{\star}
is locally finite (see Theorem
3.3
).
Second, together with an appropriate Pohozaev-type inequality
(Proposition
3.6
), which yields the
non-existence of ‘islands’ of singular set (Theorem
3.7
),
it entails that
\spt\nu_{\star}
is locally path connected, and thus an

\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable set. By exploiting the rectifiability of
\spt\nu_{\star}
and, again,
the clearing-out property, we prove in Theorem
3.10

the locally uniform convergence of
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
towards
\mathbf{M}_{\star}

away from
\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star}
.

Continuing following
[
6
]
, the crucial observation to overcome the aforementioned lack of monotonicity
formulae for the rescaled Allen-Cahn energy is the fact

\zeta_{\star}
solves the equation

(29)

\frac{\partial\omega_{\star}}{\partial{\bar{z}}}=2\frac{\partial\zeta_{\star}}{\partial z}

in the sense of distributions in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
Here,
\omega_{\star}
is the
limiting Hopf differential
,
defined by

(30)

\begin{split}\omega_{\star}=\omega^{\rm Q}+\omega^{\rm M}:=&\mbox{(weak*-)}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\left|\partial_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-2i\partial_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right)\\
&+\mbox{(weak*-)}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\left(\left|\partial_{1}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{2}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-2i\partial_{1}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{2}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right).\end{split}

Because of the strong convergence
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
in

W_{\rm loc}^{1,2}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star})
and since

\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
is smooth harmonic in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
we have
\frac{\partial\omega_{\star}^{\rm Q}}{\partial\bar{z}}=0
and, therefore, (
29
) can equivalently be written
(see Proposition
3.13
) as

\frac{\partial\omega_{\star}^{\rm M}}{\partial{\bar{z}}}=2\frac{\partial\zeta_{\star}}{\partial z}

in the sense of distributions in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
According to
[
6
]
, the system (
29
)
could be interpreted as a sort of ‘modified Cauchy-Riemann conditions’.

A remarkable consequence of (
29
) is
that, for any
x_{0}\in\Omega
, the function

r\mapsto\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}
is monotone non-decreasing
(see Proposition
3.18
).
Neither an analogous monotonicity property nor any equation similar
to (
29
) are known to hold for
\nu_{\star}
.
This motivates us to focus our attention
on
\zeta_{\star}
instead of
\nu_{\star}
.
Luckily, from the analysis of
\zeta_{\star}
we
still recover information on the set

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}:=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
because
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
coincides exactly with
the support of
\zeta_{\star}
.
Indeed, on the one hand the convergence (
28
)
and the equality
\zeta_{\star}(\spt\mu_{\star})=0

(which follows from the the monotonicity of
\frac{1}{r}\zeta_{\star}
)
imply that

\zeta_{\star}\leq\nu_{\star}

in the sense of measures in
\Omega
(see Proposition
3.18
).

On the other hand, by monotonicity,
\zeta_{\star}
has a density at every point
x_{0}\in\Omega
, which yields
the first part of statement
1
of Theorem
B
.
The monotonicity of
\zeta_{\star}
and, once again, a suitable Pohozaev inequality,
allow us to prove that the density of
\nu_{\star}
exists a
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-a.e. point of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
and is controlled by
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
through a constant depending only
on
\Omega
and
\beta
. Together with the density lower bounds for
\nu_{\star}

coming from the clearing-out theorem, it gives the density lower bounds on
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
mentioned in the second part of
1
, while the upper bounds come
as a consequence of (
19
) and, again, the monotonicity of
\zeta_{\star}
. As a consequence,

\nu_{\star}\lesssim\zeta_{\star}\quad\mbox{as measures in }\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\spt\zeta_{\star}=\mathfrak{S}_{\star}.

Once statement
1
is proved, statement
2

of Theorem
B
is an immediate consequence of the definitions
(see Section
4
).
Given
2
and assuming for a moment
3

(i.e., Theorem
C
),
item
4
follows from a classical
structure theorem for stationary
1
-varifolds with bounded
density
[
1
]
,
together with
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
.
Contrary to the case of minimisers, we do not know if
the density
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
is constant, in general,
although we suspect that the limiting
varifold
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
has integer multiplicity, see Remark
4.1
.

Finally, concerning Theorem
C
,
relying on (
29
) exactly as in
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
,
we first prove in Theorem
4.1
that

\mathbb{V}_{\star}
is stationary in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, which
implies that the first variation of
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
as a varifold in
\Omega

must be supported on the finite set
\spt\mu_{\star}
.
Then, in Theorem
4.2
, we compute explicitly

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}
. The computation starts from the Pohazaev
identity at the
\varepsilon
-level, given by Lemma
1.6
below,
and it exploits once again the above toolkit

1
–
4
in
a decisive way to obtain the balance law (
22
)–(
23
).

Plan of the paper

The paper is organised as follows. Section
1
contains some notation and preliminary results
imported from the companion paper
[
9
]
. In Section
2.3
we develop refined energy
estimates for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
, along the lines of
[
6
]
, that lead in the end to the clearing-out
theorem for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
, Theorem
2.12
.
Section
3
is devoted to the analysis of the limit measure
\nu_{\star}
, based on an adapting the arguments in
[
6
]
. (More details on the
structure of the arguments can be found at the beginning of the section.)
The proof of Theorem
B
and of Theorem
C

are given in Section
4
.

Notation
.

We use the symbol
\{X_{\varepsilon}\}
to denote a family of
objects indexed by the parameter
\varepsilon>0
, almost always
used as a shorthand to denote
the sequence
\left\{X_{\varepsilon_{k}}\right\}_{k}
, where
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
as

k\to+\infty
.
Usually, for the sake of a lighter notation, we do not relabel subsequences.

In inequalities like
A\lesssim B
, the symbol
\lesssim
means that there
exists a constant
C
, independent of
A
and
B
, such that
A\leq CB
.
In particular,
dealing with sequences indexed by
\varepsilon
, we use

\lesssim
to denote inequality up to a constant independent of
\varepsilon
.
Whenever it is relevant, we keep track of the dependences
of the implicit constants.
Whenever possible without inducing
ambiguities, for the sake of a lighter notation, we avoid writing
explicitly the measure of integration in the integrals.

1
Preliminary results

In this Section, we collect the results from the companion
paper
[
9
]
that will be needed in the following course.

1.1
Properties of the potential
f_{\varepsilon}
.

For the reader’s convenience, we summarise below some properties of the potential
f_{\varepsilon}
. All these properties have been already proved either
in
[
12
]
or in
[
9
]
, to which the
reader will be referred for detailed proof.

The constant
\kappa_{\varepsilon}
in (
2
) is uniquely determined by imposing

\inf f_{\varepsilon}=0
. It turns out that (
[
12
, Lemma B.3]
)

\kappa_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\beta^{2}+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)+\kappa_{\star}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+\mathrm{o}(\varepsilon^{2}),

where

(1.1)

\kappa_{\star}:=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\beta\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right).

Upon using the function
\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
defined in (
1.3
),
we can rewrite

(1.2)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})=\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}-1\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})+\chi_{\varepsilon},

where

(1.3)

\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}):=\frac{1}{4}\left(\left|\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}-1\right)^{2}-\beta\,\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{M}\cdot\mathbf{M}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\beta^{2}+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)

and where

(1.4)

\chi_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\kappa_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\left(\beta^{2}+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)\!

satisfies

(1.5)

\chi_{\varepsilon}\to\kappa_{\star}^{2}\qquad\mbox{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

For later reference, we recall the following characterisation of minimisers of

\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\cdot\,)
, for a given
\mathbf{Q}\neq 0
.

Lemma 1.1

(
[
9
, Lemma 1.2]
)
.

For any
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\setminus\{0\}
, the function
\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\cdot\,)

has exactly two minimisers, given by

(1.6)

\mathbf{M}_{\pm}:=\pm\left(\sqrt{2}\beta\rho+1\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}

where
\rho:=\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|
and
\mathbf{n}
is a unit eigenvector of
\mathbf{Q}
corresponding to its positive eigenvalue.
Moreover, there holds

(1.7)

\begin{split}\min\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\cdot\,)=\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}_{\pm})=\frac{\beta}{2}(1-\rho)\left(\sqrt{2}+\beta+\beta\rho\right)\!.\end{split}

For
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\setminus\{0\}
, let
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}):=\{\mathbf{M}_{+},\,\mathbf{M}_{-}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the set of minimisers
of
\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\cdot\,)
, as given by (
1.6
).
If
\mathbf{Q}\neq 0
and
\dist(\mathbf{M},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}))\leq 1
, we define

(1.8)

\pi(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{M}_{+}&\textrm{if }\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{+}\right|\leq 1\\
\mathbf{M}_{-}&\textrm{if }\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{-}\right|\leq 1.\end{cases}

\pi(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
is the closest projection of
\mathbf{M}

to
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q})
. It is defined in a non-ambiguous way,
because
\left|\mathbf{M}_{+}-\mathbf{M}_{-}\right|=2(\sqrt{2}\beta\rho+1)^{1/2}>2
.

Lemma 1.2

(
[
10
, Lemma 1.4]
)
.

For any
\Lambda>1
, there exist positive
constants
\delta_{0}(\Lambda)
and
c_{0}(\Lambda)

(depending on
\Lambda
and
\beta
only)
such that the following statement holds:
for any
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
such
that
\Lambda^{-1}\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\leq\Lambda

and
\dist(\mathbf{M},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}))\leq\delta_{0}(\Lambda)
, there holds

\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\pi(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}))\geq c_{0}(\Lambda)\Big(\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})-\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\pi(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}))\Big)\geq 0.

1.2
The maximum principle

A first consequence of the Euler-Lagrange
equations (
8
), (
9
) is the
following maximum principle, the proof of
which is detailed in
[
9
, Lemma 1.6]
.

Lemma 1.3
.

The maps
\mathbf{Q}^{*}_{\varepsilon}
,
\mathbf{M}^{*}_{\varepsilon}
are smooth inside
\Omega

and of class
C^{1}
up to the boundary of
\Omega
.
Moreover, there exist a constant
C_{\beta}
, depending only on
\beta
,
and a constant
C_{\beta,\Omega}
, depending only on
\beta
and
\Omega
,
such that

(1.9)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{Q}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\left\|\mathbf{M}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq{C_{\beta}}

(1.10)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\mathbf{Q}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\left\|\nabla\mathbf{M}^{*}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{C_{\beta,\Omega}}}{\varepsilon}.

Remark 1.1
.

As observed in
[
9
, Remark 1.6]
, the proof of Lemma
1.3

shows a little bit more. Indeed, we have, more precisely,

(1.11)

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon),\quad\left|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\leq 1+{{\sqrt{2}}}\beta\,s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon)\qquad\textrm{in }\Omega,

where
s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon)>1
is the largest root of the polynomial

P(X)=X^{3}-(1+{\beta^{2}\varepsilon})X-{\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2}}}\varepsilon
.
Elementary calculus shows that
s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon)\to 1

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, for any given value of
\beta
. Then, by differentiating
the constraint
P(s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon))=0
with respect to
\varepsilon
,
we deduce

(1.12)

s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon)=1+{\varepsilon\kappa_{\star}+{\rm O}(\varepsilon^{2})}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

1.3
Summary of the energy estimates

The following theorem summarises the main energy estimates
proved in
[
9
]
.

Theorem 1.4

(
[
9
, Theorem 2.11]
)
.

Let
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}
be a sequence of
critical points of the functional
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, subject to boundary
conditions as in (
3
), (
4
) or
as in (
5
), (
6
). Assume
that (
7
) holds. Then,

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\lesssim\left|\log\varepsilon\right|,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\lesssim\left|\log\varepsilon\right|,

(1.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\lesssim 1,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\left(1-\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}\lesssim 1,

where the implicit constants on the right-hand side depend only
on
\beta
,
{\rm C}_{\rm pot}
, and
the
L^{1}(\partial\Omega)
- and the
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
-norm of

\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\times\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}
.

Moreover, on any ball
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\!\subset\Omega
on which

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
,
there hold

(1.17)

\displaystyle\int_{B}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C(x_{0},\,R,\,\beta,\,{\rm C}_{\rm pot}),

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)\leq C(x_{0},\,R,\,\beta,\,{\rm C}_{\rm pot}),

where the constant

C(x_{0},\,R,\,\beta,\,{\rm C}_{\rm pot},\,\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}})
depends only on
x_{0}
,
R
,

\beta
,
{\rm C}_{\rm pot}
, and the
L^{1}(\partial\Omega)
- and the
L^{2}(\partial\Omega)
-norm of
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\times\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}
.
Consequently, if
K\subset\Omega
is any compact set such
that
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
on
K
for any
\varepsilon
small enough,
then there holds

(1.19)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,K)}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}=0.

1.4
Pohozaev identity

In several points of this work, we shall make use of the Pohozaev identity
satisfied by critical points
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, both at
the
\varepsilon
-level and the ‘
\star
-level’, i.e., after taking the limit

\varepsilon\to 0
. This identity has been already obtained (and exploited)
in
[
9
]
, so we just recall the result, referring the reader to
[
9
]

for a detailed proof.

As in
[
9
]
, we define the
stress-energy tensor
associated
to
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
as

(1.20)

T_{jk}^{\varepsilon}:=\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\,\partial_{j}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-e_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\,\delta_{jk}

for
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
.
Here
e_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
is the energy density,
defined by

(1.21)

e_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}):=\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

Then, the following identity holds.

Lemma 1.5

(
[
9
, Lemma 1.7]
)
.

Let
G\subseteq\Omega
be any open set with boundary
\partial G
of
class
C^{1}
and let
X\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, for any solution

(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
to (
8
), (
9
) there holds

(1.22)

\begin{split}\int_{G}T_{jk}^{\varepsilon}\,\partial_{j}X_{k}&=\int_{\partial G}\left(({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{Q})\cdot(\mathbf{X}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{Q})+\varepsilon\,({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M})\cdot(\mathbf{X}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M})\right)\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt-\int_{\partial G}(\mathbf{X}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\,e_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})\,{\mathrm{d}}s.\end{split}

In particular,
the stress-energy tensor satisfies
\partial_{j}T_{jk}^{\varepsilon}=0
in
\Omega
.

For later reference, we point out an immediate consequence of Lemma
1.5
.

Lemma 1.6
.

For each ball
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subseteq\Omega
,
there holds

(1.23)

\begin{split}\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\,{\mathrm{d}}x&+\frac{R}{2}\int_{\partial B}\left(\left|\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\varepsilon\left|\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\frac{R}{2}\int_{\partial B}\left(\left|\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\varepsilon\left|\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right)\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
is the outward unit normal and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

is the unit tangent field on
\partial B
, oriented in such a way
that
({\boldsymbol{\nu}},\,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
is a positive basis.

Proof.

The lemma follows by taking
G=B(x_{0},\,R)

and
\mathbf{X}(x):=\varphi(x)(x-x_{0})
in (
1.22
),
where
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth cut-off function
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
in a neighbourhood of
\Omega
.
∎

Finally, we recall that, being a smooth harmonic map in

\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
satisfies
an independent Pohozaev identity. More precisely, if
\mathbf{X}
is any smooth vector field with compact support in
G\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, then we have

(1.24)

\int_{G}\left\{\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\,\partial_{j}X_{k}-\frac{1}{2}(\div\mathbf{X})\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0.

Equation (
1.24
) is classical and it is obtained by
multiplying the harmonic map equation
-\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{\star}=\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
by
\mathbf{X}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
and integrating by parts twice.

2
Refined energy estimates for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

In this section, we take advantage of the ‘Allen-Cahn type’ structure of
the energy functional
E_{\varepsilon}
introduced in (
17
)
to derive several refined energy estimates
for the
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
-component of a sequence
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}

of critical points of the functional
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
.

The key point of our argument is the fact that
the Euler-Lagrange equation of
E_{\varepsilon}
is precisely (
9
),
which, as we have already seen, can be recast in the more convenient form (
20
). However, although
E_{\varepsilon}
looks like an Allen-Cahn functional
and, formally, (
20
) looks like an Allen-Cahn system,
the coupling between
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

make the wells of the potential function
V
in (
16
)
dependent on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
, putting our problem well outside the context
considered in
[
6
]
.
Nonetheless, we manage so as to exploit the improved bounds on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}

from the companion paper
[
9
]

away from
the set
\spt\mu_{\star}
given by item
4
of Theorem
A

to keep the perturbation under sufficiently precise control.
This allows us to parallel the path
traced in
[
6
]

away from
\spt\mu_{\star}
,
so that we are able to establish several a priori
estimates for critical points at the
\varepsilon
-level starting from Equation (
9
).
These estimates lead, ultimately, to a crucial
clearing-out property
for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
,
expressed by Theorem
2.12
.
In turn, Theorem
2.12
is key to analyse
the limiting situation as
\varepsilon\to 0
and, indeed, it is the cornerstone of the
further developments worked out in Section
3
.

2.1
The ‘perturbed Allen-Cahn energy’
E_{\varepsilon}

We start by studying the properties of the energy functional

E_{\varepsilon}
, already defined in (
17
) in the introduction,
and the associated potential
V
, defined in (
16
).
(For convenience, all the relevant definitions are recalled below.)
The potential
V
depends on both
\mathbf{Q}
and
\mathbf{M}
, though,
we shall see,
\mathbf{Q}
enters essentially as a ‘parameter’.
The critical points of
E_{\varepsilon}

satisfy the Euler-Lagrange equation (
9
), which is
more conveniently recast in the form (
2.8
).
As already mentioned in the Introduction,
whenever
\mathbf{Q}\neq 0
,
E_{\varepsilon}
can be
seen as a ‘perturbed Allen-Cahn energy’.
The ‘perturbation’
has to be sought in the terms depending on
\mathbf{Q}
in the
potential
V
, which make the wells
move
with the
point
x\in\Omega
instead of staying fixed, which is instead
the case in the usual Allen-Cahn theory.
In this section, we point out a few general properties
of
E_{\varepsilon}
and
V
. In particular, we prove a global,

\varepsilon
-independent bound for the energy of critical points
(see Proposition
2.2
below). Further,
we introduce some notation and terminology that will be
extensively used in the next sections.

Given any pair
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we let

V(\mathbf{M}):=\ell(\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{M})-\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,y),

where
\ell
is the function defined in (
1.3
).
Notice that
V\geq 0
.
If
G\subset\Omega
,
x\in G
,
and
x\mapsto\mathbf{Q}(x)
is a
\mathbf{Q}
-tensor field and
x\mapsto\mathbf{M}(x)

is a vector field, we define

(2.1)

V(x,\,\mathbf{M}(x)):=\ell(\mathbf{Q}(x),\mathbf{M}(x))-\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ell(\mathbf{Q}(x),\,y).

Note that, since the infimum of
\ell(\mathbf{Q}(x),\cdot\,)
does not depend on

\mathbf{M}
(see (
1.7
)), we have

(2.2)

\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})=\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}).

for any
x\in G
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\left(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right)
be any critical point of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
subject
to boundary conditions either as in (
3
)–(
4
) or as
in (
5
)–(
6
) and assume that (
7
) holds.
Then, for every
x\in\Omega
there holds the estimate

(2.3)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{2}+\rm{O}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1),\end{split}

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends only on
\beta
.

Proof.

Recalling (
1.3
), (
1.4
), (
1.7
),
(
1.2
), (
1.5
), and (
1.9
), we have the pointwise
identities

(2.4)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))&-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\\
&=\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(1-\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)^{2}+\chi_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\min_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\ell(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,y)\\
&=\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\frac{1-\rho_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\right)\left(\sqrt{2}+\beta+\beta\rho_{\varepsilon}\right)+\kappa_{\star}^{2}+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1),\end{split}

which, after applying Young’s inequality, yields estimate (
2.3
).
∎

Remark 2.1
.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.1
, (
1.16
),
and assumption (
7
), we have
the
\varepsilon
-independent bound

(2.5)

\int_{\Omega}V\left(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\lesssim 1,

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends only on

\beta
and the constant
{\rm C}_{\rm pot}
in (
7
).
A still immediate but even more important consequence
of Lemma
2.1
will be provided by
Lemma
3.1
hereafter.

Next, for any measurable set
G\subseteq\Omega
, we define

(2.6)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,G):=\int_{G}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

The uniform energy bound for critical points below is
immediate.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\left(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right)
be a critical point of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, subject
to boundary conditions either as in (
3
)–(
4
) or as
in (
5
)–(
6
). Then,

(2.7)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\Omega)\leq\mathcal{E}_{0},

where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is a positive number depending only
on
\beta
, the constant
{\rm C}_{\rm pot}
in (
7
),
and the (implicit) constant
C
on the right-hand side of (
1.15
).

Proof.

The conclusion follows immediately by
combining (
1.15
) and (
2.5
).
∎

With the notation introduced above,
the equation for
\mathbf{M}
, i.e., Equation (
9
), can be recast in the form

(2.8)

-\varepsilon\Delta\mathbf{M}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot,\,\mathbf{M})=0

in any open set
G\subset\Omega
.
This compact writing will be conveniently employed in the following
course.

Remark 2.2

(Scaling properties)
.

Let
G=B(x_{0},\,r)\subset\Omega
be a ball. Then, arguing exactly as
in
[
6
, Section 1.4.1]
, upon setting

(2.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{r},\qquad\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}(x):=\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(rx+x_{0}),\qquad\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}(x):=\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(rx+x_{0})\qquad(x\in B_{1}),

(2.10)

\displaystyle e_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})(y):=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}(y)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(y,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\qquad(y\in B(x_{0},\,r)),

we have that
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}
solves (
2.8
)
with
\varepsilon
changed into
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}
and, moreover, there holds

(2.11)

e_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}(\mathbf{M}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{r}})(x)=re_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})(rx+x_{0}),\qquad\forall x\in B_{1}.

In fact, the separate identities

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}{2}\left|\nabla\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{\widetilde{e}_{r}}\right|^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=r\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}(rx+x_{0})\right),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}V_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}\left(x,\,\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=r^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right)

hold for any
x\in B_{1}
, whence

(2.12)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,r))

\displaystyle=rE_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{r}};\,B_{1}\right),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{B(x_{0},\,r)}V(y,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\,{\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle=r\left(\frac{1}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}\int_{B_{1}}V_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}(x,\,\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right).

Thus, dividing both sides of (
2.12
) by
\varepsilon
shows that
the quantity
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}^{-1}E_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}
is scaling invariant:

(2.14)

\varepsilon^{-1}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,r))=\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}^{-1}E_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{r}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}_{{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}_{r}};\,B_{1}\right).

Assume now
G\subset\Omega
is a simply connected, open set, with smooth boundary.
Then, by well-known lifting results (cf., e.g.,
[
9
, Section 1.1]
),
given
\mathbf{Q}
, a
\mathbf{Q}
-tensor field which does not vanish in
G
,
we can find a pair
(\mathbf{n},\,\mathbf{m})
of orthonormal eigenvectors of
\mathbf{Q}

defined in the whole of
G
so that we can write

\mathbf{Q}=\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{m}\otimes\mathbf{m})\qquad\mbox{in }G,

where, as always in this paper,
\rho:=\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|
and
\mathbf{n}(x)

denotes an eigenvector of
\mathbf{Q}(x)

related to its positive eigenvalue.

Next, we
let
\mathbf{M}_{\pm}=\pm\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}

denote the maps realising the minimum of
V
, i.e., of
\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\cdot\,)

— see Lemma
1.1
— so that

V(x,\mathbf{M}(x))=\ell(\mathbf{Q}(x),\,\mathbf{M}(x))-\ell(\mathbf{Q}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\pm}(x))

for any
x\in G
. We also set

(2.15)

\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)):=\left\{\mathbf{M}_{+}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{-}(x)\right\}\qquad\mbox{for }x\in G.

Recall that, for
\mathbf{Q}\neq 0
,

(2.16)

\left|\mathbf{M}_{+}-\mathbf{M}_{-}\right|=2\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\right)^{1/2}>2

and we notice that, by a straightforward computation,
for both
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}_{+}
and
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}_{-}
there holds

(2.17)

\begin{split}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathbf{M}}\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{N})=\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\,\mathbf{I}+(\sqrt{2}\beta\rho+2)\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\,\mathbf{m}\otimes\mathbf{m}=2\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\,\mathbf{I}+2\mathbf{n}\otimes\mathbf{n}\end{split}

so that

\begin{split}\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathbf{M}}\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{N})(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\geq 2\sqrt{2}\,\beta\rho\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|^{2}\end{split}

for both
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}_{+}
and
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}_{-}
. Thus,

(2.18)

2\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|^{2}\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}V(\,\,\cdot,\,\mathbf{N})(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\leq 2\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\right)\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}.

for both
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}_{+}
and
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{M}_{-}
.
As it is immediately checked from (
2.17
),

(\mathbf{n},\,\mathbf{m})
is also an orthonormal
basis of eigenvectors for
\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathbf{M}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\pm}(x))
at any
x\in G
,
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\lambda_{\mathbf{m}}
given by

\lambda_{\mathbf{m}}=2\sqrt{2}\beta\rho,\qquad\lambda_{\mathbf{n}}=2\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho\right).

In particular, if
G\subset\Omega
is a simply connected, open set,
with smooth boundary and
\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|>0
on
G
, then

\mathrm{D}^{2}_{\mathbf{M}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\pm}(x))
is a positive-definite
quadratic form at any
x\in G
. In addition,
if
\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|
is bounded from above and from below on
G
,
then from the proof of Lemma
1.2
,
we have better control on the behaviour of
V
near
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q})
.
More precisely, we first observe that, if
1/2\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq 2
on
G
,
then

(2.19)

\lambda_{-}:=\sqrt{2}\beta\leq\lambda_{\mathbf{m}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\lambda_{\mathbf{n}}\leq\lambda_{+}:=2\left(1+2\sqrt{2}\beta\right)\qquad\mbox{on }G.

so that the lemma below follows easily and provides a counterpart of
[
6
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

There exists a positive constant
\delta_{\beta}\in(0,\,1/4]
, depending
only on
\beta
, such that the following holds.
If
G\subset\Omega
is an open, simply connected set
with smooth boundary and
\mathbf{Q}
is a
\mathbf{Q}
-tensor field with

\frac{1}{2}\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\leq 2
on
G
, then for any
x\in G

and any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\dist(y,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))<\delta_{\beta}
there holds

(2.20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{-}\dist(y,\,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))^{2}\leq V(x,\,y)\leq\lambda_{+}\dist(y,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))^{2},

(2.21)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{-}\dist(y,\,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))^{2}\leq\nabla V(x,\,y)\cdot(y-\mathbf{N})\leq 2\lambda_{+}\dist(y,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))^{2},

where
\mathbf{N}=\pi(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
is given by (
1.8
).
Moreover,

(2.22)

V(x,\,y)\geq\gamma_{\beta}:=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\beta\delta_{\beta}^{2}\qquad\mbox{for any }y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mbox{ such that }\dist(y,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))\geq\frac{\delta_{\beta}}{4}.

Proof.

Being the proof completely elementary, we just give a quick sketch of it.

The first line in (
2.20
) follows immediately by Taylor-expanding

V
close to the wells and using the absolute bounds (
2.19
), for an
appropriate choice of
\delta_{\beta}
depending only on
\beta
.
The first inequality in the second line is a straightforward consequence of
Lemma
1.2
with the choice
\Lambda=2
, and possibly reducing

\delta_{\beta}
. The second inequality is proven in a
completely analogous way.
∎

As an elementary consequence, we obtain the lemma below
(cf.
[
6
, Lemma 2.2]
).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
G\subset\Omega
be any open, simply connected set,
with smooth boundary and
\mathbf{Q}
any
\mathbf{Q}
-tensor field
with
\frac{1}{2}\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\leq 2
on
G
.
Then, for any
x\in G
and any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

V(x,\,y)\leq\gamma_{\beta}
there holds

\dist(y,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))\leq\delta_{\beta}

and, moreover,

\dist(y,\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))\leq\sqrt{4\lambda_{-}^{-1}V(x,\,y)},

where
\lambda_{-}
is given by (
2.19
).

Let
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\Omega
be a ball
such that
\frac{1}{2}\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\leq 2
on
B
.
Let
\kappa\in\left(0,\delta_{\beta}\right)
.
In analogy with
[
6
, Section 4]
, we define

(2.23)

\Upsilon:=\Upsilon(R,\,\kappa):=\left\{x\in B\,:\,\dist(\mathbf{M}(x),\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x))\leq\kappa\right\}

as well as

(2.24)

\begin{split}&\Upsilon_{\pm}:=\left\{x\in B:\,\dist(\mathbf{M}(x),\,(\mathbf{M}_{\pm})_{\varepsilon}(x)\leq\kappa\right\},\\
&\Gamma_{\pm}:=\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}\cap B,\qquad\Pi_{\pm}:=\Upsilon_{\pm}\cap\partial B.\end{split}

Notice that

\Upsilon=\Upsilon_{+}\cup\Upsilon_{-}.

Moreover, by the choice of
\kappa
, the sets
\Upsilon_{\pm}
are disjoint,
so that

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Upsilon)&=\int_{\Upsilon}\left(\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&=E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Upsilon_{+})+E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Upsilon_{-}).\end{split}

Besides the sets
\Upsilon_{\pm}
, we define, for any
R>0
, the set

(2.25)

\Theta(\mathbf{M},\,R):=\left\{x\in B(x_{0},\,R):\,\dist(\mathbf{M}(x),\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}(x)))\geq\delta_{\beta}\right\}.

Clearly,

B(x_{0},\,R)=\Upsilon\left(R,\,\delta_{\beta}\right)\cup\Theta(\mathbf{M},\,R).

Remark 2.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
be any solution
to (
8
), (
9
) subject to the boundary
conditions (
3
)–(
4
) or (
5
)–(
6
).
Then, by (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), the assumption

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq 2
required in the above statements is
automatically satisfied for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\beta}
,
where
\varepsilon_{\beta}>0
depends only on
\beta
.

Remark 2.4
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
let
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
be any solution to (
8
), (
9
)
subject to the boundary conditions (
3
)–(
4
) or
(
5
)–(
6
).
Then, by the uniform gradient bound (
1.10
), i.e.,

\left\|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1},

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends
only on the coupling parameter
\beta
and on
\Omega

(see (
1.10
)),
it follows that in the set
\Theta(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,R)
the energy density is controlled
by the potential energy density, through a constant depending
only on
\beta
and
\Omega
. In particular, we have

(2.26)

\varepsilon\left|(\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})(x)\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\qquad\mbox{for any }x\in\Theta\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,R\right),

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends
only on
\beta
and
\Omega
.

2.2
Energy estimates through the potential

In this section, we extend to our context the
local energy estimates proved
in
[
6
, Proposition 4.8]

for the pure Allen-Cahn system.
As in
[
6
]
, the argument is mainly based on
refined energy estimates, obtained by
multiplying Equation (
9
) (i.e., Equation (
2.8
))
by suitable functions and then integrating
the result on appropriate domains.
We proceed in two steps, proving first a refined energy
estimate
close
to the zero set of the potential
V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})

and then obtaining an estimate valid in a whole ball.
Since this strategy does not depend on the precise form of the
potential at hand and since Equation (
9
)
looks
formally
exactly the same as in
[
6
]

(this is best seen by looking at the equivalent
form of (
9
) given by (
2.8
)),
on a formal level the same strategy works in our situation as well,
the difference being the appearance of perturbation terms containing the
variable
\mathbf{Q}
, which are due to the coupling term in
V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})
.
However, we will see that
such additional terms are essentially of lower order
away from
\spt\mu_{\star}
and they do not alter the general structure of the
arguments in
[
6
]
. The results of this section
are at the level of a single critical point, i.e., at fixed
\varepsilon>0
.
In the next sections, we shall consider
sequences
of critical points
and we will deal with the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The main result of this section is Proposition
2.5

below, which is the analogue in our context of

[
6
, Proposition 4.8]
. In the statements below,

\varepsilon_{\beta}>0
is the number, depending only on
\beta
, defined
in Remark
2.3
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\,\varepsilon_{\beta}]
and let
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})=(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

be any critical point of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
.
Let
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\Omega
be any ball such that

\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}
on
B
. Let
\kappa\in(0,\,\delta_{\beta})
and

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}\right]
.
Assume that, for the given value of
\kappa
, there holds

(2.27)

\dist(\mathbf{M},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}))<\kappa\qquad\mbox{on }\partial B.

Then, there exists a constant
{\rm C}_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{E}_{0})>0
,
depending only on
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\beta
, such that

(2.28)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Upsilon(\varrho_{\varepsilon}R,\,\kappa))\leq{\rm C}_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{E}_{0})&\left[\kappa\int_{B(x_{0},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}R)}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}(x))+{\varepsilon}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right.\\
&\left.+\varepsilon\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\partial B(x_{0},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}R))+\varepsilon\int_{\partial B(x_{0},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}R)}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\right)\right].\end{split}

Here,
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the uniform bound on
E_{\varepsilon}
provided by
Proposition
2.2
.

Remark 2.5
.

A crucial feature of the estimate (
2.28
)
is that, exactly as in
[
6
]
, it is

linear in
\kappa
.

Remark 2.6
.

As seen in Proposition
2.2
,
the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}
(and hence
{\rm C}_{\Upsilon}
) depends on
\beta
,
the constant
{\rm C}_{\rm pot}
in (
7
),
\Omega
,

\left\|\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\times\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\right\|_{L^{1}(\partial\Omega)}
, and

\left\|\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\times\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{bd}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}
. However, we preferred to
state Proposition
2.5
in
the form above to emphasise that, exactly as in
[
6
]
,
it holds, in fact, given

any
uniform bound on
E_{\varepsilon}
.

It will be convenient to obtain Proposition
2.5

from its scaled version, Proposition
2.6
below,
and Remark
2.2
.

Notation
.

In what follows,
\varepsilon\in(0,\,\varepsilon_{\beta}]
is a fixed number and,
to simplify the notation,

(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
denotes a critical pair
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
after
rescaling according to (
2.9
).
We assume that
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

satisfies the same hypotheses as in
Proposition
2.5
.
Furthermore, in all the results below up to the statement of
Proposition
2.11
,
we set
B=B_{1}
and we always assume that
\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}

on
\overline{B}
. For notational convenience, we set
B_{r}:=B(0,\,r)
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,\,\delta_{\beta})
and

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}\right]
.
Assume that, for the given value of
\kappa
, there holds

(2.29)

\dist(\mathbf{M},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}))<\kappa\qquad\mbox{on }\partial B.

Then, there exists a constant
{\rm C}_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{E}_{0})>0
,
depending only on
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\beta
, such that

(2.30)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Upsilon(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\kappa))\leq{\rm C}_{\Upsilon}(\mathcal{E}_{0})&\left[\kappa\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}(x))+\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right.\\
&\left.+\varepsilon\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}})+\varepsilon\int_{\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\right)\right].\end{split}

Here,
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the uniform bound on
E_{\varepsilon}
provided by
Proposition
2.2
.

The proof of Proposition
2.6

will be achieved through a series of lemmas, adapting to our setting
results in
[
6
, Section 4]
.

Lemma 2.7
.

We have

(2.31)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Upsilon_{\pm})\lesssim\mathfrak{Q}_{\pm},

where

\mathfrak{Q}_{\pm}:=\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\left(\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M})\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x

and the implicit constant on the right-hand side in (
2.31
)
depends only on
\beta
.

Proof.

By definition of
\Upsilon_{\pm}
and Lemma
2.3
,

\mathbf{M}
is so close to the wells
\mathbf{M}_{\pm}
on
\Upsilon_{\pm}
that there holds

\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\gtrsim\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\gtrsim V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})

pointwise, where the implicit constants depend only on
\beta
.
The conclusion follows immediately by dividing the above inequalities by
\varepsilon

and integrating the result over
\Upsilon_{\pm}
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

There holds

(2.32)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{Q}_{\pm}\lesssim&\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&+\varepsilon\kappa\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Pi_{\pm})+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Pi_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma.\end{split}

As a consequence,

(2.33)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\Upsilon_{\pm})\lesssim&\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&+\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}}(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon\kappa\int_{\Gamma_{\pm}}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Pi_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Pi_{\pm}).\end{split}

The implicit constants on the right-hand sides
of (
2.32
), (
2.33
) depend only on
\beta
.

Proof.

For the sake of clarity, we divide the proof into two steps.

Step 1

(Proof of (
2.32
))
.

Testing Equation (
2.8
) against
\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}
and integrating
the result over
\Upsilon_{\pm}
, we obtain

\begin{split}-\varepsilon\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}&(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0.\end{split}

As a consequence (cf.
[
6
, Lemma 4.3]
),

(2.34)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{Q}_{\pm}&=\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&=\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\pm}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma.\end{split}

As in
[
6
, Lemma 4.3 and Lemma 4.5]
, we observe that

\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}=\Gamma_{\pm}\cup\Pi_{\pm}
and that on
\Pi_{\pm}

(which is a subset of
\partial B
, cf. (
2.24
))
the normal derivative equals the radial
derivative, so that

(2.35)

\begin{split}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}&=2\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})-\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M}_{\pm})}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\\
&\lesssim\sqrt{V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M})}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}-\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}\right|^{2}+\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M})\quad\mbox{on }\Pi_{\pm},\end{split}

Thus,

(2.36)

\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\Pi_{\pm}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\lesssim\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Pi_{\pm})+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Pi_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma,

where the implicit constant depends only on
\beta
,
and plugging (
2.36
) into (
2.34
)
we obtain

\begin{split}\mathfrak{Q}_{\pm}\lesssim&\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&+\varepsilon\kappa\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Pi_{\pm})+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Pi_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma.\end{split}

Then, (
2.32
) follows by applying Young’s inequality to
the term
\varepsilon\int_{\Upsilon_{\pm}}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma
.

Step 2

(Proof of (
2.33
))
.

Once (
2.32
) has been obtained,
inequality (
2.33
) follows easily. Indeed, first we
separate the contributions from
\Gamma_{\pm}
and
\Pi_{\pm}
in

\int_{\partial\Upsilon_{\pm}}(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma
,
and we repeat the same computations as in (
2.35
),
(
2.36
). Then, we observe that

(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm})\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\lesssim\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M})\quad\mbox{on }\Pi_{\pm}

We conclude by recalling that, by (
1.6
),
\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\lesssim\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|

pointwise on
\overline{B}
, where the implicit constant depends only on

\beta
, and applying (
2.31
).
∎

Exactly as in
[
6
, Lemma 4.7]
, the coarea formula and a
standard averaging argument yield the following intermediate lemma.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}\right]
.
There exists some number

\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\delta_{\beta},2\delta_{\beta}\right]

such that

(2.37)

\begin{split}\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon,\pm}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\,\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon})}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma&\lesssim\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma_{\varepsilon,\pm}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\,\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon})}\left(\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}\right|+\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&\lesssim E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\Theta(\mathbf{M},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\lesssim\int_{\Theta(\mathbf{M},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon})}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M})\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\end{split}

where the set

\Theta(\mathbf{M},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon})\subset B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}

is defined in (
2.25
) and the implicit constant on the
right-hand side depends only on
the coupling parameter
\beta
and on
\Omega
.

With Lemma
2.7
, Lemma
2.8
, and
Lemma
2.9
at hand, we can prove both
Proposition
2.10
, providing the analogue
of
[
