6
, Proposition 4.1]
, and
Proposition
2.6
.
Recall once again that, by (
1.9
),
whenever
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})=(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
is a solution
to (
8
), (
9
) in
\Omega
, subject to
either (
3
)–(
4
) or (
5
)–(
6
),
the uniform bound

\left\|\mathbf{M}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq{\rm M}_{\beta},

holds, for some positive constant
{\rm M}_{\beta}
depending only on
\beta
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}\right]
. There
exists a constant
K_{\Upsilon}({\rm M}_{\beta})
such that the inequality

(2.38)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M};\Upsilon\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\delta_{\beta}\right)\right)&\leq K_{\Upsilon}({\rm M}_{\beta})\left[\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left(\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right.\\
&\quad\left.+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M};\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}\right)\right]\end{split}

holds.

Proof.

Since the sets
\Pi_{+}
,
\Pi_{-}
are disjoint and their union is contained in
\partial B

(see (
2.24
)), we have

\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Pi_{\pm}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\Pi_{\pm})\leq\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\partial B}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\partial B).

Therefore, choosing
\kappa=\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}
(where

\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}
is the number provided by Lemma
2.9
),
taking the sum of the two inequalities (
2.33
),
and combining the result with (
2.37
), the
conclusion follows from (
2.26
) exactly as
explained in
[
6
, Proposition 4.1]
.
∎

Note that there are no assumption of closeness to the wells
in the statement of Proposition
2.10
,
but at the same time, it provides an estimate on a
fixed
level set.
As in
[
6
]
,
Proposition
2.10
will not be used in the
proof of Proposition
2.6
but rather in
the proof of Proposition
2.11
, which will
be another crucial tool in
Section
3
.

We have finally at disposal all the ingredients to
proceed to the proof of Proposition
2.6
.

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

The estimate (
2.38
) in
Proposition
2.10
is apparently close
to (
2.30
) but it is on a
fixed
level set.
Exactly as in
[
6
]
,
the crux of the proof consists in allowing the level set to vary,
exploiting the assumption of closeness to the wells,
i.e., (
2.29
).

Fix any
\kappa\in\left(0,\,\delta_{\beta}\right)
, and suppose
that (
2.29
) holds.
By continuity of
\mathbf{M}
, the condition (
2.29
) can
be satisfied by
either

\mathbf{M}_{+}

or

\mathbf{M}_{-}
.
Set, for brevity,

\mathbf{N}:=\mathbf{M}_{+}
or
\mathbf{N}:=\mathbf{M}_{-}
, according to which
of the two options in (
2.29
) is realised,
and denote

\mathcal{C}:=\left\{x\in B:\,\kappa\leq\left|\mathbf{M}(x)-\mathbf{N}(x)\right|\leq\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}\right\},

where
\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}
is the number provided by
Lemma
2.9
.

Testing (
2.8
) against
\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}
and
integrating the result over
\mathcal{C}
, we obtain

(2.39)

\begin{split}-\varepsilon&\int_{\partial\mathcal{C}}\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{C}}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M})\cdot\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0,\end{split}

where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
denotes the exterior normal to
\partial\mathcal{C}
.
Note that, by calculus and recalling (
2.2
),

(2.40)

\begin{split}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M})\cdot\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)&=\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}\left(\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})-\ell(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{N})\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\\
&\gtrsim\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\geq 0.\end{split}

Clearly,

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}=\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|+\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{N}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|},

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla\mathbf{M}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)&=\nabla(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)+\nabla\mathbf{N}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\\
&=\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\left|\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\right|^{2}+\nabla\mathbf{N}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right).\end{split}

Assumption (
2.29
) implies

\partial\mathcal{C}=\Gamma(\kappa)\cup\Gamma(\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}),

where we have set

\Gamma(\alpha):=\left\{x\in B_{\varrho}(x_{0}):\,\left|\mathbf{M}(x)-\mathbf{N}(x)\right|=\alpha\right\}

for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, by the identity (
2.39
) we obtain
(recall that the exterior normal
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
to
\Gamma
points in the direction that
increases
\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|
)

(2.41)

\begin{split}\varepsilon&\int_{\Gamma(\kappa)}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma-\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma\left(\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}\right)}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&+\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}\int_{\Gamma(\kappa)}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{N}\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma-\frac{\varepsilon}{\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}}\int_{\Gamma\left(\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}\right)}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{N}\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&+\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{C}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\left|\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{C}}\nabla\mathbf{N}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:test-eq-compu1},\eqref{eq:calculus}}}{{\leq}}0.\end{split}

On the other hand, we have

(2.42)

\varepsilon\int_{\mathcal{C}}\nabla\mathbf{N}\cdot\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\left|\nabla\mathbf{N}\right|^{2}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\left|\nabla\left(\frac{\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\right)\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

and therefore, from (
2.41
), (
2.42
),
and (
2.37
),

(2.43)

\begin{split}\varepsilon&\int_{\Gamma(\kappa)}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}\int_{\Gamma\left(\kappa\right)}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{N}\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:test-eq-compu2},\eqref{eq:test-eq-compu3}}}{{\leq}}\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma\left(\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}\right)}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}\int_{\Gamma\left(\widetilde{\delta}_{\varepsilon}\right)}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{N}\cdot(\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{\left|\nabla\mathbf{N}\right|^{2}}{\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:coarea+average}}}{{\lesssim}}\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa}\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{N}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{N}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\end{split}

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends only on
the parameter
\beta
in the potential
V
and
\Omega
.

Multiplying (
2.43
) by
\kappa
and
combining the result with (
2.33
), we obtain

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\Upsilon)\lesssim\kappa\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left(\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}(x))\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\varepsilon\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}})+\varepsilon\int_{\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\right),

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends only on
\beta
and on
\Omega
. This is precisely the desired
inequality (
2.30
), and so we are done.
∎

With Proposition
2.6
at hand, the proof of
Proposition
2.5
follows immediately.

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

Just scale back Proposition
2.6
to

B(x_{0},\,R)
using the scaling properties in Remark
2.2
.
∎

Proposition
2.5
is concerned
with an energy estimate
close
to the zero set of the potential

V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M})
, in any ball
B=B(x_{0},\,R)
on which
\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\geq 1/2
.
As in
[
6
, Proposition 4.11]
,
using Proposition
2.10
,
we can obtain an estimate of the energy on the
whole
ball

B^{\prime\prime}:=B(x_{0},\,R/2)
essentially in terms of the
integral of the potential on the ball
B^{\prime}:=B(x_{0},\,3R/4)

plus a lower order term.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
be a critical point
of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
. There exists a constant
{\rm K}_{\beta}
, depending
only on
\beta
, so that, for any ball
B:=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\Omega

such that
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
in
B
, there holds

(2.44)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B^{\prime\prime})\leq&{\rm K}_{\beta}\left\{\int_{B^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\varepsilon}{R}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B\setminus B^{\prime\prime})+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{R}\int_{B\setminus B^{\prime\prime}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right\},\end{split}

where
B^{\prime}:=B(x_{0},\,3R/4)
and
B^{\prime\prime}:=B(x_{0},\,R/2)
.

Proof.

By the scaling properties in Remark
2.2
, we may
assume that
B=B_{1}
and then obtain (
2.44
) by
scaling back.

The argument involves two steps.
The first step, corresponding to
[
6
, Lemma 4.12]
,
consists in obtaining the estimate

(2.45)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}})\leq&{\rm C}_{\beta,\,\Omega}\left\{\int_{B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}})+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}\int_{\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\right\},\end{split}

where
\varrho\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}\right]
and

{\rm C}_{\beta,\,\Omega}
is a constant depending only on
\beta

and on
\Omega
.
Similarly to as in
[
6
, Lemma 4.12]
, this follows from the
decomposition

B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}=\Theta\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right)\cup\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}\left(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\,\delta_{\beta}\right)

by applying Remark
2.4

and Proposition
2.10
.
Then, (
2.44
) follows by combining the above inequality
with a usual averaging argument,
ensuring the existence of a distinguished radius

\sigma_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\varrho_{\varepsilon},3/4\right]
such that

E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}\right)\leq 8E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{3/4}\setminus B_{1/2}\right),

and then scaling back, according to (
2.14
).
∎

2.3
Energy decreasing and clearing-out property for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

The goal of this section is to extend to our context
the energy decreasing property and the clearing-out
property proved, for the pure Allen-Cahn system,
in
[
6
, Proposition 1.12 and Proposition 1.13]
,
respectively.
The energy decreasing estimate in
[
6
, Proposition 1.12]
,
here replaced by Proposition
2.14
below,
is the starting point of a delicate iterative
scheme which leads to an extremely
rapid decreasing of the energy in small balls whenever the
(rescaled) energy on the boundary is below a certain fixed threshold.
In turn, such a quantitative decay is
the key ingredient in the proof of the clearing-out property for critical points of the
pure Allen-Cahn system — see
[
6
, proof of Theorem 1.11]
.

In our context,
the coupling term in the potential
is responsible for the presence of perturbation terms and
this entails several modifications to Bethuel’s arguments.
We handle them by exploiting the
quantitative decay and convergence results for the
\mathbf{Q}
-component
already proved in the companion paper
[
9
]
. Relying on them,
we can still follow the same path as in

[
6
, Section 6]
to obtain, in the end,
Theorem
2.12
, which provides the counterpart
of
[
6
, Proposition 1.13]
.

Notation
.

In this section, we consider a
sequence

\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}
of critical
points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}

satisfying the boundary conditions (
3
)–(
4
)
or (
5
)–(
6
) as well as the
assumption (
7
).

We assume throughout that
such a subsequence has been fixed
, although we will keep
extracting subsequences from it whenever necessary.
To keep the notation simple, we
will not emphasise the dependence of the various constants that will
appear in the following course on this fixed subsequence.

As usual, we shall often set

\rho_{\varepsilon}:=\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|
. In the following,
\delta_{\beta}
denotes the positive number, depending
only on
\beta
, given by Lemma
2.3
.
Finally, we recall from Proposition
2.2

that we have the uniform energy bound

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\Omega)\leq\mathcal{E}_{0},

where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
depends only on
\beta
and the boundary data.

Convergence of
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and decay of the Ginzburg-Landau energy of
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
away from
\spt\mu_{\star}

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be any compact set.
Then, for any given
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, there exists a positive number

C_{\alpha}(K)
, depending only
\alpha
and
K
, such that, for any ball

B_{R}=B(x_{0},\,R)\subseteq K
, we have (see
[
9
, Proposition 2.6 and Remark 2.6]
) the quantitative decay

(2.46)

\int_{B_{R}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-1\right)^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}.

Remark 2.7
.

Given a compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
by (
25
) we can find a number

\varepsilon_{1/2}>0
(depending only on
K
)
so that, for any ball
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
and any

\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1/2}R
, there holds

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
on
B(x_{0},\,R)
.
The number

\varepsilon_{1/2}
depends only on the chosen sequence of
critical points
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}
, on the parameter
\beta
,
and on
K
. On the other hand, by (
1.11
), we have

\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq s_{*}(\beta,\,\varepsilon)=1+\varepsilon\kappa_{\star}+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\varepsilon)
,
whence
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq 2
for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{\beta}R
, where

\varepsilon_{\beta}
depends only on
\beta
.
Therefore, choosing

(2.47)

\varepsilon_{K,\beta}:=\min\{\varepsilon_{1/2},\varepsilon_{\beta}\},

we have that
\varepsilon_{K,\beta}
depends only on the sequence of
critical points,
K
,
and
\beta
, and that

(2.48)

\frac{1}{2}\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq 2,\qquad\forall x\in B(x_{0},\,R),

for all balls
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
and
for all
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{K,\beta}R
.

Clearing-out theorem for
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

The main result of this section is the following theorem, providing
an analogue of
[
6
, Proposition 1.13]
in our context.

Theorem 2.12
.

For any compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, there exists positive constants

\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
,
\varepsilon_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
,

R_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
,
C_{\rm well}
,
C_{\rm nrg}
, depending
only on
K
,
\alpha
,
\beta
, and the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}
,
such that the following holds:
if
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
has radius
R\leq R_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
,
if
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\,\varepsilon_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}R\right]
, and if

(2.49)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))\leq 4\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}R,

then

(2.50)

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}))\leq C_{\rm well}\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))}{R}\right)^{1/6}\leq\delta_{\beta}

on
B(x_{0},\,R/2)
. Moreover,

(2.51)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R/2))\leq C_{\rm nrg}\varepsilon\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))}{R}+C_{\alpha}R^{\alpha}\right).

We start by adapting the ‘very weak clearing-out property’ contained in

[
6
, Proposition 6.1]
to our needs. The proof
of Proposition
2.13
below only relies on the clearing-out
property for the
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
-components, the
a priori
bounds
entailed by the maximum principle, and the quadratic structure of
the potential
V
near the set

\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon})
defined in (
2.15
).

Proposition 2.13
.

For any compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

there exists a positive constant
\varepsilon(K,\,\beta)
, depending only
on
K
and
\beta
, and positive constants
\eta_{\beta}

and
C_{\rm weak}=C_{\rm weak}(\beta)
, depending only on

\beta
and
\Omega
, such that
if
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K

is any ball, if
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon(K,\,\beta)R
, and if

(2.52)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))\leq\eta_{\beta}\varepsilon,

then there holds

(2.53)

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x)))\leq C_{\rm weak}\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},R))}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/6}\leq\delta_{\beta}

for any
x\in B(x_{0},\,{7R/8})
.

Proof.

For convenience, we denote
B_{r}:=B(x_{0},\,r)
the ball
of radius
r
and centre
x_{0}
.

First, we choose
\varepsilon(K,\,\beta)=\varepsilon_{K,\beta}
, where
\varepsilon_{K,\,\beta}

is the constant (depending only on
K
and
\beta
) given by (
2.47
).
This ensures that (
2.48
) holds, so that the set

\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon})
is well-defined, for any
\varepsilon
with

0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon(\beta,K)R
; in turn, this implies that
Lemma
2.3

and Lemma
2.4
hold. For ease of notation, let us
temporarily denote
\epsilon:=\varepsilon(K,\,\beta)
.

Assume that (
2.52
) holds, for some constant

\eta_{\beta}>0
to be determined later.
From this assumption and recalling (
2.2
)
as well as the uniform
bounds (
1.9
), (
1.10
), we see that,
for any
\varepsilon>0
,

(2.54)

\displaystyle\left|\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right|\leq\frac{C_{\beta}}{\varepsilon}\qquad\mbox{for any }x\in B_{R},

(2.55)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq\eta_{\beta}\varepsilon^{2},

where
C_{\beta}
is a constant depending only on
\beta
and
\Omega
.
(In fact, (
2.54
) holds globally on
\Omega
.)
Set

(2.56)

\gamma:=\left(\frac{16C_{\beta}^{2}}{\pi}\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{R})}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/3},

and assume

\eta_{\beta}=\frac{\pi\gamma_{\beta}^{3}}{16C_{\beta}^{2}},

where
\gamma_{\beta}:=\frac{\sqrt{2}\beta}{2}\delta_{\beta}^{2}

is the same number as in Lemma
2.3
.
Then, by definition of
\gamma
and the assumption

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))<\eta_{\beta}\varepsilon
,
it follows that
\gamma\leq\gamma_{\beta}
. Next, we claim
that

(2.57)

V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\leq\gamma\leq\gamma_{\beta},\qquad\mbox{for all }x\in B(x_{0},\,7R/8).

Indeed, assume, for the sake of a contradiction, that there
exists some
x_{1}\in B(x_{0},\,7R/8)
such that

V(x_{1},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x_{1}))>\gamma
. Then, by the gradient
bound (
2.54
), we have

(2.58)

V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\geq\frac{\gamma}{2},\qquad\mbox{for all }\in B\left(x_{1},\frac{\gamma\epsilon}{2C_{\beta}}R\right).

On the other hand, since
x_{1}\in B(x_{0},\,7R/8)
,
we can shrink
\epsilon

dependingly only on
\beta
in such a way that there holds

B\left(x_{1},\,\frac{\gamma\epsilon}{2C_{\beta}}R\right)\subset B\left(x_{1},\,\frac{\gamma_{\beta}\epsilon}{2C_{\beta}}R\right)\subset B(x_{0},\,R).

Integrating (
2.58
) over

B\left(x_{1},\,\frac{\gamma\epsilon}{2C_{\beta}}R\right)
and
recalling the definition (
2.56
) of
\gamma
readily
leads to a contradiction, establishing the claim (
2.57
).

With (
2.57
) at hand, we may employ
Lemma
2.4
to yield both

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x)))\leq\delta_{\beta}

and (recalling that
\lambda_{-}=\sqrt{2}\beta
—
see (
2.19
))

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x)))\leq\sqrt{4\left(\sqrt{2}\beta\right)^{-1}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))},

for every
x\in B_{7R/8}
and any
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon\leq\epsilon R
.
It follows that, for every
x\in B_{7R/8}

and any
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon\leq\epsilon R
,

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x))\leq\inf\left\{\delta_{\beta},\,\sqrt{4\left(\sqrt{2}\beta\gamma\right)^{-1}}\right\}\leq\inf\left\{\delta_{\beta},C_{\rm weak}\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,B_{R})}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/6}\right\},

where the constant
C_{\rm weak}
is defined as

C_{\rm weak}:=\left(\frac{512C_{\beta}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\beta^{3}\pi}\right)^{1/6}

and depends only on
\beta
.
We choose

\eta_{\beta}=\min\left\{\frac{\pi\gamma_{\beta}^{3}}{16C_{\beta}^{2}},\left(\frac{\delta_{\beta}}{2C_{\rm weak}}\right)^{6}\right\},

and we conclude the proof by
observing that
\eta_{\beta}
depends only on
\beta

and
\Omega
.∎

Proposition 2.14
.

There exists a constant
C_{0}
, depending only on
\beta

and the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}
given by Proposition
2.2
,
and, for any compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, a number

\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(K,\,\alpha)
such that, for any ball
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
and any
\varepsilon

with
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}R
, there holds

(2.59)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R/2)\right)\leq C_{0}\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)^{3/2}}{\sqrt{R}}+\frac{\varepsilon\,E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)}{R}+C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}\right)\!,\end{split}

where
C_{\alpha}(K)
is the constant, depending only on
\alpha
and
K
,
appearing in (
2.46
).

The proof of Proposition
2.14
follows
a pattern similar to that of
[
6
, Proposition 1.12]
and relies
on the energy estimates of the previous subsection.
The overlap with the arguments leading to
[
6
, Proposition 1.12]

is considerable, therefore we sketch the
main points and address the reader to
[
6
]

when the details are exactly the same.

First of all, we need an analogue of
[
6
, Lemma 2.6]
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})\in C^{\infty}\left(\Omega,\,\mathscr{S}_{0}^{2\times 2}\right)\times C^{\infty}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})

and let
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\Omega
be a ball such that

1/2\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\leq 2
in
B
.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
r\in(\varepsilon R,\,R]
be given. There exists a
constant
{\rm C}_{\rm unf}>0
, depending only on the parameter

\beta
, such that, for any given pair
(\mathbf{Q},\,\mathbf{M})
as above, there exists an element

\mathbf{N}\in\Sigma(\mathbf{Q})
such that

\left|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{N}\right|\leq{\rm C}_{\rm unf}\sqrt{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M};\,\partial B_{r})}\qquad\mbox{on }\partial B_{r}(x_{0}).

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
2.3
, Lemma
2.4
, and
the assumption
1/2\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}\right|\leq 2
in
B
(which implies, on the one hand,
that
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q})
is well-defined and, on the other hand, that it is contained
in the ball of radius
2(1+2\sqrt{2}\beta)^{1/2}
),
the reasoning in
[
6
, Lemma 2.6]
(which is based only on the
previous ingredients, smoothness, and some clever distinction of cases)
carries over, word for word.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

In particular, by (
2.48
), given a sequence
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}

of critical points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
and a compact set

K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
up to extraction of a subsequence,
Lemma
2.15
holds for any
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subseteq K

and any pair
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
,
for any
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{K,\beta}R
,
where
\varepsilon_{K,\beta}
is the number given by (
2.47
).

In the following, we shall denote

(2.60)

\left(\mathbf{M}_{\pm}\right)_{\varepsilon}=\pm\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho_{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\varepsilon}

the maps realising the minimum of the potential
\ell(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

for a given pair
(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
.

We are now ready for proving Proposition
2.14
.

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.

Fix a compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
(Of course, it suffices to consider the case in which
K
has
non-empty interior.)
Let
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{1/2}
, where
\varepsilon_{1/2}>0
is the number, depending
on
K
, in Remark
2.7
.
Let
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\,\varepsilon_{0}R]
.
Then, it follows from
Remark
2.7
that
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2
on
B
.
In particular, this implies that
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x))
is well-defined
for any
x\in B
and
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x))=\{(\mathbf{M}_{+}(x))_{\varepsilon},\,(\mathbf{M}_{-}(x))_{\varepsilon}\}
.

By a usual averaging argument via Fubini’s theorem, we can find a radius

\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{3}{4}R,R\right]
such that

(2.61)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};B)}{R},

(2.62)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{R}\int_{B}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}.

Now, we choose

\kappa_{\varepsilon}:={\rm C}\sqrt{\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)}{R}},

and, by Lemma
2.15
and definition of
\kappa_{\varepsilon}
,
it follows that

(2.63)

\left|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(s)-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}(s)\right|\leq\frac{\kappa_{\varepsilon}}{2},\qquad\mbox{ for all }s\in\partial B_{\varrho_{\varepsilon}},

for either
\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}=\left(\mathbf{M}_{+}\right)_{\varepsilon}
or

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}=\left(\mathbf{M}_{-}\right)_{\varepsilon}
, where
(\mathbf{M}_{\pm})_{\varepsilon}
are given by (
2.60
).

Step 1

(Improved estimates close to
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon})
)
.

This step corresponds to
[
6
, Proposition 5.1]
.
Its purpose is to obtain the inequality

(2.64)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\,\kappa_{\varepsilon})\right)\leq&{\rm K}_{\Upsilon}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{R}}\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)+\int_{B}\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)^{3/2}\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\varepsilon}{R}\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)+\int_{B}\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\right],\end{split}

where the constant
{\rm K_{\Upsilon}}
depends only on the energy bound

\mathcal{E}_{0}
in (
2.7
) and on
\beta
.

Since (
2.64
) is obtained from
Proposition
2.6
, (
2.63
),
and the choice of
\kappa_{\varepsilon}

exactly by the same argument as in
[
6
, Proposition 5.1]
,
we skip the details.

Step 2

(Improved potential estimates)
.

In analogy with
[
6
, Proposition 5.2]
, we now
obtain an ‘improved potential estimate’.
We claim that, for
\varepsilon
and
B
as in the statement, for any given

\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, there exists a constant

{\rm C}_{1}
, depending only on
\beta
and the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}

in Proposition
2.2
, such that

(2.65)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{B_{5R/8}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq{\rm C}_{1}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{R}}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)^{3/2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{R}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)+C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}\right].

To this purpose, by (
2.63
),
we can apply classical averaging arguments
(see, e.g.,
[
6
, Lemma 2.10]
) to deduce that, for some
radius
\tau_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{5R}{8},\,\varrho_{\varepsilon}\right]
, there holds

\tau_{\varepsilon}E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}\right)\leq 16E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\Upsilon_{\varepsilon}(\varrho_{\varepsilon},\,\kappa_{\varepsilon})\right)

Thus, by (
2.64
),

(2.66)

\begin{split}\tau_{\varepsilon}E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}\right)\leq&16{\rm K}_{\Upsilon}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{R}}\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)+\int_{B}\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)^{3/2}\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\varepsilon}{R}\left(\int_{B}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)+\int_{B}\varepsilon\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\right)\right],\end{split}

On the other hand, thanks to Lemma
1.6
,
Lemma
2.1
, and (
2.46
), we have

(2.67)

\tau_{\varepsilon}E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{B_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\,{\mathrm{d}}x-C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}

for every
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
. Now, we combine (
2.67
)
and (
2.64
), and we shrink
\varepsilon_{0}
, depending
only on
K
and
\alpha
, in such a way that

\varepsilon_{0}(\diam K)\left(2^{3/2}\varepsilon^{1/2}C_{\alpha}(K)^{1/2}(\diam K)^{\alpha/2}+\varepsilon_{0}\right)\leq 1.

Then, (
2.65
)
follows by choosing
{\rm C}_{1}=\max\{32{\rm K}_{\Upsilon},\,2\}
and observing
that
{\rm C}_{1}
depends only on the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}
in (
2.7
)
and
\beta
.

Step 3

(Conclusion)
.

Once that (
2.65
) has been obtained,
the desired inequality (
2.59
) follows by
matching (
2.65
)
with (the scaled version of) (
2.45
)
and with (
2.61
), possibly shrinking
again
\varepsilon_{0}
(still in a way depending only on
K
and
\alpha
only) and
choosing appropriately the constant
C_{0}
, depending only on
the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}
in (
2.7
) and
\beta
.
The argument goes on exactly the same way
as in the proof of
[
6
, Proposition 1.12]

(see
[
6
, Section 5.3]
),
to which the reader is addressed for full details.
∎

We now show that, under a suitable smallness assumption
for the energy, (
2.68
) below, the
estimate (
2.59
) can be
iterated

to yield that
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x)
stays
close to
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x))
,
uniformly with respect to
x
and
\varepsilon
.
More precisely, we have the following statement, providing the
counterpart in our context of
[
6
, Proposition 6.5 and Corollary 6.4]
.

Proposition 2.16
.

For any compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and any
\delta>0
,
there exist positive constants
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(K,\,\delta)
,

R_{1}=R_{1}(K,\,\delta)
,
\eta_{1}=\eta_{1}(K,\,\delta)
,
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(K,\,\delta)
, depending only on
K
,
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, such that the following holds:
if a ball
B=B(x_{0},\,R)\subseteq K
has radius
R\leq R_{1}
,
if
\varepsilon
satisfies
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}R
and if

(2.68)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)\leq\eta_{1}R,

then

(2.69)

\dist\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right)\leq\delta

for any
x\in B(x_{0},\,3R/4)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in B(x_{0},\,3R/4)
be an arbitrary point.
For the sake of brevity, we will use the
notation
m_{\varepsilon}:=\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x)))
,

\bar{R}:=R/4
, and

E(r):=E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{r}(x)),\qquad\Phi(r):=\frac{E(r)}{r}

for any
r\in(0,\,\bar{R}]
.
Moreover, we fix an arbitrary
\alpha\in(1,\,2)

and we consider
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(K,\,\alpha)

as given by
Proposition
2.14
, and, with
a slight abuse of notation,

C_{0}=C_{0}(K,\,\alpha,\,\beta)

as the maximum between
the constant
C_{0}
in the right-hand side of (
2.59
)
and
C_{0}C_{\alpha}(K)
.
We define

(2.70)

\Lambda_{\alpha}:=2^{\alpha}C_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}2^{(\alpha-2)j}

and note that the series at the right-hand side
converges, because
\alpha<2
.

Now, we choose suitable parameters
\varepsilon_{1}
,

R_{1}
, and
\eta_{1}
. First, we take

(2.71)

\varepsilon_{1}:=\min\left\{\varepsilon_{0},\,\frac{1}{8C_{0}}\right\}\!,

so that
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(K,\,\alpha,\,\beta)
.

Due to the
L^{\infty}
-gradient bound on
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

given by (
1.9
)
and the quadratic growth of
V

(cf. (
2.1
) and (
1.3
)),
there exists a constant
\lambda_{1}
,
depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
(and hence on
K
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
)
but not on
\varepsilon
, such that

(2.72)

E\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)\geq\lambda_{1}\varepsilon\,m_{\varepsilon}^{2}

Next, we take
\eta_{1}
and
R_{1}
small enough that

(2.73)

\left(4\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{8C_{0}},\qquad\left(16\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\right)^{1/2}\leq\delta(\varepsilon_{1}\lambda_{1})^{1/2}

In particular, our choice of
\varepsilon_{1}
,
\eta_{1}
, and
R_{1}
implies that

(2.74)

C_{0}\left(4\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\right)^{1/2}+C_{0}\varepsilon_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4}.

For further reference, we also note that

(2.75)

\begin{split}\Phi(\bar{R})\leq\frac{4E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B)}{R}\leq 4\eta_{1}\end{split}

because of the assumption (
2.68
).

For any positive integer
n
such
that
2^{n}\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}\bar{R}
, we claim that

(2.76)

E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)\leq\frac{E(\bar{R})}{4^{n}}+2^{\alpha}C_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}2^{(\alpha-2)j}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\!.

To prove this claim, we iterate the estimate given
by Proposition
2.14
,
along the lines of classical iteration arguments
(see, e.g.,
[
3
, Lemma B.3]
or
[
14
, Lemma 5.13]
).
We will give a complete proof of (
2.76
)
below; for the time being, we assume (
2.76
)
holds and complete the proof of the proposition.
We evaluate both sides of (
2.76
)
when
n
is such that
\varepsilon 2^{n}=\varepsilon_{1}\bar{R}
.
Keeping (
2.70
) and (
2.75
)
into account, we obtain

\begin{split}E\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}E(\bar{R})}{\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\bar{R}^{2}}+\Lambda_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)^{\alpha}\leq\frac{4\varepsilon^{2}\eta_{1}}{\varepsilon_{1}^{2}\bar{R}}+\Lambda_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)^{\alpha}\end{split}

and, since
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}R=4\varepsilon_{1}\bar{R}

and
R\leq R_{1}
by assumption,

(2.77)

E\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)\leq\left(16\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\right)\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}

Combining this estimate with (
2.72
),
we obtain

m_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\varepsilon}E\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{1}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}\varepsilon_{1}}\left(16\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\right)\!.

Keeping (
2.73
) into account, we finally
conclude
m_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\delta^{2}
, which is what
we wanted to prove.
∎

Proof of (
2.76
).

The proof proceeds by induction on
n
. For
n=1
,
Proposition
2.14
implies

\begin{split}E\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2}\right)\leq C_{0}\left(\Phi(\bar{R})^{1/2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{\bar{R}}\right)E(\bar{R})+C_{0}\bar{R}^{\alpha}\end{split}

On the other hand, recalling (
2.74
),
(
2.75
), and (
2.73
), we have

\begin{split}E\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2}\right)\leq C_{0}\left(2\eta_{1}^{1/2}+\varepsilon_{1}\right)E(\bar{R})+C_{0}\bar{R}^{\alpha}\leq\frac{E(\bar{R})}{4}+C_{0}\bar{R}^{\alpha}.\end{split}

Therefore, the claim (
2.76
)
holds true when
n=1
. Now, suppose
n

is a positive integer, with
2^{n}\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}\bar{R}
,
such that (
2.76
) is satisfied.
Then, we can apply Proposition
2.14

again:

(2.78)

\begin{split}E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n+1}}\right)\leq C_{0}\left(\Phi\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{1/2}+\frac{2^{n}\varepsilon}{\bar{R}}\right)E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)+C_{0}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\end{split}

By dividing both sides of (
2.76
)
by
\bar{R}/2^{n}
, and then applying (
2.70
)
and (
2.75
) to further estimate the right-hand side,
we deduce

(2.79)

\begin{split}\Phi\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)\leq\frac{\Phi(\bar{R})}{2^{n}}+2^{\alpha}C_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}2^{(\alpha-2)j}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha-1}\leq 4\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\end{split}

We inject (
2.79
) into the right-hand
side of (
2.78
), use the assumption
that
\varepsilon 2^{n}\leq\varepsilon_{1}\bar{R}
and our choice (
2.74
)
of
\eta_{1}
and
R_{1}
:

(2.80)

\begin{split}E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n+1}}\right)&\leq C_{0}\left(\left(4\eta_{1}+\Lambda_{\alpha}R_{1}^{\alpha-1}\right)^{1/2}+\varepsilon_{1}\right)E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)+C_{0}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{4}E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)+C_{0}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\end{split}

Finally, from the induction assumption we deduce

\begin{split}E\!\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n+1}}\right)&\leq\frac{E(\bar{R})}{4^{n+1}}+2^{\alpha-2}C_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}2^{(\alpha-2)j}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}+C_{0}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\\
&\leq\frac{E(\bar{R})}{4^{n+1}}+C_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{n}2^{(\alpha-2)j}\left(\frac{\bar{R}}{2^{n}}\right)^{\alpha}\end{split}

This completes the proof of (
2.76
),
and hence of Proposition
2.16
.
∎

Next, following the line of the argument in
[
6
, Section 6]
,
we use Proposition
2.16
to show that, if the energy is
appropriately small in a ball, then it decays as fast as
\varepsilon
in
a slightly smaller ball.
Proposition
2.17
below is the analogue, in our context,
of
[
6
, Proposition 6.8]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

For any compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

there exists positive a constant
C_{\rm dec}
,
depending only on
\beta
,
such that, for any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
,
if
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
, if
\varepsilon\in(0,\,\varepsilon_{K,\,\beta}R]
,
and if

(2.81)

\dist\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon})\right)\leq\delta_{\beta}\qquad\mbox{in }B(x_{0},\,{3R/4}),

then

(2.82)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,5R/8))\leq C_{\rm dec}\varepsilon\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))}{R}+C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}\right).

Proof.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be a compact set
and assume that
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{K,\,\beta}
, where
\varepsilon_{K,\,\beta}

is the constant defined in (
2.47
),
so that
1/2\leq\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq 2
in
K

for any
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{K,\,\beta}
. From now on,
we argue exactly as in
[
6
, Proposition 6.8]
.

By a classical averaging argument based on Fubini’s theorem, we can find

r_{\varepsilon}\in\left[\frac{5R}{8},\,\frac{3R}{4}\right]
such that

(2.83)

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\partial B_{r_{\varepsilon}}\right)\leq 16\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{R})}{R}

(2.84)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\sqrt{V(\,\cdot,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}s\leq 16\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{R})}{R},

where, for the sake of brevity, we have set
B_{R}:=B(x_{0},\,R)
and

B_{r_{\varepsilon}}:=B(x_{0},\,r_{\varepsilon})
.
By assumption (
2.81
) and recalling that

\left|(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon}-(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}\right|>2
independently of
x\in K

by (
2.16
), we have

\left|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}\right|\leq\delta_{\beta}

for
either

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}=(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon}

or

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}=(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}
.
Testing (
2.8
) against
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}

and integrating over
B_{r_{\varepsilon}}
, we find

(2.85)

\begin{split}\int_{B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}&\left(\varepsilon\nabla(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\cdot(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&=\int_{\partial B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\varepsilon(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon})\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\,{\mathrm{d}}s.\end{split}

By (
2.81
) and Lemma
2.3
,
the pointwise estimate

(2.86)

\nabla_{\mathbf{M}}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\cdot(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon})\gtrsim V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

holds on
B_{3R/4}
, where the implicit constant depends only on
\beta
.
On the other hand, by Young’s inequality and (
1.6
), for
any choice of
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, we have

(2.87)

\begin{split}\varepsilon\int_{B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\nabla\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\,{\mathrm{d}}x&\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon\int_{B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\left(C_{\beta}\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\leq\varepsilon C_{\alpha}C_{\beta}R^{\alpha}+\varepsilon\int_{B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\end{split}

where
C_{\beta}>0
is a suitable constant depending only on
\beta
and

C_{\alpha}=C_{\alpha}(K)

is the constant in (
2.46
). Finally,
by (
2.81
), (
2.83
),
Lemma
2.3
, and an application of Young’s inequality,
we get

(2.88)

\begin{split}\varepsilon\int_{\partial B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon})\partial_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\,{\mathrm{d}}s&\leq\varepsilon\int_{\partial B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\left|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}\right|\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon\int_{\partial B_{r_{\varepsilon}}}\sqrt{V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:eps-decay-E-fubini}}}{{\lesssim}}\varepsilon\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B_{R})}{R}\right)\end{split}

Combining (
2.85
)
with (
2.86
), (
2.87
),
and (
2.88
)
yields (
2.82
) for an appropriate choice of
C_{\rm dec}
,
depending only on
\beta
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

With Proposition
2.16
,
Proposition
2.17
,
and Proposition
2.13

at hand, it is now easy to deduce the next result,
the last piece of information still missing to carry out the
argument in
[
6
, Section 6]
and
conclude the proof of Theorem
2.12
.

Proposition 2.18
.

For any compact set
K\subseteq\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, there exists positive constants

\varepsilon_{2}
,
\eta_{2}
,
R_{2}
, and
C_{\rm well}
, depending only on

K
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
, such that, if
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K

has radius
R\leq R_{2}
, if
\varepsilon\in(0,\,\varepsilon_{2}R]
, and if

(2.89)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))\leq\eta_{2}R,

then

(2.90)

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}))\leq C_{\rm well}\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,R))}{R}\right)^{1/6}\leq\delta_{\beta}\qquad\mbox{on }B(x_{0},\,R/2).

Proof.

The proof follows very closely the path
of
[
6
, Proposition 6.9]
.

First, for fixed
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
and
\delta=\delta_{\beta}
,
we first choose
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{1}
,
\eta_{2}=\eta_{1}
, and

R_{2}=R_{1}
as given by Proposition
2.16
and we let

R\leq R_{2}
, so that we the conclusion of
Proposition
2.16
holds.
Thus, for any
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{2}R
, we have

\dist\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right)\leq\delta_{\beta}\qquad\mbox{for any }x\in B(x_{0},\,3R/4).

Then, we may apply Proposition
2.17

to obtain that (
2.82
) holds. In turn, up to reducing

\eta_{2}
and
R_{2}
so that
(again, this can be done dependingly only on
K
,
\alpha
,
and
\beta
)

C_{\rm dec}\eta_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\{\eta_{1},\eta_{\beta}\},\qquad C_{\rm dec}C_{\alpha}(K)R_{2}^{\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\{\eta_{1},\eta_{\beta}\},

it follows that

C_{\rm dec}\left(\eta_{2}+C_{\alpha}(K)R_{2}^{\alpha}\right)\leq\min\{\eta_{1},\eta_{\beta}\}.

Thus, we are allowed to
employ Proposition
2.13
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
2.12
.

Proof of Theorem
2.12
.

Exactly as for
[
6
, Theorem 1.11]
(of which the
aforementioned
[
6
, Proposition 1.13]
is just a
scaled version), the proof is achieved immediately
choosing

\varepsilon_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}=\varepsilon_{2},\qquad R_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}=R_{2},\qquad\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}=\frac{1}{4}\eta_{2},

and applying Proposition
2.18
to
get (
2.50
), followed by an application of
Proposition
2.17
to
get (
2.51
).
∎

3
The limiting measures
\nu_{\star}
and
\zeta_{\star}

In this section, we study the asymptotic behaviour of
the energy densities
\nu_{\varepsilon}
and the potential energy
densities
\zeta_{\varepsilon}
(introduced in (
13
)
and in (
21
), respectively) as
well as
the properties of the respective limiting measures

\nu_{\star}
and
\zeta_{\star}
. We shall see that, exactly as in the
pure vectorial Allen-Cahn theory as developed in
[
6
]
,
the prominent role in the investigation of the structure
of the set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}:=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

is played by
\zeta_{\star}
rather than by
\nu_{\star}
.
This is because
\spt\zeta_{\star}=\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
, and
the ultimate reason for this lies in the fact
that
\zeta_{\star}
solves an equation, Equation (
3.56
),
while an analogous property is not known for
\nu_{\star}
.
Deferring a deeper discussion of this crucial point to later,
we observe that such an equation is key to prove that the function

r\mapsto\frac{1}{r}\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))
is monotone
non-decreasing for every
x_{0}\in\Omega

(while, once again, an analogous
property is not known for
\nu_{\star}
, in sharp contrast with the
scalar case). In turn, the monotonicity of
\frac{1}{r}\zeta_{\star}
immediately
implies that
\zeta_{\star}
has a well-defined density

\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})
at any point
x_{0}\in\Omega
with respect
to the measure
\lambda_{\star}:=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
,
hence in particular it is
absolutely continuous with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
. Furthermore, in
Proposition
3.19
we prove that the density

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}
of
\nu_{\star}
exists at
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-a.e. point of

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
and that the inequality

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}\lesssim\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
holds at
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-a.e. point
of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
for a constant depending only on
\beta
and

\Omega
. Moreover, from Proposition
3.18

and the inequality
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}\lesssim\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
, we obtain
the inequalities of measures

\zeta_{\star}\leq\nu_{\star}\quad\mbox{in }\Omega,\qquad\nu_{\star}\lesssim\zeta_{\star}\quad\mbox{in }\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}.

In particular, the set
\spt\zeta_{\star}

coincides exactly with
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.

The monotonicity of
\frac{1}{r}\zeta_{\star}
will be proved
in Proposition
3.18
,
at the end of a rather long path that goes trough the same steps
as in
[
6
]
.
As we will see
along this Section, this is possible because we can get rid of the
perturbation terms containing
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
at the
\varepsilon
-level and
prove that they vanish in an appropriately strong sense as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
so that the analysis at the ‘
\star
-level’ boils down to the one
for the pure Allen-Cahn system in
[
6
]
.

The key tools to this purpose are Theorem
2.12
and the strong convergence
properties of
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
towards
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
away from
\spt\mu_{\star}
,
obtained in
[
9
]
and recalled in Theorem
A
,
which, combined, yield a clearing-out property
for
\nu_{\star}
, see item
2
in Theorem
3.3
.
Such a property is crucial for our arguments.
Moreover, arguing like in
[
6
, Theorem 1.16]
,
we obtain in Theorem
3.7

another kind of clearing-out type property for
\nu_{\star}
, stating essentially that there are
no ‘islands’ in
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. The argument
relies on Proposition
2.11
and the Pohozaev
identity (Lemma
1.5
). Again, this is
possible because the contribution of the
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
-part is well controlled at the
\varepsilon
-level thanks to the improved bounds from

[
9
, Proposition 2.6]
.

Together,
the two types of clearing-out results above entail,
by arguing along the lines of
[
6
, Section 7]
,
rectifiability and local connectedness properties of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.

Concerning the proof of the monotonicity of
\frac{1}{r}\zeta_{\star}
,
we argue again along the lines of
[
6
]
.
A pivotal role will be played by
the
limiting Hopf differential

\omega_{\star}
, introduced in
Section
3.3
. The limiting Hopf differential
\omega_{\star}
is related to

\zeta_{\star}
by Proposition
3.13
, specifically by (
3.56
).
Testing (
3.56
) against suitable vector fields and
using the rectifiability properties of

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
(see item
4
in Theorem
3.3
and the results in Section
3.4
),
we can show that there exist certain
crucial relations between
\zeta_{\star}
and the components of
\omega_{\star}

satisfied at
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-almost every point of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
,
see Theorem
3.20
. With those relations
at hand, and using again (
3.56
), we can prove that

\zeta_{\star}
is the weight measure of a one-dimensional
varifold

\mathbb{V}_{\star}
in
\Omega
, for which can compute the first variation.
We show that
\mathbb{V}_{\star}
is stationary as a varifold in

\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

(Theorem
4.1
), and then we take advantage of this fact to
compute its first variation as a varifold in
\Omega
,
obtaining the balance law (
4.6
)–(
4.7
)
in Theorem
4.2
. Such a balance law relates
\spt\mu_{\star}
and

\spt\nu_{\star}
and provides a criticality property for them.
Relying again on Theorem
3.3
and on a classical structure
results for stationary 1-varifolds with locally bounded positive density due to W. Allard
and F.J. Almgren
[
1
]
, we finally conclude
in Theorem
4.3

that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is locally the union of relatively open segments,
with locally constant densities.
In fact, from
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
,
at sufficiently small scale around any point in

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
, where the exceptional set

\mathfrak{E}_{\star}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is a
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-null set,
such a union consists of a
single
segment with constant density.

3.1
Lower bounds on
\nu_{\star}
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
, and its properties

In Equation (
13
),
we defined the energy densities

\nu_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}),

dually seen as measures in
\Omega
. It follows
from Theorem
1.4
and (
7
)
that, up to extraction
of a subsequence,
\nu_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly* in the
sense of measures in
\Omega
to a limiting
Radon measure
\nu_{\star}
.
In this section,
we prove some
properties of
\nu_{\star}
. More precisely,
following the reasoning in
[
6
, Section 7]
,
we prove clearing-out properties for
\nu_{\star}
far from

\spt\mu_{\star}
that allow us to show that the support
of
\nu_{\star}
coincides locally, i.e., in
any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
with the concentration set for the
1
-density of
\nu_{\star}

and that
it is locally a finite union of path-connected components.
(See Theorem
3.3
below for the precise statements.)
As a consequence,
we obtain locally uniform convergence in

\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})

of the
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
-component towards the
limiting map
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
given by
item
1
of Theorem
A

(Cf. Theorem
3.10
.)

Notation
.

In this section, and in the forthcoming ones, we continue using
the notation introduced in Section
2.3
.

Auxiliary lemmas and preliminary remarks

First of all, we show that the full potential

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

can be locally replaced with the ‘Allen-Cahn’ potential energy

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})
in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
This result, contained in Lemma
3.1
below,
will be key to many results of this and the next sections.
The proof of Lemma
3.1
(and in turn that
of Lemma
3.2
) exploits crucially (
26
)

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\left\{\left(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right\}
be a sequence of critical
points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, subject to boundary conditions either as
in (
3
)–(
4
) or as in (
5
)–(
6
), and
assume that (
7
) holds.
Let
B:=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be any ball.
Then,

(3.1)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B}\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0.

Proof.

Integrating both sides of (
2.4
) over
B
,
we have

\begin{split}\int_{B}&\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&=\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}\left(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{B}\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\frac{1-\rho_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\right)\left(\sqrt{2}+\beta+\beta\rho_{\varepsilon}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\chi_{\varepsilon}\left|B\right|.\end{split}

By (
1.5
), the last term on the right-hand
side tends to
\kappa_{\star}^{2}\left|B\right|
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
On the other hand, by (
26
) (with
p=2
)
and (
25
), as
\varepsilon\to 0

we get both

\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}\left(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\to\kappa_{\star}^{2}\left|B\right|

and (recalling also the definition (
1.1
) of
\kappa_{\star}
)

\int_{B}\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\frac{1-\rho}{\varepsilon}\right)\left(\sqrt{2}+\beta+\beta\rho_{\varepsilon}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\to-\kappa_{\star}\frac{\beta}{2}\left(\sqrt{2}+2\beta\right)=-2\kappa_{\star}^{2}\left|B\right|.

Summing the three contributions above, we obtain
exactly (
3.1
).
∎

The following strengthened version of Lemma
3.1

will be useful later on.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\left\{\left(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right\}
be a sequence of critical
points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, subject to boundary conditions either as
in (
3
)–(
4
) or as in (
5
)–(
6
), and
assume that (
7
) holds.
Let
B:=B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be any ball.
Then, for every
p
with
1\leq p<+\infty
,

(3.2)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B}\left|\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

The conclusion follows easily by
combining (
2.4
), the definition (
1.1
) of

\kappa_{\star}
, and the uniform convergence

\rho_{\varepsilon}\to 1
in
B

from (
25
) with (
26
).
∎

Properties of
\nu_{\star}

We now come to the limiting energy measure
\nu_{\star}
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, we let

(3.3)

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot\,,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}).

Next, we define

(3.4)

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0}):=\liminf_{r\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{2r},\qquad\forall x_{0}\in\Omega.

Similarly, we set

(3.5)

\mathfrak{e}^{\star}(x_{0}):=\limsup_{r\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{2r},\qquad\forall x_{0}\in\Omega.

Next, taken a compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
we define the set

(3.6)

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}:=\left\{x\in K\,\colon\,\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x)>0\right\}.

Theorem
3.3
and Theorem
3.10
below,
providing analogues of
[
6
, Theorem 1.14 and Theorem 1.16]

and of
[
6
, Theorem 1.2]
, respectively, constitute the main results
of this section.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\nu_{\varepsilon}
be the energy-measures defined by (
13
).
Then, there exists a non-negative Radon measure
\nu_{\star}
on
\Omega

and a (not-relabelled)
subsequence so that
\nu_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\nu_{\star}
weakly*
as Radon measures in
\Omega
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
In addition, the measure
\nu_{\star}
has the following properties.

1.

Let
\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
be the energy densities defined
in (
3.3
). Then,
for any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
we have
\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K\rightharpoonup\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K

weakly* as measure in
K
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

2.

For any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and
any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
,
there exists positive constant
\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
and

R_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
,
depending only on
K
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
,
such that, if
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
has radius
R\leq R_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}

and if

(3.7)

\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,R)}\right)\leq\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}R,

then

(3.8)

\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{B\left(x_{0},\,\frac{R}{2}\right)}\right)=0.

3.

Define the sets

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}:=\left\{x\in K\,\colon\,\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x)\geq\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}\right\},\qquad\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{({\rm sup})}:=\left\{x\in K\,\colon\,\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x)\geq\eta_{\star,K}\right\},

where

(3.9)

\eta_{\star,K}:=\sup_{\alpha\in(1,\,2)}\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}.

Then,

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}=\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}=\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{({\rm sup})}.

4.

The support of
\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K
coincides with
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
. In
addition,
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
is a closed,

\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable set,
with
\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K})<+\infty
. Moreover, for any

x_{0}\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and for any
r>0
such that

B(x_{0},\,2r)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
there exists

\rho_{0}\in(r,\,2r)
such that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\cap B(x_{0},\,\rho_{0})

is a finite union of path-connected components.

Remark 3.1
.

The very same argument as in the proof
of
[
6
, Proposition 7.3]
shows that

(3.10)

\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K})\leq{\rm C}_{{\rm H},K}\mathcal{E}_{0},

where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the global bound on
E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Omega)

given by (
2.7
) and
{\rm C}_{{\rm H},K}

can actually be chosen as
{\rm C}_{{\rm H},K}=50/\eta_{\star,K}
.

Remark 3.2
.

At the current stage, we do not know whether

\nu_{\star}(\spt\mu_{\star})=0

or not.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
, first part.

Here, we accomplish the first three steps in the proof
of Theorem
3.3
, i.e., the existence of

\nu_{\star}
and the proof of items
1
,
2
,
and
3
.
Proving item
4
requires some further clearing-out
properties for
\nu_{\star}
that, being of interest in their own, will
be stated and proved separately. The proof of item
4
will
be therefore provided thereafter.

Step 1

(Existence of
\nu_{\star}
and proof of item
1
)
.

By definition, we have

(3.11)

\nu_{\varepsilon}=\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}+\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right)

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Thus, on the one hand,
the mass of
\nu_{\varepsilon}
on
\Omega
is given by

(3.12)

\nu_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)=E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Omega)+\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x;

and, thanks to the global energy
bound (
2.7
), assumption (
7
),
and (
2.5
),
the right-hand side of (
3.12
)
is bounded independently of
\varepsilon
,
so that, by standard compactness properties of
Radon measures, there exist a (not-relabelled) subsequence
and a non-negative Radon measure
\nu_{\star}
in
\Omega
such that

(3.13)

\nu_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\nu_{\star}

weakly* as measures in
\Omega
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
. On the
other hand, if
K
is any compact set contained in

\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, then (
3.11
)
and Lemma
3.1
together with a
standard covering argument entail that

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K
converge to
\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K

weakly* as measures in
K
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Step 2

(Proof of item
2
and item
3
)
.

The proof of is almost identical to that of
[
6
, Theorem 1.14]

(see
[
6
, Section 7.1]
).
The only difference is given by the presence of the perturbation
term in (
2.51
), which however vanishes in the limit

\varepsilon\to 0
. Anyway, for the reader’s convenience, we provide some details.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be any compact set,
which we may assume to have non-empty interior, and fix any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
.
Let
\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
,
R_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
, and

\varepsilon_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}
be the same numbers,
depending only on
K
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
and the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, as given by Theorem
2.12
.
(For ease of notation, we drop
the indices
K
and
\alpha
in the following computations.)

By the definition (
3.3
) of
\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}
,
for any ball

B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, we have

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,B(x_{0},\,R))=\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(B(x_{0},\,R))=\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,R)}\right),

and therefore, by item
1
,
for any
r\in(0,\,R)
we have

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(B(x_{0},\,r))=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nu_{\varepsilon}(B(x_{0},\,r))\leq\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,r)}\right)\leq\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,R)).

Thus, if
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset K
is any ball with radius
R\leq R_{\star}

and we assume that (
3.7
) holds, then for any

r\in(0,\,R)
, there exists
\varepsilon_{r}>0

so that, for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{r}R
,

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(B(x_{0},\,r))\leq\frac{5}{4}\eta_{\star}R.

Therefore, choosing
r=8R/9
we get

\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}(B(x_{0},\,r))\leq 2\eta_{\star}r.

Using (
2.51
),
we obtain

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\left(B\left(x_{0},\,\frac{5R}{9}\right)\right)=\widetilde{\nu}_{\varepsilon}\left(B\left(x_{0},\,\frac{5r}{8}\right)\right)&\leq{\rm C}_{\rm nrg}\varepsilon\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}+C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}\right)\\
&\leq\varepsilon\left(\frac{9}{8}\eta_{\star}+C_{\alpha}(K)R^{\alpha}\right).\end{split}

for any
\varepsilon\leq\min\{\varepsilon_{\star},\,\varepsilon_{r}\}R
.
Letting

\varepsilon\to 0
, the conclusion follows.

Once item
2
has been obtained, item
3

follows immediately. Indeed, on the one hand, the inclusions

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{({\rm sup})}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}

are obvious. On the other hand, it follows from item
2
that
for any
x_{0}\in K
,
either

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})=0
or the
inequality

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})\geq\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}

holds for every
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, hence it must hold also passing to the
supremum over
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
on both sides. Since
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})

does not depend on
\alpha
, we must have

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})\geq\sup_{\alpha\in(1,\,2)}\eta_{\star,K}^{(\alpha)}=\eta_{\star,K}.

Thus
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}^{({\rm sup})}
,
and the conclusion follows.
∎

A first consequence of Theorem
3.3
is
the following lemma, which refines
[
9
, Lemma 3.17]

and will be useful later on.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\left\{\left(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right\}
be a sequence of critical
points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
, subject to boundary conditions either as
in (
3
)–(
4
) or as in (
5
)–(
6
), and
assume that (
7
) holds.
Then,
for any simply connected open set
G\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

with smooth boundary, we have

(3.14)

c_{\beta}\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathrm{S}_{\mathbf{M}_{\star}}\cap G)\leq\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}G,

where
c_{\beta}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right)^{3/2}
.
Consequently,

(3.15)

c_{\beta}\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathrm{S}_{\mathbf{M}_{\star}}\leq\nu_{\star}

as measures in
\Omega
.

Proof.

The inequality (
3.14
) is a straightforward
consequence of
[
9
, (3.59)]

and of item
1
of Theorem
3.3
.
Given (
3.14
), (
3.15
) follows easily by the strong convergence

\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{M}_{\star}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
given by
Theorem
A
.
∎

As another elementary consequence of the clearing-out
property for
\nu_{\star}
in
2
of
Theorem
3.3
, we
have the following lemma, corresponding
to
[
6
, Lemma 7.9 and Proposition 7.2]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be
any compact set.

1.

For any
x_{0}\in{\rm int}\,K\setminus\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
,
there exists a positive radius
r_{x_{0}}
such that

B(x_{0},\,r_{0})\subset K
and

\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})}\right)=0
. In
particular,
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x)=\mathfrak{e}^{\star}(x)=0

for any
x\in B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})
.

2.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
be such that

B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})\subset K
. Then,

\nu_{\star}\left(B(x_{0},\,r)\right)\geq\eta_{\star,K}r

for any
r\in(0,\,r_{0}]
.

3.

The set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
is a closed subset
of
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
(and hence of
\Omega
).

Proof.

Being the proof truly elementary, for the sake of brevity,
it is left to the reader (who can however
consult
[
6
, Section 7.2]

for full details).
∎

The proof of item
4
of Theorem
3.3
requires
to adapt some results from
[
6
]
. Our fist goal is
providing an analogue of
[
6
, Proposition 4.15]
accounting
for the presence of the perturbation due to the coupling term in
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
.

Similarly to as in
[
6
, Section 1.5]
,
we introduce the following piece of notation:
let
\mathcal{U}\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

be a bounded, open set and, for
\delta>0
, denote

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\delta}:=\left\{x\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}:\dist(x,\,\mathcal{U})\leq\delta\right\},

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\delta}:=\left\{x\in\Omega\setminus\left(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\mathcal{U}\right):\dist(x,\,\mathcal{U})\leq\delta\right\}.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be a compact set,
let
\mathcal{U}\subset K
be a bounded, open
set, and let
\delta>0
be so small that

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}\subset K
.
Then, for any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)

there exists a constant
C_{\rm ext}(\mathcal{U},\,\delta)
, possibly depending
on
\alpha
,
\beta
,
K
,
\Omega
, and
\mathcal{U}

and
\delta
, but not on
\varepsilon
, such that

(3.18)

E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{U}_{\delta/4}\right)\leq C_{\rm ext}(\mathcal{U},\,\delta)\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{V}_{\delta})+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\mathcal{U}_{\delta})+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\right).

Proof.

The proof of
[
6
, Proposition 4.15]
involves only
three ingredients: an application of the Pohozaev
inequality with an appropriate test field to obtain
control of the energy on
\mathcal{U}_{\delta/4}

with that on ‘external domain’
\mathcal{V}_{\delta}

(
[
6
, Proposition 3.11]
); a standard
covering argument; and
[
6
, Proposition 4.13]
,
whose analogue in our case has already been obtain
in Proposition
2.11
.

In our case, the proofs are largely similar to as in
[
6
]
,
but some adaptation is required, especially because
we need to take care of the further dependence on
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}

when dealing with Pohozaev inequalities (i.e., when testing
the stress-energy tensor
T_{jk}^{\varepsilon}
in (
1.20
)).

Before going on, we explicitly remark that, in our case,
the condition
[
6
, (4.33)]
, i.e., a uniform local bound
on
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
,
is globally satisfied in
\Omega
, thanks to (
1.9
),
and therefore we do not need to verify the further condition

[
6
, (4.24)]
(which, as a matter of fact, is used in

[
6
]
only to ensure a local uniform bound on
u_{\varepsilon}
).
As a consequence, Proposition
2.11

above provides the analogue of the three statements

[
6
, Proposition 4.11, Proposition 4.13, and Proposition 4.14]
,
without changes (if not in the dependence of the constants, as
specified in Proposition
2.11
).

Step 1

(Controlling the energy on external domains)
.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be a
given compact set and, as described in the statement, choose

\delta>0
so small that
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}\subset K
.
Fix
\chi_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, a smooth cut-off function so that

\chi_{\delta}(x)=1\quad\mbox{for }x\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2},\qquad\chi_{\delta}(x)=0\quad\mbox{for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta},\qquad\left|\nabla\chi_{\delta}\right|\leq\frac{4}{\delta}\quad\mbox{on }\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Note that the support of
\chi_{\delta}
is anyway
contained in
\Omega
and that

\mathcal{V}_{\delta}\supseteq\mathcal{U}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}
.
Going back to Proposition
1.6
and
testing the stress-energy tensor
T^{\varepsilon}_{jk}

in (
1.20
) against the
field
\mathbf{X}(x)=x\chi_{\delta}(x)
,
we obtain

(3.19)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\delta}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})(\div\mathbf{X})\,{\mathrm{d}}x=&\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\delta}}\varepsilon\left\{\partial_{j}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{j}X_{k}-\frac{1}{2}(\div\mathbf{X})\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&+\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\delta}}\left\{\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{j}X_{k}-\frac{1}{2}(\div\mathbf{X})\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right\}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\end{split}

We observe that, since
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
by item
5
of Theorem
A
,
we have
strong convergence
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
in

W^{1,2}(\mathcal{U}_{\delta})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Thus, by (
1.24
), we deduce that
the second line in (
3.19
) vanishes as

\varepsilon\to 0
. Consequently, recalling (
3.16
), (
3.17
),
by an easy computation we obtain the inequality

(3.20)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C(\mathcal{U},\,\delta)E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{V}_{\delta})+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1),

where
C(\mathcal{U},\delta)
is a constant depending on
\mathcal{U}
and
\delta
, but not on
\varepsilon
.
On the other hand, exploiting
Lemma
3.1
, (
3.20
) becomes

(3.21)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C(\mathcal{U},\,\delta)\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{V}_{\delta})+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\right),

which replaces
[
6
, (3.27)]
,
and where the constant
C(\mathcal{U},\delta)
depends on
\mathcal{U}
and
\delta
, but not on
\varepsilon
.

Step 2

(Local bounds and covering argument)
.

By a standard argument using Lebesgue’s covering lemma, we may cover

\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\delta/4}}

using only finitely many balls
B(x_{i},\,\delta/8)
,
with
x_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta/4}
,

i\in I
,
\sharp I<+\infty
.
Notice that we have also

\cup_{i\in I}B(x_{i},\,\delta/4)\subset\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}
.
Using Proposition
2.11

with
x_{0}=x_{i}
and
R=\delta/4
, for any
i\in I
, and
recalling
2.46
, we see that

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{i},\,\delta/8))\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{B(x_{i},\,3\delta/16)}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{i},\,\delta/4))+\varepsilon C_{\alpha}(K)\delta^{\alpha},

for any
\alpha\in(1,\,2)
, where the implicit constant
on the right-hand side depends only on
\beta
and on
\Omega
.
Summing all these contributions,
we get

(3.22)

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{U}_{\delta/4})\lesssim\sharp I\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{U}_{\delta/2})+\varepsilon C_{\alpha}(K)\delta^{\alpha}\right),

where the implicit constant on the right-hand side depends
only on
\alpha
,
\beta
,
K
,
\Omega
(while
\sharp I

depends on
\delta
, of course), and not on
\varepsilon
.

Step 3

(Conclusion)
.

The desired inequality (
3.18
) follows immediately
by combining (
3.21
) and (
3.22
).
∎

With Proposition
3.6
at hand, we
easily obtain the analogue of
[
6
, Theorem 1.16]
,
which is the key tool in the proof of part
4

of Theorem
3.3
.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

be an open set.
Assume that, for some
\delta>0
, there holds

\nu_{\star}(\mathcal{V}_{\delta})=0
. Then,

\nu_{\star}(\overline{\mathcal{U}})=0
.

Proof.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be a
compact set so that
\mathcal{U}\subset\!\subset K
.
Proceeding similarly to as in
[
6
, Section 7.3]
, since

\nu_{\star}(\mathcal{V}_{\delta})=0
, we have

E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathcal{V}_{\delta})\to 0\qquad\mbox{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

Hence, by Proposition
3.6
, we obtain

\nu_{\star}\left(\mathcal{U}_{\delta/8}\right)\lesssim\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathcal{V}_{\delta})+\varepsilon E_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\mathcal{U}_{\delta/4}\right)\right)=0,

which implies the claimed conclusion.
∎

We are now ready for completing the proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
, completion.

In order to complete the proof of Theorem
3.3
,
we need analogues of the results
in
[
6
, Section 7.4 and Section 7.5]
.
However, with Lemma
3.5
and
Theorem
3.7
at hand,
these results follow with no significant changes
by exactly the same arguments as in
[
6
]
.
Therefore, we just sketch the main points of the argument
below, addressing the reader to
[
6
]
for
full details.

Step 3

(
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
is closed, it has finite
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure,
and it coincides with
\spt(\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K)
)
.

The fact that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
is closed in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

(hence, in
\Omega
) is already contained
Lemma
3.5
, from which it also follows
immediately that
\spt(\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K)=\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
. The fact that

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
has finite
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure,
with the estimate

\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\right)\leq{\rm C}_{{\rm H},K}\mathcal{E}_{0},

where
\mathcal{E}_{0}
is the constant on the right-hand side
of (
2.7
) and

{\rm C}_{{\rm H},K}=50\,/\,\eta_{\star,K}

follows again from Lemma
3.5

exactly as in
[
6
, Proposition 7.3]
.
Being the argument based on a covering argument and a
trivial application of Lemma
3.5
,
we leave it to the reader to check that the details presented
in
[
6
]
carry over completely unchanged.

Step 4

(Connectedness properties of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
)
.

Given
r>0
and any
x_{0}\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

such that
B(x_{0},\,2r)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
we consider the compact set

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K,\rho}(x_{0}):=\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\cap\overline{B(x_{0},\,r)}\qquad\mbox{for }\rho\in[0,\,2r).

Then, arguing exactly as in
[
6
, Proposition 7.4]
,
we can prove that the compact set

\mathcal{C}_{\star,r}(x_{0}):=\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K,r}(x_{0})\cup\mathbb{S}^{1}(x_{0},\,r)

is a continuum, i.e., it is compact and connected.
Once that Theorem
3.7
is given,
the details are identical to as in
[
6
]
.
Since they are based only on an approximation argument in the Hausdorff
metric for the set
\mathcal{C}_{\star,r}(x_{0})
by continua
and general properties of continua under Hausdorff convergence,
we leave them to the reader (who, for the general theory of
continua, may consult, for instance,
[
13
]
).

Path-connectedness of
\mathcal{C}_{\star,r}(x_{0})
now
follows immediately, because any continuum with finite

\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure is path-connected
(see, e.g.,
[
13
, Lemma 3.12]
).

Gathering the pieces of information above and arguing as
in
[
6
, Section 7.4.1]
, we obtain the
analogue of
[
6
, Proposition 1.17]
, i.e.,
the fact that for any

x_{0}\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and for any
r>0
such that

B(x_{0},\,2r)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
there exists

\rho_{0}\in(r,\,2r)
such that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\cap B(x_{0},\,\rho_{0})

is a finite union of path-connected components.
(Being an argument in general topology based solely on the fact
that
\mathcal{C}_{\star,r}(x_{0})
is path-connected,
no changes are needed with respect to
[
