6
]
,
as the reader may easily check.)

Step 5

(Rectifiability)
.

Since rectifiability is a local property, it
suffices to show that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K,r}

is rectifiable. To this purpose, it is clearly
enough to prove that
\mathcal{C}_{\star,r}(x_{0})

is rectifiable, for
x_{0}
and
r>0
as in the
previous step. But this follows immediately,
because every continuum with finite
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure
is rectifiable (cf.
[
13
, Lemma 3.13]
).
∎

The set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

Taking advantage of the connectedness and rectifiability properties
of the sets
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
,
we show that the set
\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\nu_{\star}
is
locally path-connected, countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, with locally finite measure.
First of all, we introduced a couple of definitions that will be used throughout.

Definition 3.1
.

We define

(3.23)

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}:=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and

(3.24)

\lambda_{\star}:=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}.

Proposition 3.8
.

The set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
defined in (
3.23
)
satisfies

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}=\{x\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}\,:\,\mathfrak{e}_{\star}>0\}.

Moreover,
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

is countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, with locally finite

\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure and locally path-connected.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
equals the set of points with
positive lower density for
\nu_{\star}
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

is an obvious consequence of item
3
of
Theorem
3.3
. In addition, clearly,

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap K=\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}

for any
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, hence
the property
\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap K)<+\infty
and
the fact that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap K
is
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable
for any
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

are obvious consequences of item
4
of Theorem
3.3
.
Again in view of item
4
of Theorem
3.3
,
it also follows that
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is locally path-connected.
Finally,
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable
because we could write it as a countable union of sets
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K_{n}}

(which are rectifiable by item
4
of Theorem
3.3
),
where
\{K_{n}\}
is a sequence of compact sets

K_{n}\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
such that

K_{n}\subset K_{n+1}
and

\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}=\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

(such an exhaustion in nested compact set certainly exists, as

\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
is an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
— recall that

\spt\mu_{\star}
is a finite set of points. Then, the conclusion follows because countable rectifiability is a local property,
stable under countable unions
(and, clearly, the precise choice of the sequence
\{K_{n}\}
is irrelevant).
∎

Remark 3.3
.

As an obvious consequence of Proposition
3.8
,
the measure
\lambda_{\star}=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is locally
finite on
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, hence it is a
Radon measure on
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

Locally uniform convergence away from
\spt\nu_{\star}\cup\spt\mu_{\star}

We are finally ready to improve on the
L^{p}
-compactness for
the
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
-component (already given by Theorem
A
)
and obtain locally uniform convergence to the limiting map
\mathbf{M}_{\star}

away from
\spt\nu_{\star}\cup\spt\mu_{\star}
.
To this purpose, we prove first an auxiliary lemma which says that, thanks to
the clearing-out property of
\nu_{\star}
and to (
3.15
), and up to
redefinition on a set of measure zero, the
regularity of
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
(which was defined only almost everywhere)
can be improved.
We recall from
[
9
, Theorem 3.14]
that almost every point
x\in\Omega
,

(3.25)

\mathbf{M}_{\star}(x)=\tau(x)\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\star}(x),

where
\mathbf{n}_{\star}(x)
is a unit eigvector of
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}(x)
corresponding
to its positive eigenvalue.

Lemma 3.9
.

Up to redefinition on a set of (Lebesgue) measure zero,
the map
\mathbf{M}_{\star}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

defined in (
3.25
) is continuous on the open set

\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
and
given by

\mathbf{M}_{\star}=\tau\left(\sqrt{2}\beta+1\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\star},

where
\tau(x)\in\{-1,\,+1\}
and the function
x\mapsto\tau(x)

is constant in each connected component of

\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
.

Proof.

Let
x_{0}\in\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
. Then,
by Lemma
3.5
,
we can find a radius
r_{x_{0}}>0
so that

B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and

\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}}))=0
. Hence, we can find a
compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
with
non-empty interior
G:={\rm int}\,K
, such that

x_{0}\in G\setminus\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
. Thus,
from (
3.15
) and Lemma
3.5
,
we have

0\leq\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathrm{S}_{\mathbf{M}_{\star}}\cap B(x_{0},\,r)\right)}{r}\leq\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}=0.

Since, by Theorem
A
,

\mathbf{M}_{\star}\in\SBV(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
and it is bounded,
recalling

[
2
, Theorem 7.8]
, we see that

x_{0}\not\in\mathrm{S}_{\mathbf{M}_{\star}}
. By the continuity of
\mathbf{n}_{\star}
in
G
,
it follows that, up to
redefining
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
on a set of zero Lebesgue measure,
\tau
must constant
in
G
, so that
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
is continuous in
G
.
Consequently,
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
is continuous, and
\tau
is constant,
in the connected component of

\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
containing
x_{0}
.
The conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

In particular,
\overline{\mathrm{S}_{\mathbf{M}_{\star}}}\subset\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star}
.
Thus, in view of (
3.15
) and of (
3.6
), we have

\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\overline{\mathrm{S}_{\mathbf{M}_{\star}}}\cap K\right)<+\infty
for every compact set

K\subset\Omega
.

With Theorem
3.3
and
Lemma
3.9
at hand, we can
finally establish local uniform convergence of
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

towards
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
, in every connected component of

\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
\{(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\}
be a sequence of critical points of
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
,
subject to either boundary conditions as in (
3
)–(
4
)
or as in (
5
)–(
6
), and assume that (
7
)
holds. Set
U_{\star}:=\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
.
Then, up to extraction of a (not-relabelled) subsequence, for every connected
component
U_{j}
of
U_{\star}

we have that
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{M}_{\star}
locally uniformly on
U_{j}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

The proof is along the lines of that of

[
6
, item (ii) of Theorem 1.2]
, presented
in
[
6
, Section 7.6 and Section 7.7]
,
but it requires some modifications in order to deal with the moving
wells.

Step 1
.

We prove that
for any
x_{0}\in U_{\star}
and for the radius
r_{x_{0}}>0
given by
Lemma
3.5
, there exists a
(not relabelled) subsequence so that

(3.26)

\left\|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{M}_{\star}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4))}\to 0\qquad\mbox{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

Let
K
be any compact set such that

B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})\subset\!\subset K\subset U_{\star}
. (It will turn out
that the choice of
K
is not important.) Then,

\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})}\right)=0
,
so that

(3.27)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}}))=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\nu_{\varepsilon}(B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}}))\leq\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})}\right)=0

Hence, we can apply Theorem
2.12
,
so that (
2.50
) yields

(3.28)

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x),\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}(x)))\leq\delta_{\beta}\qquad\mbox{for any }x\in B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4),

for any
\varepsilon>0
small enough that, say,
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2

in
K
.
Recall that
\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon})=\{(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon},\,(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}\}

and that
(\mathbf{M}_{\pm})_{\varepsilon}:=\pm(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho_{\varepsilon}(x))^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\varepsilon}(x)
, so that

\left|(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon}-(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}\right|>2
independently of

x\in K
, for any
\varepsilon
small enough that
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|\geq 1/2

in
K
(see (
2.60
)).
Since
\delta_{\beta}\leq 1
,
this implies that, at
x_{0}
, (
3.28
) is
satisfied for
either

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})=(\mathbf{M}_{+})_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})

or

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})=(\mathbf{M}_{-})_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
(and not for both).
By (
3.28
), the uniform convergence
\rho_{\varepsilon}\to 1

given by (
25
), and the continuity of
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
,
it follows that

(3.29)

\|\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}(x)-\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})\|\leq\delta_{\beta}\qquad\mbox{for any }x\in B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4).

Thus, we see that, for any
\varepsilon>0
small enough,

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\tau_{\varepsilon}\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\rho_{\varepsilon}(x)\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\varepsilon}(x),\qquad\mbox{for all }x\in B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4)

where
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\tau_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})\in\{-1,\,+1\}
depends on
\varepsilon
but not on

x\in B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4)
.
Hence, again by (
25
), and after possible extraction of a subsequence,
it follows that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

(3.30)

\mathbf{N}_{\varepsilon}\to\tau\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\star}\qquad\mbox{uniformly in }B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4),

where
\tau\in\{-1,\,1\}
.
On the other hand, Theorem
2.12
and (
2.50
)
yield not only (
3.28
) but also

(3.31)

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}))\leq{\rm C}_{\rm well}\left(\frac{E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}}))}{r_{x_{0}}}\right)^{1/6}\qquad\mbox{in }B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4).

for any
\varepsilon>0
so small
that (
3.28
) holds. Therefore,
combining (
3.31
) and (
3.27
),
we obtain

(3.32)

\dist(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon},\,\Sigma(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}))=\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\qquad\mbox{in }B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4).

Thus, for the previously selected subsequence,
by (
3.32
), (
3.29
),
and (
3.30
),

\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\tau\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\star}\qquad\mbox{uniformly in }B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4).

Finally, from Lemma
3.9
we may assume that

\mathbf{M}_{\star}
is continuous on
B(x_{0},\,r_{x_{0}})
and so,
by the pointwise a.e. convergence

\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{M}_{\star}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
given by
Theorem
A
, it follows that

(3.33)

\mathbf{M}_{\star}(x)=\tau\left(1+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)^{1/2}\mathbf{n}_{\star}(x)\qquad\mbox{for all }x\in B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/4),

for the same
\tau
as in (
3.30
).
Combining (
3.33
) with (
3.32
),
we obtain (
3.26
).

Step 2
.

Let
K\subset U_{\star}
be compact and connected. Then, there
exists a (not relabelled) subsequence such that

(3.34)

\left\|\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{M}_{\star}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\to 0,\qquad\mbox{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

With (
3.26
) at hand, the proof
of (
3.34
) follows by a standard
covering argument involving the connectedness of
K
and the
continuity of
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
on each connected component of

\Omega\setminus(\spt\mu_{\star}\cup\spt\nu_{\star})
given
by Lemma
3.9
.

Step 3

(Conclusion)
.

Along the lines of
[
6
, Section 7.7]
,
we consider the sets

U_{n}:=\left\{x\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}:\dist\left(x,\,\spt\nu_{\star}\right)>\frac{1}{n}\right\},\qquad\mbox{for }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then,
\overline{U_{n}}
is a compact subset of
U_{\star}
.
Moreover, there hold

\overline{U_{n}}\subset U_{n+1}\quad\mbox{for any }n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}=U_{\star}.

Letting
U_{n}^{j}
, with
j\in J_{n}
, be the connected components of
U_{n}
and
arguing as in
[
6
, Section 7.7]
, we can first
prove that the set
J_{n}
is at most countable and, from here and
a diagonal argument involving Step
2
,
that
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{M}_{\star}
uniformly in
U_{n}^{j}
, for any
j\in J_{n}
.
In turn, letting now
U_{j}
be any connected component of
U_{\star}

and
K\subset U_{j}
be any compact set,
the conclusion follows as in
[
6
]
using
again Step
2
and the fact that
the sign function
\tau
in
\mathbf{M}_{\star}
takes only two values and so it must
be constant in
U_{j}\cap K
.
∎

Tangent cone property at regular points of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

By Proposition
3.8
, the set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

is
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable, with locally finite
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-measure. Thus, by
standard results in Geometric Measure Theory (see, for instance,

[
20
, Chapter 3, Definition 1.4, Remark 1.5(3), and Theorem 1.6]
), there exists a
set
\mathfrak{A}_{\star}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
,
with
\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathfrak{A}_{\star})=0
, so that, if

x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
,
then

(3.35)

\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B(x_{0},\,r)\right)}{2r}=1,

i.e.,

\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\lambda_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{2r}=1.

Moreover, there exists a unit vector
\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
(depending on
x_{0}
) with
the following
tangent line property
.
For any
\theta>0
, there holds

(3.36)

\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap\left(B(x_{0},\,r)\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\rm one}(x_{0},\,\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}},\,\theta)\right)\right)}{2r}=0,

where
\mathcal{C}_{\rm one}(x_{0},\,\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}},\,\theta)
is the cone
defined by

(3.37)

\mathcal{C}_{\rm one}(x_{0},\,\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}},\,\theta)=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\left|\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}^{\perp}\cdot(y-x_{0})\right|\leq\tan\theta\left|\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}\cdot(y-x_{0})\right|\right\}.

According to standard terminology in Geometric Measure Theory,
a point
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}

is called a
regular point
of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
and
\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}

is said to be
tangent to
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
at
x_{0}
,
see, e.g.,
[
13
, Section 2.1]
.

Remark 3.5
.

In other words, the tangent line property (
3.36
) allows
us to choose, at any
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
,
an orthonormal frame
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{e}_{2})
so that
\mathbf{e}_{1}
is the
unit vector tangent to
\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
at
x_{0}
,
i.e.,
\mathbf{e}_{1}=\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
.
As in
[
6
]
, this yields an
intrinsic
moving
frame associated with
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
whose importance will be
crucial in the analysis later on.

With the above results at hand, in particular with the connectedness
properties of the sets
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
, and arguing
as in the proof of
[
6
, Proposition 1.18]
,
we obtain a relevant strengthening of the
tangent line property at regular points of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
,
i.e., the
tangent cone property
below.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
be any regular point of

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. Given any
\theta>0
, there exists
a radius
R_{\rm cone}(\theta,\,x_{0})
such that

(3.38)

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B(x_{0},\,r)\subset\mathcal{C}_{\rm one}(x_{0},\,\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}},\,\theta),\qquad\mbox{for any }0<r\leq R_{\rm cone}(\theta,\,x_{0})

Proof.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
be any given regular point of

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. Since
x_{0}
must be contained in
some compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
we can find
r_{x_{0}}>0
such that

\dist(\{x_{0}\},\,\spt\mu_{\star})=2r_{x_{0}}>0
, and so

B(x_{0},\,2r_{x_{0}})\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
Let, for instance,
K_{x_{0}}:=\overline{B(x_{0},\,3r_{x_{0}}/2)}
.
Then,
K_{x_{0}}
is a compact set contained in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and
the proof presented in
[
6
, Section 7.8]
,
being based only on general arguments in Geometric Measure Theory
(in particular, on (
3.36
)) and on the connectedness
properties of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K_{x_{0}}}
, which are the same as
in
[
6
]
, carries over without modifications.
∎

A crucial consequence of Proposition
3.11

is Proposition
3.12
below. In order to state
Proposition
3.12
, we need to introduce some notation, analogous to that in
[
6
, Section 8.1]
.

Given
x_{0}=\left(x_{0,1},\,x_{0,2,}\right)\in\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and
\rho>0
such that
B(x_{0},\,2\rho)\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, we
denote
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})
the closed square defined by

(3.39)

Q_{\rho}(x_{0})=\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(x_{0,1})\times\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(x_{0,2}),

where

(3.40)

\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(s)=[s-\rho,\,s+\rho]\qquad\mbox{for }s\in(0,\,\rho).

Next, we let

\mathcal{R}_{\rho}(x_{0}):=\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(x_{0,1})\times\mathcal{I}_{\frac{3\rho}{4}}(x_{0,2}),

so that
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\rho}(x_{0})
is the union
of two disjoint rectangles. The following condition plays a pivotal
role in most arguments in
[
6
, Section 8]
:

(3.41)

\nu_{\star}\left(\overline{Q_{\rho}(x_{0})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{\rho}(x_{0})}\right)=0.

If (
3.41
) holds, then
\nu_{\star}
is concentrated,
locally near
x_{0}
, in a neighbourhood of the segment

(x_{0}-\rho\mathbf{e}_{1},\,x_{0}+\rho\mathbf{e}_{1})
.
In
[
6
, Proposition 8.4]
, it is proved
that (
3.41
) holds at any regular point
of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. This property holds also in our context
(by exactly the same argument, in fact).

Proposition 3.12
.

Assume that
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
.
Then, there exists
\rho_{0}>0
such that (
3.41
) is
satisfied for any
0<\rho\leq\rho_{0}
.

Proof.

The argument in
[
6
]
, which relies just
on choosing an orthonormal basis at
x_{0}
as in
Remark
3.5
and on the tangent cone property
in Proposition
3.11
, carries
over without modifications.
∎

3.2
The limiting potential energy
\zeta_{\star}

This short section will serve us to introduce a fundamental
object in the following course, the
limiting potential energy

\zeta_{\star}
.

To begin with, following
[
6
, (1.14)]
,
for any
\varepsilon>0
, we define the potential energy densities

(3.42)

\zeta_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon},

dually seen as measures in
\Omega
. In view of
the
bound (
2.7
), it follows immediately that

(3.43)

\zeta_{\varepsilon}(\Omega)\leq E_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon};\,\Omega)\leq\mathcal{E}_{0}

independently of
\varepsilon>0
,
so that we can find a (not-relabelled)
subsequence and a non-negative Radon measure
\zeta_{\star}

on
\Omega
such that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

(3.44)

\zeta_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\zeta_{\star}

weakly* as measures in
\Omega
.

In the scalar theory,
as direct consequences of the positivity of the discrepancy
function in the entire case
[
19
]
or, in the case
of bounded domains, of its asymptotic vanishing
[
16
]

and of the resulting monotonicity formula for
\nu_{\star}
,
one has both that
\zeta_{\star}

controls
\nu_{\star}
and that
\zeta_{\star}
is absolutely
continuous with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
. In fact, the
equipartition property
\nu_{\star}=2\zeta_{\star}
holds.
In the vectorial case, no monotonicity formula is known for

\nu_{\star}
. In addition, the discrepancy should be not
expected to have a sign (in the entire case, a counterexample to the positivity of
the discrepancy is presented in
[
21
]
).
For these reasons, the vectorial theory developed in
[
6
]

goes along a different path and it based on exploiting
the relationship between
\zeta_{\star}
and the
limiting Hopf differential

\omega_{\star}
,
to be introduced in the next section. This relationship can be proved to hold
also in our ‘perturbed’ setting thanks to the refined energy estimates that
we have already obtained at
\varepsilon
-level and the strong convergence of

\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
to a harmonic map
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
away from
\spt\mu_{\star}
, and it is
key to further developments.
In particular, in Section
3.5

we will prove the monotonicity
of the function

r\mapsto\frac{1}{r}\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))
for any
x_{0}\in\Omega
,
which immediately entails the absolute continuity of
\zeta_{\star}

with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
. In Section
3.6

we will instead recover the equivalence between

\zeta_{\star}
and
\nu_{\star}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.
Finally, in Section
4
, we will use Equation (
3.56
)
below to obtain refined structure properties for
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.

3.3
The limiting Hopf differential

In this section, we show that testing the Pohozaev
inequality in Lemma
1.5
against smooth vector field
with support in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
produces
a limiting measure
\omega_{\star}
that
will be called, adopting the terminology in
[
6
]
,
the
limiting Hopf differential
.
Proposition
3.13

below relates the limiting Hopf differential with the limiting
potential energy measure
\zeta_{\star}
and it is the analogue,
in our context, of
[
6
, Lemma 1.19]
. Crucially,
thanks to (
3.53
) below,
only the contribution
\omega_{\star}^{\rm M}

to the Hopf differential coming from the
\mathbf{M}
-component appears
in Equation (
3.56
).
Combined with a natural decomposition of
\omega_{\star}^{\rm M}
,
(
3.51
) below, Proposition
3.13

will be key to prove, in the forthcoming sections,
the aforementioned further properties of
\zeta_{\star}
and
its precise relationship with the limiting energy density
\nu_{\star}
.

For a start, we notice that,
in view of the global bound (
1.15
),
the functions
\varepsilon\partial_{j}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
,
for
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
, are bounded in
L^{1}(\Omega)
.
Therefore, as
\varepsilon\to 0
(after possible extraction of a subsequence),
we have

(3.45)

\varepsilon\partial_{j}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k},\qquad\mbox{for any }(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2},

weakly* in the sense of measures in
\Omega
, where

\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}
is a bounded signed Radon measure
on
\Omega
for any
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
.

On the other hand, for any compact set

K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
the maps
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
converge strongly in
W^{1,2}(K)
to
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}

(see item
5
of Theorem
A
) and hence
we have

(3.46)

\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\partial_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\cdot\partial_{k}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\qquad\mbox{for any }(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2},

strongly in
L^{1}(K)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Now, we define the following quantities:

(3.47)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Q}:=\left|\partial_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-2i\partial_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}

(3.48)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}:=\left|\partial_{1}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{2}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-2i\partial_{1}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{2}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}

(3.49)

\displaystyle\omega_{\varepsilon}:=\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Q}+\varepsilon\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}.

According to the terminology in
[
6
]
, we will call

\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Q}
,
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}
, and
\omega_{\varepsilon}

Hopf differentials (at the
\varepsilon
-level)
. (Although such terminology
is usually referred to the above objects multiplied by the complex differential

{\mathrm{d}}z
; see, e.g.,
[
15
, Chapter 4]
.)

Since the sequence
\{\varepsilon\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}\}
is bounded in
L^{1}(\Omega)
,
we can find a (not relabelled)
subsequence and a locally bounded Radon measure

\varepsilon\omega_{\star}^{\rm M}
such that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

(3.50)

\varepsilon\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}\rightharpoonup^{*}\omega_{\star}^{\rm M}

weakly* in the sense of measures in
\Omega
.
On the other hand, (
3.45
) implies

(3.51)

\omega_{\star}^{\rm M}=(\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,1}-\mathbbm{m}_{\star,2,2})-2i\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,2}

as measures in
\Omega
.

Next, denoting

\omega_{\star}^{\rm Q}:=\left|\partial_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}-2i\partial_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\cdot\partial_{2}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}

the Hopf differential of
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
, we see that (
3.46
) implies

(3.52)

\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Q}\to\omega_{\star}^{\rm Q}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
both weakly* in the sense of measures and pointwise a.e.
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
On the other hand, by a classical result in harmonic maps
(see, e.g.,
[
15
, Chapter 3, p. 28]
), the
Hopf differential of any harmonic map from an open set in

\mathbb{R}^{2}\simeq\mathbb{C}
is a holomorphic function, and therefore we have

(3.53)

\frac{\partial\omega_{\star}^{\rm Q}}{\partial{\bar{z}}}\equiv 0

pointwise in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

From the above, it follows that
\{\omega_{\varepsilon}\}
is a
bounded sequence in
L^{1}(K)
for any
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
so that there exist a (not relabelled) subsequence and a locally bounded Radon measure

\omega_{\star}
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
such that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

(3.54)

\omega_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup^{*}\omega_{\star}

weakly* as measures in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

We are now ready to prove the main result of this section, relating

\omega_{\star}
,
\omega^{\rm M}_{\star}
, and
\zeta_{\star}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
\omega_{\star}
,
\omega^{\rm M}_{\star}
, and
\zeta_{\star}
be the
bounded Radon measures given by (
3.54
), (
3.50
)
and (
3.44
), respectively.
Then, for any
X\in C^{1}_{c}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star},\,\mathbb{C})
,
we have

(3.55)

\left\langle\omega_{\star},\frac{\partial X}{\partial\bar{z}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\omega_{\star}^{\rm M},\frac{\partial X}{\partial\bar{z}}\right\rangle

and

(3.56)

\left\langle\omega_{\star}^{\rm M},\frac{\partial X}{\partial\bar{z}}\right\rangle=\left\langle 2\zeta_{\star},\frac{\partial X}{\partial z}\right\rangle.

Proof.

The equality (
3.55
)
is an immediate consequence of (
3.53
),
so we focus on (
3.56
).

Step 1
.

We prove that

(3.57)

\Re\left(\left\langle\omega_{\star}^{\rm M},\frac{\partial X}{\partial\bar{z}}\right\rangle\right)=\left\langle 2\zeta_{\star},\Re\left(\frac{\partial X}{\partial z}\right)\right\rangle.

Reasoning as in
[
6
, (3.20)]
,
by the Pohozaev identity (
1.22
) we obtain

(3.58)

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\Re\left(\omega_{\varepsilon}\frac{\partial X}{\partial{\bar{z}}}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x&=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\div X\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\Omega}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\Re\left(\frac{\partial X}{\partial z}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\end{split}

for any
X\in C^{1}_{c}(\Omega;\,\mathbb{C})
and for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Take a compact set

K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and assume that

\spt X\subseteq K
. Recalling Lemma
3.1
and that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot\,,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}\zeta_{\star}

weakly* as measures in
K
, we obtain

(3.59)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}\zeta_{\star}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
and weakly* as measures in
K
. Thus,
using (
3.54
), (
3.55
), and (
3.59
)
to pass to the limit in (
3.58
),
we get (
3.57
).

Step 2

(Proof of (
3.56
))
.

Using
iX
as a test function in (
3.57
),
we obtain

(3.60)

\Im\left(\left\langle\omega_{\star}^{\rm M},\frac{\partial X}{\partial\bar{z}}\right\rangle\right)=\left\langle 2\zeta_{\star},\Im\left(\frac{\partial X}{\partial z}\right)\right\rangle.

Taking the sum of (
3.57
) with (
3.60
),
the conclusion follows.
∎

As an immediate consequence of (
3.56
),
we have that
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,2}
satisfies

modified Cauchy-Riemann relations
(
3.61
) below
(cf.
[
6
, (1.72)]
).

Corollary 3.14
.

The equations

(3.61)

\begin{cases}&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}(2\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,2})=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}(2\zeta_{\star}-\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,1}+\mathbbm{m}_{\star,2,2})\\
&\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}(2\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,2})=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}(2\zeta_{\star}+\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,1}-\mathbbm{m}_{\star,2,2})\end{cases}

hold in the sense of distributions in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

Proof.

Equations (
3.61
) follow immediately from (
3.56
)
and the definition of Wirtinger’s operators

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}-i\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\right),\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\right).

∎

Remark 3.6

(Changing coordinates in the limiting Hopf differential)
.

The expressions (
3.47
), (
3.48
), and (
3.49
)
for
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Q}
,
\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}
, and
\omega_{\varepsilon}
, respectively,
depend on the chosen orthonormal basis. However, it is well-known that, under conformal maps, they transform simply by multiplication by the conformal
factor (in fact, the same quantities multiplied by the complex differential
{\mathrm{d}}z
are invariant under conformal
maps, see e.g.
[
15
, Chapter 4]
).
Consequently, for any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\mu_{\star}
, the
boundedness of the sequences
\{\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm Q}\}
and

\{\varepsilon\omega_{\varepsilon}^{\rm M}\}
, and hence of
\{\omega_{\varepsilon}\}
, is a property invariant
under conformal maps. In turn, the same is true for the existence of their limits;
moreover, the explicit expression for the limiting maps change only by
multiplication for the conformal factor. In particular, this is true for
coordinate changes (from any orthonormal moving frame to any other).
Detailed computations, accounting for all cases that will be used
thereafter, are provided in
[
6
, Section 8.9.3]
.

3.4
Properties near good points of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

With Proposition
3.13
at hand, not only we can replicate the
arguments in
[
6
, Section 8]
, but actually
we could also
apply them directly
.
However, the proof of the monotonicity
of the (rescaled) limiting potential energy in Proposition
3.18
in the next section requires some adaptation with respect to
[
6
, Section 9]
. The reason is that, in order to prove that
\zeta_{\star}
does not charge

\spt\mu_{\star}
(i.e., item
1
of Proposition
3.18
), which is a key ingredient in the proof of the balance law (
22
) in Theorem
4.2
,
we really have to go through the argument of
[
6
, Proposition 1.23]
, because we need it to be developed on

annuli
rather than on full balls (as it is instead done in
[
6
]
). However, while stressing that using annuli rather balls is absolutely necessary to our purposes, we also emphasise that only notational modifications are needed with respect to
[
6
, Section 9]
, since a careful reading shows that the argument presented there is
already valid on annuli
, despite being written in terms of full balls.
Nonetheless, this circumstance forces us to introduce some notation and to retrace in this section and in the next ones the main steps of
Bethuel’s arguments. Such arguments are made-up by a remarkable
(and quite long) combination of elementary pieces that rely, essentially,
on testing the equation (
3.56
) against suitable test vector fields and
on identifying a class of
good points

(see Definition
3.2
below) having full
\lambda_{\star}
-measure
at which remarkable relationships between the various measures
at stake exist. The main result of this section is
Proposition
3.15
, which extends

[
6
, Proposition 1.20]
to our situation.

Decomposition of the relevant measures with respect to

\lambda_{\star}

Similarly to as in
[
6
]
, the first
step in the analysis consists in decomposing
\nu_{\star}
,
\zeta_{\star}
,
and each of the measures
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}
into their
absolutely continuous and singular part
with respect to the measure
\lambda_{\star}=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
:

(3.62)

\displaystyle\nu_{\star}=\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac}+\nu_{\star}^{\rm sing},

\displaystyle\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac}\perp\nu_{\star}^{\rm sing},

(3.63)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\star}=\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac}+\zeta_{\star}^{\rm sing},

\displaystyle\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac}\perp\zeta_{\star}^{\rm sing},

(3.64)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}=\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm ac}+\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm sing},

\displaystyle\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm ac}\perp\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm sing},

for any
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
. We can write

(3.65)

\displaystyle\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac}=\mathfrak{e}_{\star}\lambda_{\star},

(3.66)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac}=\mathfrak{v}_{\star}\lambda_{\star},

(3.67)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm ac}=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}\lambda_{\star},\qquad\mbox{for }(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2},

so that
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}
,
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
, and
\mathfrak{m}_{\star}

denote the densities of
\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac}
,
\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac}
, and

\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm ac}
with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
.

Remark 3.7
.

For later purposes, we observe that the values of the
densities
\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}
strongly depend on the
choice of the (Cartesian) orthonormal frame in their
definition (
3.45
). In the sequel, it will be convenient
to have more intrinsic objects at disposal and therefore,
following the discussion in
[
6
, Section 1.3]
,
we define the ‘intrinsic versions’ of the functions
\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}
,
i.e., their expressions in the moving frame associated
with
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
introduced in Remark
3.5

above. To this purpose, given any

x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
and choosing
the orthonormal frame
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{e}_{2})
so that
\mathbf{e}_{1}=\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
, we define

(3.68)

\mathfrak{m}_{\star,\perp,\perp}(x_{0})=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,2,2}(x_{0}),\quad\mathfrak{m}_{\star,||,||}(x_{0})=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,1}(x_{0}),\quad\mathfrak{m}_{\star,\perp,||}(x_{0})=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,2}(x_{0}).

Then, letting
x_{0}
vary in
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}

gives rise to the ‘intrinsic’ measures

(3.69)

\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\perp,\perp}=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,\perp,\perp}\lambda_{\star},\quad\mathbbm{m}_{\star,||,||}=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,||,||}\lambda_{\star},\quad\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\perp,||}=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,\perp,||}\lambda_{\star},

whose importance will be clear later on, in light of
Theorem
3.20

and Theorem
4.1
below.

The crux of the following sections will
be proving that the singular parts of the measures above are actually zero,
while the absolutely continuous parts enjoy suitable bounds. To this purpose,
the first step, carried out below, is ruling out certain bad,
\lambda_{\star}
-null
sets from
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. Then, the decisive step, performed in
Section
3.5
, will be showing the monotonicity of the rescaled measure

\zeta_{\star}
,
at any
x_{0}\in\Omega
.

Exceptional points and good points

In Section
3.1
, we defined the
\lambda_{\star}
-null
set
\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
of points at
which
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

does not have density
1
. Now, following
[
6
]
,
we define two other classes of exceptional points. Precisely,
in view of (
3.62
), we may find a subset
\mathfrak{B}_{\star}

of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
so that

(3.70)

\lambda_{\star}(\mathfrak{B}_{\star})=0

and

(3.71)

\nu_{\star}^{\rm sing}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{\star})=0,\qquad\zeta_{\star}^{\rm sing}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{\star})=0,\qquad\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm sing}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{B}_{\star})=0,

for any
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
.

In addition to
\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\star}
,
we define the set
\mathfrak{C}_{\star}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

as the complementary set of Lebesgue’s points for the densities of the measures

\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac}
,
\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac}
, and
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}^{\rm ac}

for
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
with respect to the measure
\lambda_{\star}
.
By Besicovitch’s differentiation theorem and the decomposition (
3.62
), there holds

(3.72)

\lambda_{\star}(\mathfrak{C}_{\star})=0.

Finally, like in
[
6
, (1.76)]
, we define the set
of
exceptional points

(3.73)

\mathfrak{E}_{\star}:=\mathfrak{A}_{\star}\cup\mathfrak{B}_{\star}\cup\mathfrak{C}_{\star}.

Since each of the sets on the right-hand side of (
3.73
) is null for
the measure
\lambda_{\star}
, we have

(3.74)

\lambda_{\star}(\mathfrak{E}_{\star})=0.

Definition 3.2
.

The points
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}

will be called the
good points
of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.

Remark 3.8
.

In particular, for any good point
x_{0}
, the density

\mathrm{D}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star})(x_{0})

of
\nu_{\star}
with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
at
x_{0}
exists and satisfies

(3.75)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star})(x_{0})=\mathrm{D}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac})(x_{0})=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{2r}<+\infty,

(3.76)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star}^{\rm sing})(x_{0})=0.

Eliminating derivatives along the transversal direction to the moving frame

Let
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}
and choose
an orthonormal basis at
x_{0}
as described in Remark
3.5
.
Let
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})
be a (closed) square as in (
3.39
). Along the lines of

[
6
, Section 1.6]
, we define the
localised measures

(3.77)

\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,j,k}=\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\rho}(x_{0})}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}\quad\mbox{for }(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2},\qquad\widetilde{\zeta}_{\star}=\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\rho}(x_{0})}\zeta_{\star},

where
\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\rho}(x_{0})}
denotes the characteristic function of the
square
Q_{\rho}(x_{0})
.
Denoting
\mathbb{P}
the orthogonal projection onto the tangent line

\gamma_{x_{0}}:=\{x_{0}+s\mathbf{e}_{1}\,:\,s\in\mathbb{R}\}
, we let

\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,j,k}^{1}:=\mathbb{P}_{\sharp}(\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,j,k}),\qquad\widetilde{\zeta}_{\star}^{1}:=\mathbb{P}_{\sharp}(\widetilde{\zeta}_{\star})

be the push-forward of the localised measures (
3.77
)
with respect to
\mathbb{P}
. As in
[
6
, (1.80)]
, we
introduce the measures on the interval
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(x_{0,1})

(defined as in (
3.40
))

(3.78)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{J}_{x_{0},\rho}=\mathcal{J}_{\rho}=\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,1,2}^{1},\\
\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},\rho}=\mathcal{L}_{\rho}=2\widetilde{\zeta}_{\star}^{1}-\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,1,1}^{1}+\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,2,2}^{1},\\
\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},\rho}=\mathcal{N}_{\rho}=2\widetilde{\zeta}_{\star}^{1}+\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,1,1}^{1}-\widetilde{\mathbbm{m}}_{\star,2,2}^{1}.\\
\end{cases}

The properties of the one-dimensional measures (
3.78
) are given by

[
6
, Proposition 8.2, Proposition 8.4, Proposition 8.6, Proposition 8.7, Proposition 8.11, and Proposition 8.12]
. Although the proofs of

[
6
, Proposition 8.2, Proposition 8.4, and Proposition 8.6]

involve several computations and occupy a few pages, they
are based only on the validity of the condition (
3.41
) and on testing the Equation (
3.56
) against suitable fields, of the form

X_{f}(x_{1},\,x_{2})=f_{1}(x_{1})f_{2}(x_{2})\mathbf{e}_{i}

for either
i=1
or
i=2
and appropriate choices of the
functions
f_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(x_{0,j}))
,
for
j\in\{1,\,2\}
. (Specifically, we address the reader to

[
6
, (8.18), (8.24), (8.28), (8.31)]
.)
Therefore, these propositions carry over to our case
without modifications and thus, for the sake
of brevity, we avoid repeating the arguments; the reader is referred to

[
6
, Sections from 8.1 to 8.7]

for fully detailed proofs.

About
[
6
, Proposition 8.7]
, the first part of
its statement claims the validity of (
3.41
) at
points of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{A}_{\star}

and corresponds precisely to Proposition
3.12
.
Combined with
[
6
, Proposition 8.2]
, it
yields immediately that the measures
\mathcal{J}_{\rho}
,

\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
are proportional to the Lebesgue measure
on
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}(x_{0,1})
and satisfy the differential relations

[
6
, (8.15), (8.16)]
(which are part of
the statement of
[
6
, Proposition 8.2]
and are, once again,
direct consequences of (
3.56
)).

Assume now the stricter condition

x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
, so
that
x_{0}
is a Lebesgue point for the absolutely continuous part (with
respect to
\lambda_{\star}
) of the measures
\nu_{\star}
,

\zeta_{\star}
, and
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}
, for
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
.
This means that the densities
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}
,
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
,
\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}
in (
3.65
), (
3.66
), (
3.67
) satisfy

\begin{split}&\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{r}\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B(x_{0},r)}\left|\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(\tau)-\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\tau=0,\\
&\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{r}\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B(x_{0},r)}\left|\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(\tau)-\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\tau=0,\\
&\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{r}\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B(x_{0},r)}\left|\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}(\tau)-\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}(x_{0})\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}\tau=0\quad\mbox{for }(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2},\end{split}

whence the existence of some
K(x_{0})>0
so that

(3.79)

\nu_{\star}^{\rm ac}(B(x_{0},\,r))\leq K(x_{0})r,\qquad\mbox{for any }0<r\leq\rho_{0}.

Arguing word-for-word as in
[
6
, Proposition 8.11]
,
one obtains that every point
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}

is a Lebesgue point for the densities
J_{\rho_{0}}
,
L_{\rho_{0}}
, and
N_{\rho_{0}}

associated with
\mathcal{J}_{\rho}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{\rho}
,
respectively, and that the identities

\displaystyle J_{\rho_{0}}(x_{0,1})=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,2}(x_{0}),

\displaystyle L_{\rho_{0}}(x_{0,1})=2\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})-\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,1}(x_{0})+\mathfrak{m}_{\star,2,2}(x_{0}),

\displaystyle N_{\rho_{0}}(x_{0,1})=2\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})+\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,1}(x_{0})-\mathfrak{m}_{\star,2,2}(x_{0}),

hold. Moreover, by
[
6
, Proposition 8.12]
(which is essentially
a direct consequence of (
3.79
) and of the tangent cone property), we have

\displaystyle J_{x_{0},\rho_{0}}(s)=0\qquad\mbox{for }s\in(x_{0,1}-\rho_{0},\,x_{0,1}+\rho_{0}),

\displaystyle N_{x_{0},\rho_{0}}(x_{0,1})=0.

Gathering these identities leads to the following analogue of

[
6
, Proposition 1.20]
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
be arbitrary
and let
\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
be the tangent vector to
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
at
x_{0}
.
Assume that the orthonormal frame
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{e}_{2})
is chosen so that

\mathbf{e}_{1}=\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
. Then, we have the identities

(3.80)

\begin{cases}2\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,2,2}(x_{0})-\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,1}(x_{0}),\\
\mathfrak{m}_{\star,1,2}(x_{0})=0.\end{cases}

Remark 3.9
.

Using the notation in (
3.68
), (
3.69
),
we could rephrase (
3.80
) in the ‘intrinsic’ form

\begin{cases}2\mathfrak{v}_{\star}=\mathfrak{m}_{\star,\perp,\perp}-\mathfrak{m}_{\star,||,||},\\
\mathfrak{m}_{\star,\perp,||}=0,\end{cases}

which is an identity holding on the whole set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
of good points in
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
.

From the discussion at the end of Section
3.3
, we immediately draw
a counterpart of
[
6
, Lemma 1.21]
.

Lemma 3.16
.

For any
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
,
we have the identity

(3.81)

\omega_{\star}^{\rm ac}(x)=-2e^{-2i\gamma_{\star}(x)}\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac}(x),

where
\gamma_{\star}(x)\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{2},\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
denotes
the angle between
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and the tangent vector
\mathbf{e}_{x}
to
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

at
x
.

3.5
Monotonicity properties of the limiting potential
\zeta_{\star}

In this section, we show that, for any
x_{0}\in\Omega

and any
annulus centred at
x_{0}
and properly contained in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
the function
r\mapsto\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r)}{r}
is monotone non-decreasing
for
0<r\leq R
, where
R
denotes the exterior radius of the
annulus. As a consequence, we obtain the existence of the density
of
\zeta_{\star}
at
x_{0}
and, hence, the absolute continuity of
\zeta_{\star}

with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
.

The first step towards the desired monotonicity property is
the following result, which is analogous to
[
6
, Lemma 1.24]
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
x_{0}\in\Omega
be any point. Then, for every
R>0
and every radii

r_{1}
,
r_{2}
with
0<r_{1}<r_{2}\leq R
and such that

B(x_{0},\,r_{2})\setminus B(x_{0},\,r_{1})\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
we have the identity

(3.82)

\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r_{2}))}{r_{2}}-\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r_{1}))}{r_{1}}=\int_{B(x_{0},\,r_{2})\setminus B(x_{0},\,r_{1})}\frac{1}{4r}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{N}_{x_{0},\star},

where

(3.83)

\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}=(\mbox{weak*-})\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}V(\,\cdot,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\varepsilon\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right).

Proof.

Let us set, for brevity,
B_{r}:=B(x_{0},\,r)
, for any
r\in(0,\,R)
.

By (
1.23
), the pointwise identity (
2.4
),
and in view of Lemma
3.1
, we can write

(3.84)

\begin{split}r^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon r}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\right)&=\frac{r}{2\varepsilon}\int_{\partial B_{r}}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(x))\\
&+\frac{r}{4}\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left(\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|+\varepsilon\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\varepsilon\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)\\
&+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\end{split}

In virtue of the strong convergence
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
in

W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star})
and recalling that,
being harmonic,
\mathbf{Q}_{\star}
satisfies

\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}=\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left|\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}

for any
r\in(0,\,R)
, passing to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0

in (
3.84
), it follows that, for a.e.
r\in(0,\,R)
,

r^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}\left(\zeta_{\star}(B_{r})\right)=\frac{r}{4}\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}(\partial B_{r}),

where
\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}
is as in (
3.83
).
The conclusion follows straightforwardly.
∎

With Lemma
3.17
at hand,
the monotonicity of
r\mapsto\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}
is proved once we show
that
\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}
is a non-negative measure, for any

x_{0}\in\Omega
.
Although we could also conclude immediately by invoking

directly
the results in
[
6
, Section 9]
,
to facilitate the reader we go through the main points of the argument.

First of all, it is convenient to switch to polar coordinates
(r,\,\theta)
centred
at
x_{0}
. Upon defining

\begin{cases}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,r,r}:=\cos^{2}\theta\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,1}+\sin^{2}\theta\mathbbm{m}_{\star,2,2}+2\sin\theta\cos\theta\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,2},\\
\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\theta,\theta}:=\sin^{2}\theta\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,1}+\cos^{2}\theta\mathbbm{m}_{\star,2,2}-2\sin\theta\cos\theta\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,2},\end{cases}

we have, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\varepsilon\left|\partial_{r}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,r,r},\qquad\varepsilon\left|\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\theta,\theta},

as measures in
\Omega
. Using this notation, we can rewrite (
3.83
)
in the form

(3.85)

\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}=2\zeta_{\star}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\theta,\theta}+\mathbbm{m}_{\star,r,r}.

Decomposing also the densities
\mathfrak{m}_{\star,j,k}
,
for
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
,
in the polar frame
(r,\,\theta)
and using
(
3.85
) and Lemma
3.16
as
in the proof of
[
6
, Lemma 9.1]
, we end up with

(3.86)

\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}^{\rm ac}=4\sin^{2}(\gamma_{\star}-\theta)\zeta_{\star}^{\rm ac},

where
\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}^{\rm ac}
denotes the absolutely continuous part of
\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}

with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
.

Next, let
\rho>\delta>0
be given. Following
[
6
, Section 9.3]
,
we introduce the auxiliary functions

\displaystyle Z(r):=\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r)),

\displaystyle F(r):=\frac{Z(r)}{r},

\displaystyle G_{\delta}(r):=\int_{B(x_{0},\,r)\setminus B(x_{0},\,\delta)}\frac{1}{\left|x-x_{0}\right|}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}},

where
r\in[\delta,\,\rho]
.
Letting

\Pi:B(x_{0},\rho)\setminus\{x_{0}\}\to(0,\rho)\quad\colon\quad x\mapsto\Pi(x):=\sqrt{\left(x-x_{0,1}\right)^{2}+\left(x-x_{0,2}\right)^{2}},

we also introduce the auxiliary measures

\check{\zeta}_{\star}:=\Pi_{\sharp}(\zeta_{\star}),\qquad\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}:=\Pi_{\sharp}(\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}),

so that, for any Borel sets
A\subset[\delta,\,\rho]
, there we have

\check{\zeta}_{\star}(A)=\check{\zeta}_{\star}(\Pi^{-1}(A)),\qquad\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}(A)=\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}(\Pi^{-1}(A)).

Exactly as in
[
6
, Lemma 9.3]
, as a direct
consequence of the definitions and Fubini’s theorem, it follows
that
Z
and
G_{\delta}
have bounded variation and that

(3.87)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}Z=\check{\zeta}_{\star}\geq 0,\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}G_{\delta}=\frac{1}{r}\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}},\qquad\mbox{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((\delta,\,\rho)).

Using these facts, one proves that
F
has bounded variation
and that

(3.88)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}F=\frac{1}{r}\check{\zeta}_{\star}-\frac{1}{r^{2}}Z=\frac{1}{4r}\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}},\qquad\mbox{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((\delta,\,\rho)).

cf.
[
6
, Lemma 9.4]
. Then, arguing as
in
[
6
, Section 9.4]
,
we take advantage of (
3.86
) and of the two different forms for

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}F
to show that the latter is a non-negative measure.
More precisely, once defined the annulus

A_{\delta,\rho}:=B(x_{0},\,\rho)\setminus B(x_{0},\,\delta),

we consider the set

\mathbb{A}_{\delta,\rho}=\Pi^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{E}\cap A_{\delta,\rho}\right).

Then,
\mathscr{H}^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{\delta,\rho})=0
and, since
Z
is a bounded function,

(3.89)

\frac{Z}{r}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}{\mathbb{A}_{\delta,\rho}}=0.

Furthermore, since

\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}=\mathscr{N}_{\star,x_{0}}^{\rm ac}
on
B(x_{0},\,\rho)\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
,
we have

(3.90)

\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}((0,\,\rho)\setminus\mathbb{A}_{\delta,\rho})\geq 0,

by (
3.86
). On the other hand, by (
3.88
),

(3.91)

\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}=4\left(\check{\zeta}_{\star}-\frac{Z}{r}\right)\qquad\mbox{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}((\delta,\,\rho)).

Since both sides of (
3.91
) are (not only distributions
but also) bounded measures,
the identity (
3.91
) is an identity of measures.
On the other hand, in view of (
3.89
), from (
3.91
) it follows that

(3.92)

\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathbb{A}_{\delta,\rho}=\check{\zeta}_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathbb{A}_{\delta,\rho}\geq 0.

Gathering (
3.90
) and (
3.92
),
we obtain

\check{\mathscr{N}}_{\star,x_{0}}\geq 0\qquad\mbox{on }(0,\,\rho)

and, by (
3.88
),
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}F\geq 0
on
(\delta,\,\rho)

for any
\delta>0
, hence
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}F\geq 0
on
(0,\,\rho)
.
Thus, recalling the definition of
F
, we see that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}\left(\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}\right)\geq 0\qquad\mbox{on }(0,\,\rho),

and we have therefore obtained the same conclusion
of
[
6
, Proposition 1.23]
, summarised below.

Proposition 3.18
.

The measure
\zeta_{\star}
has the following properties.

1.

Let
x_{0}\in\Omega
be any point. Then, for every
R>0

and for almost every radii
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
with
0<r_{1}<r_{2}\leq R

and such that
B(x_{0},\,r_{2})\setminus B(x_{0},\,r_{1})\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, we have the inequality

(3.93)

\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r_{2}))}{r_{2}}\geq\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r_{1}))}{r_{1}}.

2.

The measure
\zeta_{\star}
does not charge
the support of
\mu_{\star}
, that is,

(3.94)

\zeta_{\star}(\spt\mu_{\star})=0.

3.

For every
x_{0}\in\Omega
, the
function
r\mapsto\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}
has a limit when

r\to 0
, and we have, in fact,

(3.95)

\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{2r}\leq\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,R))}{2R},

for every
R>0
such that

\left(B(x_{0},\,2R)\setminus\{x_{0}\}\right)\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

4.

There holds

(3.96)

\zeta_{\star}\leq\nu_{\star}

in the sense of measures in
\Omega
.

5.

The measure
\zeta_{\star}
is absolutely continuous with respect
to
\lambda_{\star}=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

in
\Omega
. In fact,

\zeta_{\star}=\mathfrak{v}_{\star}\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

in
\Omega
.

Remark 3.10
.

We stress that
x_{0}
can be
any point in
\Omega
; in particular,
it may very well be a point of the support of
\mu_{\star}
.

Proof.

Inequality (
3.93
) has been already obtained above.
Item
2
follows immediately from
inequality (
3.93
).
Item
3
is an obvious consequence of (
3.93
).
About item
4
,
the inequality

\zeta_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star})\leq\nu_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star})

follows already from the definitions of
\zeta_{\star}

and
\nu_{\star}
and item
1
of
Theorem
3.3
. On the other hand,
by (
3.94
) we have also

\zeta_{\star}(\spt\mu_{\star})=0
,
and the conclusion follows.
Finally, item
5
follows straightforwardly
by combining
item
3
, item
4
,
and item
2
.
∎

3.6
Upper bounds, absolute continuity of
\nu_{\star}
with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
, and consequences

By item
4
of
Proposition
3.18
, we know that the
inequality
\zeta_{\star}\leq\nu_{\star}
holds in the sense
of measure in
\Omega
.
We now show
that also a converse inequality holds locally on
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
so that
\nu_{\star}
is equivalent to
\zeta_{\star}
in

\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and, in turn, absolutely continuous with respect to
\lambda_{\star}

in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

Proposition 3.19
.

The measure
\nu_{\star}
is equivalent to
\zeta_{\star}
and
absolutely continuous with respect to

\lambda_{\star}
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
Moreover, we have
\nu_{\star}=\mathfrak{e}_{\star}\lambda_{\star}
,
with

(3.97)

\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})\leq 2{\rm K}_{\beta}\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})\quad\mbox{ for $\lambda_{\star}$-almost every }x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star},

where
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}:\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
denotes the
density of
\nu_{\star}
and the constant
{\rm K}_{\beta}
depends only on
\beta

and on
\Omega
.

Proof.

The proof combines
[
6
, Lemma 9.7]

with
[
6
, Corollary 9.8]
and
Proposition
3.18
.
To keep it more transparent, we divide it into two steps.

Step 1

(
\nu_{\star}\sim\zeta_{\star}
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
)
.

From Proposition
2.11
(in
particular, from (
2.44
)) and the logarithmic
bound on the energy
\mathscr{F}_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.13
), we derive
that, for any ball
B(x_{0},\,R)\subset\!\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
there holds

\nu_{\varepsilon}\left(B(x_{0},\,R/2)\right)\leq{\rm K}_{\beta}\,\zeta_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,3R/4)}\right)+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1),

where
{\rm K}_{\beta}
is the constant in the right-hand side
of (
2.44
), which depends only
on
\beta
and
\Omega
. Thus, passing to the limit
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get

(3.98)

\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,R/2))\leq{\rm K}_{\beta}\,\zeta_{\star}\left(\overline{B(x_{0},\,3R/4)}\right),

which is the same conclusion as in
[
6
, Lemma 9.7]
.
As a consequence of (
3.98
), if we fix any
compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
and we take any
Borel set
E\subset K
which is
null for the measure
\zeta_{\star}
, by covering
E
with balls
B(x_{j},\,R/2)

such that
B(x_{j},\,4R/5)\subset K

and then using the outer regularity of
\zeta_{\star}
, it follows that
E
is null
also for the measure
\nu_{\star}
,
hence
\nu_{\star}\ll\zeta_{\star}
in
K
. In view of (
3.96
),
it follows that
\nu_{\star}
and
\zeta_{\star}
have the same null sets
in
K
, for any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.
By exhausting
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
by a countable family of
nested compact sets, the same holds true
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, and therefore
\nu_{\star}\sim\zeta_{\star}

in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

Step 2

(Proof of (
3.97
) and conclusion)
.

From (
3.75
), (
3.76
), (
3.98
) and (
3.95
), as in
[
6
, Corollary 9.8]
,
denoting
\overline{\mathrm{D}}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star})(x_{0})
the upper density
of
\nu_{\star}
with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
at
x_{0}
,
it follows that, for every

x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
,

(3.99)

\begin{split}\overline{\mathrm{D}}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star})(x_{0})&=\limsup_{r\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{2r}=\limsup_{r\to 0}\frac{\nu_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r/2))}{r}\\
&\leq{\rm K}_{\beta}\,\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\zeta_{\star}(B(x_{0},\,r))}{r}=2{\rm K}_{\beta}\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0}).\end{split}

This implies that
\nu_{\star}
is absolutely continuous with respect
to
\lambda_{\star}
.
Moreover,
since
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
,
and, therefore,
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}(x_{0})={\overline{\mathrm{D}}}_{\lambda_{\star}}(\nu_{\star})(x_{0})
,
(
3.97
) follows as well. The proof is complete.

∎

Remark 3.11
.

A straightforward consequence of the absolute continuity of

\nu_{\star}
,
\zeta_{\star}
with respect to
\lambda_{\star}

on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is the fact that such measures do not
have singular part with respect to
\lambda_{\star}
, i.e.,

\nu^{\rm sing}_{\star}
,
\zeta^{\rm sing}_{\star}\equiv 0
on

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
. In particular, the inequality (
3.97
)
implies that

\spt\zeta_{\star}=\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

and that

\nu_{\star}\leq 2{\rm K}_{\beta}\,\zeta_{\star},

as measures in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
. Therefore, we can
really study the properties of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
using
\zeta_{\star}

rather than
\nu_{\star}
.

As immediate consequences of the absolute continuity of
\nu_{\star}
,
\zeta_{\star}
with
respect to
\lambda_{\star}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

(in other words, of the fact that
\nu^{\rm sing}_{\star}
,
\zeta^{\rm sing}_{\star}\equiv 0
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
), we have the following stronger versions of
Proposition
3.15

and of Lemma
3.16
.

Theorem 3.20
.

We have the identities

(3.100)

2\zeta_{\star}=\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\perp,\perp}-\mathbbm{m}_{\star,||,||},\qquad\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\perp,||}=0,

where the measures
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\perp,\perp}
,
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,||,||}
,
and
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,\perp,||}
are defined
in (
3.68
), (
3.69
).

Proof.

The identities in (
3.100
) follow both from
Proposition
3.15
and the fact
that
\nu_{\star}^{\rm sing}
,
\zeta_{\star}^{\rm sing}
are identically
zero on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
,
along with the trivial observation that

-2\nu_{\star}\leq\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}\leq 2\nu_{\star}

for any
(j,\,k)\in\{1,\,2\}^{2}
.
∎

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{e}_{2})
be any orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We have the identity

(3.101)

\omega_{\star}=-2e^{-2i\gamma_{\star}}\zeta_{\star},

where
\gamma_{\star}(x)\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{2},\,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
denotes
the angle between
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and the tangent vector
\mathbf{e}_{x}
to
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

at
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
.

Proof.

The lemma follows immediately by
combining Lemma
3.16

with (
3.13
), (
3.48
),
Proposition
3.18
, and (
3.100
).
∎

4
Proof of Theorem
B
and Theorem
C

As an easy consequence of
Proposition
3.18
, Corollary
3.14
,
and Theorem
3.20
(with the aid
of Proposition
3.21
),
we conclude that the limiting
measure
\zeta_{\star}
is the weight measure
of a countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable varifold

\mathbb{V}_{\star}:=\mathbf{v}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
in
\Omega
,
which is also stationary in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
. This gives further structure to the set

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, which turns out
to be locally a union of segments with locally constant densities.
In addition, we are able to compute the first variation of

\mathbb{V}_{\star}
.
As a consequence, we obtain the proof of Theorem
B

and of Theorem
C
.

Varifolds

For the reader’s convenience, we briefly recall the concepts of
varifold
,

rectifiable varifold
,

first variation of a varifold
, and
stationary varifold
.
We refer to
[
20
]
for a comprehensive treatment of varifolds.

A
k
-varifold
\mathbb{V}

on an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a Radon measure
on
G_{k}(U):=U\times G(n,\,k)
, where
G(n,\,k)

denotes the Grassmanian manifold of
k
-dimensional
planes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The
weight measure

\left\|\mathbb{V}\right\|
of
\mathbb{V}

is the Radon measure defined by setting

\int_{U}\phi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\left\|\mathbb{V}\right\|(x):=\int_{G_{k}(U)}\phi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{V}(x,\,S),

for all
\phi\in C_{c}(U)
.
A
k
-varifold is
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-rectifiable

(or
k
-rectifiable
, for short)
if there exist a
countably
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-rectifiable set
\mathfrak{S}
and
a locally
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-integrable function

\theta\colon\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
(called
density function

or
multiplicity
) so that

\int_{G_{k}(U)}\varphi(x,\,S)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{V}(x,\,S)=\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\varphi(x,\,\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S})\,\theta(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{k}(x)

for all
\varphi\in C_{c}(G_{k}(U))
, where
\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}
denotes
the approximate tangent space to
\mathfrak{S}
at
x\in\mathfrak{S}
.
Thus, if
\mathbb{V}
is
k
-rectifiable, then

\left\|\mathbb{V}\right\|=\theta\mathscr{H}^{k}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}.

Two pairs
(\mathfrak{S},\,\theta)
and

(\mathfrak{S}^{\prime},\,\theta^{\prime})
identify the same
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-rectifiable varifold
if and only if the symmetric difference
\mathfrak{S}\Delta\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}

is a
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-null set and
\theta=\theta^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{H}^{k}
-almost all of

\mathfrak{S}\cap\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
.
These conditions define an equivalence relation,
so that rectifiable varifolds are actually equivalence classes of
pairs
(\mathfrak{S},\,\theta)
.
We will write
\mathbb{V}=\mathbf{v}(\mathfrak{S},\,\theta)

for the rectifiable varifold carried by
\mathfrak{S}

with density function
\theta

(see e.g.
[
20
, Chapter 4]
).
The
first variation
of a varifold
\mathbb{V}

is the distribution
\delta\mathbb{V}
in
U
satisfying

\delta\mathbb{V}(\mathbf{X}):=\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\div_{\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}}\mathbf{X}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{H}^{k}(x)

for all
\mathbf{X}\in C^{1}_{c}(U,\,\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If it happens that
\delta{\mathbb{V}}(\mathbf{X})=0

for any
\mathbf{X}\in C^{1}_{c}(U,\,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\mathbb{V}
is said to be
stationary

in
U
.

Theorem 4.1
.

The pair
(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
is
a representative of a countably

\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable varifold in
\Omega
whose
weight measure is
\zeta_{\star}
. In addition, the varifold

\mathbb{V}_{\star}:=\mathbf{v}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
is stationary
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps. First, we formalise the fact, already
implied by the results in the above sections, that

(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
is a representative of
a countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable
varifold in
\Omega
. Second, following the argument
of
[
6
, Theorem 1.5]
, we show that it is
indeed stationary as a varifold in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

(i.e., with respect to variations supported in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
).

Step 1

(Rectifiability)
.

By Proposition
3.8
,

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
is a countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable set.
By Proposition
3.18
,

\zeta_{\star}
is
absolutely continuous with respect to the measure

\lambda_{\star}=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
as measures in

\Omega
, with associated
density
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
.
It then follows from the very definition of countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable
varifold (e.g.,
[
20
, Chapter 4]
) that the pair

(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})

is a representative of a countably
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-rectifiable
varifold in
\Omega
whose weight measure is precisely
\zeta_{\star}
.

Step 2

(Stationarity)
.

We argue exactly as in
[
6
, Section 11]
.
The key point is proving that the ‘modified Cauchy-Riemann
relations’ (
3.61
) are, in fact, equivalent to the
stationarity condition

(4.1)

\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\div_{\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\mathbf{X}\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta_{\star}=0

for any
\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\,X_{2})\in C^{1}_{c}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star},\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
where (by definition)

(4.2)

\div_{\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\mathbf{X}(x)=\left(\mathbf{e}_{x}\cdot\nabla\mathbf{X}(x)\right)\cdot\mathbf{e}_{x}

at any
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
.

To this purpose, we consider the measure

\mathbf{N}_{\star}:=2\zeta_{\star}-\mathbbm{m}_{\star,2,2}+\mathbbm{m}_{\star,1,1},

For any
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
, the
unit tangent vector
\mathbf{e}_{x}
can be decomposed along the Cartesian frame

(\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{e}_{2})
as

\mathbf{e}_{x}=\cos\gamma(x)\mathbf{e}_{1}+\sin\gamma(x)\mathbf{e}_{2},

where
\gamma(x)
denotes the angle between
\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{e}_{x}

(possibly flipped, so that
\gamma(x)\in[-\pi/2,\,\pi/2]
).
Plugging this identity in (
4.2
), we obtain

\begin{split}\div_{\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\mathbf{X}(x)=&\cos^{2}\gamma(x)\frac{\partial X_{1}}{\partial x_{1}}(x)+\sin^{2}\gamma(x)\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial x_{2}}(x)\\
&+\sin\gamma(x)\cos\gamma(x)\left[\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial x_{1}}(x)+\frac{\partial X_{1}}{\partial x_{2}}(x)\right],\end{split}

so that the stationarity condition (
4.1
) can
be recast in the form (cf.
[
6
, (11.2)]
)

(4.3)

\left\langle\zeta_{\star},\cos^{2}\gamma(x)\frac{\partial X_{1}}{\partial x_{1}}+\sin^{2}\gamma(x)\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial x_{2}}+\sin\gamma(x)\cos\gamma(x)\left[\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial x_{1}}+\frac{\partial X_{1}}{\partial x_{2}}\right]\right\rangle=0.

On the other hand, in view of
Proposition
3.21
,
we may rewrite

\mathbf{N}_{\star}=4\sin^{2}(\gamma)\zeta_{\star},

so that, by Theorem
3.20
,
the modified Cauchy-Riemann relations (
3.61
) can be rewritten in the form

(4.4)

\begin{cases}&-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}(\sin(2\gamma)\zeta_{\star})=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}(1+\cos(2\gamma)\zeta_{\star}),\\
&-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}(\sin(2\gamma)\zeta_{\star})=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}(1-\cos(2\gamma)\zeta_{\star}).\end{cases}

Integrating (
4.3
) by parts in the sense of distributions
(recalling that
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
can be chosen independently of each other)
and combining the
result with (
4.4
), the conclusion follows.
∎

We now take advantage of the stationarity of

\mathbb{V}_{\star}

in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
to compute its first variation as a varifold in
\Omega
.
Before stating the result, we establish some further notation. First, we shall
denote
\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}
the first variation of

\mathbb{V}_{\star}
, taken in the sense of distributions
in
\Omega
. Next, we recall that
\spt\mu_{\star}
is a finite set, with cardinality
N_{\star}\in\mathbb{N}

and we denote
a_{j}
, for
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}

and
n_{\star}\leq N_{\star}
, the points of
\spt\mu_{\star}
lying in
\Omega
.
Finally, we write
\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\dots,\,a_{n_{\star}})

for the gradient of the renormalised energy with respect to its
j
-th variable.
We recall that, from
[
4
, Theorem VII.4 and Theorem VIII.3]

and
[
17
, Theorem 5.1]
,

\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\dots,\,a_{n_{\star}})
satisfies

(4.5)

\mathbf{e}\cdot\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\dots,\,a_{n_{\star}})=\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\partial_{\mathbf{e}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{Q}_{\star}-\frac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol{\nu}}\cdot\mathbf{e})\left|\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\star}\right|^{2}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}s

for any constant vector field
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
where
\rho>0
is any radius small enough that,
for any
j
,
k\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}
, there holds

\overline{B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\cap\overline{B_{\rho}(a_{k})}=\emptyset
whenever
j\neq k
.

We are now ready for the proof of Theorem
C
. For the
reader’s convenience, we recall its statement below.

Theorem 4.2
.

The first variation
\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}
of

\mathbb{V}_{\star}:=\mathbf{v}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})

as a varifold in
\Omega
is the
distribution in
\Omega
defined by

(4.6)

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\star}}z_{j}\cdot\mathbf{X}(a_{j}),

for any
\mathbf{X}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
where

(4.7)

z_{j}:=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\dots,\,a_{n_{\star}}),

for any
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}
.

Proof.

We begin by observing that, since
\spt\mu_{\star}
is a finite set and

\mathbb{V}_{\star}:=\mathbf{v}(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
is stationary in

\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
by Theorem
4.1
,
it suffices to work at fixed
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}

and it is enough to consider vector fields
\mathbf{X}=(X_{1},\,X_{2})

compactly supported near
\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\Omega
, i.e.,

\mathbf{X}\in C_{c}^{\infty}\left(\cup_{j=1}^{n_{\star}}B_{s}(a_{j}),\,\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
,
for some small
s>0
.
In fact, by linearity of the first variation it
is enough to consider vector fields of the form

\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{e}\varphi
, for
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
a constant vector field and

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{s}(a_{j}))
such that

(4.8)

\varphi(x)=\varphi(\left|x\right|),\qquad\varphi(a_{j})=1\quad\mbox{in }B_{s/2}(a_{j}),\qquad\left|\varphi^{\prime}(x)\right|\lesssim 1/s,

for any
x\in\spt\varphi
.
Here and in the rest of the proof, we assume that

s>0
is any radius small enough that
B_{s}(a_{j})\subset\!\subset\Omega

and
B_{s}(a_{j})
does not contain points of
\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\Omega
other than

a_{j}
.

Step 1
.

We let

(4.9)

\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X}):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\left(\varepsilon\left|\partial_{1}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\partial_{1}X_{1}+\varepsilon\partial_{1}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{2}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{1}X_{2}+\partial_{2}X_{1})\right.\\
\left.+\varepsilon\left|\partial_{2}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\partial_{2}X_{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\div\mathbf{X}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x,\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\div\mathbf{X}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x

for any
\mathbf{X}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and we claim that

(4.10)

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{I}_{\star}\qquad\mbox{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Proof of (
4.10
).

To prove the claim, we first use that, by (
3.45
),
the various terms on the right-hand side of the
definition of
\mathbb{I}_{\star}
have a limit in the sense of distributions in

\Omega
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, so that,
when
\mathbb{I}_{\star}
is applied to
\mathbf{X}
, we can write (dropping the subscript
\star

for the measure
\mathbbm{m}_{\star,j,k}
for ease)

\begin{split}\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=&\int_{\Omega}\partial_{1}X_{1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbbm{m}_{1,1}+\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{1}X_{2}+\partial_{2}X_{1})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbbm{m}_{1,2}+\int_{\Omega}\partial_{2}X_{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbbm{m}_{2,2}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{1}X_{1}+\partial_{2}X_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mathbbm{m}_{1,1}+\mathbbm{m}_{1,2})-\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{1}X_{1}+\partial_{2}X_{2})\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta_{\star}.\end{split}

Now, we pass to the moving frame associated with
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
and we
rewrite in this basis the right-hand side of
\mathbb{I}_{\star}
.
To this purpose, for any good point
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
,
we let
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{x}
be a
unit tangent vector to
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
at
x
and we write
\gamma(x)
for the
angle formed by
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}(x)
with the
canonical orthonormal frame
(\mathbf{e}_{1},\,\mathbf{e}_{2})
. Up to flipping
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}(x)
if necessary, we
may assume that
\gamma(x)\in[-\pi/2,\,\pi/2]
, so that we can write

\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}(x)=\mathbf{e}_{1}\cos\gamma(x)+\mathbf{e}_{2}\sin\gamma(x),\\
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(x)=-\mathbf{e}_{1}\sin\gamma(x)+\mathbf{e}_{2}\cos\gamma(x),\end{cases}

where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\perp}
. By elementary computations and
recalling from Theorem
3.20
that

\begin{cases}2\zeta_{\star}=\mathbbm{m}_{\perp,\perp}-\mathbbm{m}_{||,||}=\mathbbm{m}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}-\mathbbm{m}_{{\boldsymbol{\tau}},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}},\\
\mathbbm{m}_{\perp,||,||}=\mathbbm{m}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}}=0,\end{cases}

it follows that

(4.11)

\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=-2\int_{\Omega}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\mathbf{X}\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta_{\star}=-2\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\div_{\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\mathbf{X}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta_{\star}(x),

because, in view of Proposition
3.19
and of item
5

of Proposition
3.18
,

\zeta_{\star}\ll\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
in
\Omega
. Thus, the claim follows
from (
4.11
) and the definition of first variation of
a varifold.
∎

Step 2
.

For any
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and for any
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}
,
there holds

(4.12)

\begin{split}-\mathbf{e}\cdot\nabla_{a_{j}}&\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}})\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{e}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}(\mathbf{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right).\end{split}

Proof of (
4.12
).

The proof of (
4.12
) is almost immediate.
Indeed, since

\zeta_{*}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star})={\mbox{(}weak)}^{*}{\mbox{-}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})

by Lemma
3.1
, (
4.12
) follows
from the stress-energy identity (
1.22
)
with the choices
G=B_{\rho}(a_{j})
and
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{e}\chi
, where
\chi

is a smooth-cut off function such that
\chi\equiv 1
in a neighbourhood
of
B_{\rho}(a_{j})
,
and from (
4.5
).
∎

Step 3
.

There exist
z_{1},\,\dots,z_{n_{\star}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such
that (
4.6
) holds, i.e., such that

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\star}}z_{j}\cdot\mathbf{X}(a_{j})

for any vector field
\mathbf{X}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Proof of (
4.6
).

We begin by observing that
for any
\mathbf{X}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
and any

\mathbf{Y}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
vector fields
such that

(4.13)

\mathbf{X}(a_{j})=\mathbf{Y}(a_{j}),\qquad\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\spt\mathbf{X}=\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\spt\mathbf{Y}=\{a_{j}\}.

there holds

(4.14)

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{Y}).

To prove (
4.14
), we first notice that,
by linearity, we may assume
\mathbf{Y}=0
. Let
s>0
be as small
as specified at the beginning of the proof, and
assume that

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,\,s))
satisfies (
4.8
).
Then, by linearity of the first variation,

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}((1-\varphi)\mathbf{X})+\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\varphi\mathbf{X})=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\varphi\mathbf{X}),

where the second equality follows
because
(\mathfrak{S}_{\star},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
is stationary
in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
. On the other hand, by definition,

\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}\div_{\mathrm{T}_{x}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}}(\varphi\mathbf{X})\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta_{\star}

and, by the bound on
\varphi^{\prime}
in (
4.8
),

\left\|\nabla(\varphi\mathbf{X})\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{s}(a_{j}))}\lesssim\frac{1}{s}\left\|\mathbf{X}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{s}(a_{j}))}+\left\|\nabla\mathbf{X}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{s}(a_{j}))}\lesssim 1,

because
\mathbf{X}(a_{j})=0
by (
4.13
). Thus,

\left|\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})\right|\lesssim\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B_{s}(a_{j})}\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta_{\star}\to 0\qquad\mbox{as }s\to 0

because
\zeta_{\star}(\spt\mu_{\star})=0
by (
3.94
).
This shows that (
4.14
) holds for any two vector fields

\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Y}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying (
4.13
).
On the other hand,
by letting
a_{j}
vary in
\spt\mu_{\star}\cap\Omega
and using appropriate
cut-off functions as in the above, it follows from the stationarity
of
\mathbb{V}_{\star}

in
\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
that

(4.15)

\mathbf{X}(a_{j})=\mathbf{Y}(a_{j})\quad\mbox{for any }j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}\implies\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{Y}).

Finally, let
s>0
be again as in the above, let

j
,
k\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}
be any two indexes such that

j\neq k
. Let
\mathbf{V}_{1}
,
\mathbf{V}_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{s}(0),\,\mathbb{R}^{2})

be any two vector fields with
\mathbf{V}_{1}(0)=\mathbf{e}_{1}

and
\mathbf{V}_{2}(0)=\mathbf{e}_{2}
. Given
\mathbf{X}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega,\,\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
define

\overline{\mathbf{X}}(x):=\mathbf{X}(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\star}}\sum_{k=1}^{2}X_{k}(a_{j})\mathbf{V}_{k}(x-a_{j}).

Clearly,
\overline{\mathbf{X}}(a_{j})=0
for any
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}

and thus, from (
4.15
), we have
