0=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\overline{\mathbf{X}})=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})-\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\star}}\sum_{k=1}^{2}X_{k}(a_{j})\mathbf{V}_{k}(x-a_{j})

Therefore, defining
z_{j}
by setting

(4.16)

(z_{j})_{k}:=\delta\mathbb{V}_{\star}(\mathbf{V}_{k}(\cdot-a_{j}))

for any
k\in\{1,\,2\}
and any
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}

concludes the proof of (
4.6
).
∎

Step 4
.

For the same vectors
z_{j}
defined by (
4.16
), there holds

(4.17)

\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{\star}})=-2z_{j}

for any
j\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n_{\star}\}
.

Proof of (
4.17
).

As observed at the beginning of the proof, we can restrict
to vector fields of the form
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{e}\varphi
, where

\varphi\in C^{\infty}([0,\,s)
satisfies
\varphi\equiv 1

for
\rho
, say, in
[0,\,s/2)
and
\left|\varphi^{\prime}\right|\lesssim 1/s

and where
s>0
is small enough
that
B_{s}(a_{j})\subset\!\subset\Omega
does not contain points of

\spt\mu_{\star}
other than
a_{j}
.

Keeping the above in mind, for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
and any
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we let

J_{\varepsilon}(\rho):=\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{e}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}(\mathbf{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right).

We already know from Step
1
and Step
3

that
\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=-2\mathbf{e}\cdot z_{j}
if
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{e}\varphi
.
Upon using the explicit expression for
\mathbf{X}
, we can compute

\partial_{i}X_{k}=e_{k}\varphi^{\prime}(\left|x-a_{j}\right|)\nu_{i},\qquad\div\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\varphi^{\prime}(\left|x-a_{j}\right|),

where
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(x)=\frac{x-a_{j}}{\left|x-a_{j}\right|}
, we may
write the integral on the right-hand side of (
4.9
)
in a more explicit form, that is,

(4.18)

\begin{split}\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{s}(a_{j})}\varphi^{\prime}(\left|x-a_{j}\right|)\left(\varepsilon\partial_{\mathbf{e}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\partial_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon(\mathbf{e}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\right)\right)\\
&=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{s}\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)J_{\varepsilon}(\rho)\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho,\end{split}

where the second line follows from Fubini’s theorem and the
definition of
J_{\varepsilon}
. From Step
2
, we
already know that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}J_{\varepsilon}(\rho)=J_{\star}

pointwise for
\rho\in[0,\,s)
, where
J_{\star}
does not
depend on
\rho
and, in fact,

(4.19)

J_{\star}=-\mathbf{e}\cdot\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\dots,\,a_{n_{\star}}),

as it is easily seen from (
4.12
). On the other hand,
recalling item
5
of Theorem
A
,
Lemma
3.2
,
and (
26
), we have

\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\to\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{\star},\qquad\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\left|\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}\right|-1)\to\kappa_{\star},\qquad\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}f_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon},\,\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon})\to 0

strongly in
L^{p}(B_{s}(a_{j})\setminus B_{s/2}(a_{j}))
,
for any
p
with
1\leq p<\infty
.
This implies that
J_{\varepsilon}\to J_{\star}

strongly in
L^{p}((s/2,\,s))
for any
p
with
1\leq p<\infty
,
and recalling that
\varphi(\rho)=1

for any
\rho\in(0,\,s/2)
, we can pass to the limit
inside the integral on the right-hand side of the first line
in (
4.18
) to obtain

\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=J_{\star}\int_{0}^{s}\varphi^{\prime}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s=-J_{\star}.

Thus, from (
4.19
) it follows that

(4.20)

\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbb{I}_{\star}(\mathbf{e}\varphi)=-2\mathbf{e}\cdot z_{j}=\mathbf{e}\cdot\nabla_{a_{j}}\mathbb{W}_{\star}(a_{1},\,\dots,\,a_{n_{\star}}),

and we obtain (
4.17
) because
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
was
arbitrary.
∎

Step 5

(Conclusion)
.

Since (
4.6
) was already proved
in Step
3
and (
4.7
) is just a
restatement of (
4.17
), the desired
conclusion follows by combining (
4.6
)
and (
4.17
).
∎

Joining Theorem
4.1
, Theorem
3.3
,
Proposition
3.19
, a classical
results of Allard and Almgren
[
1
]
on the structure of stationary

1
-varifolds with strictly positive density, and
[
6
, Theorem 1.3]
,
we obtain in Theorem
4.3
the refined structure properties of

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
contained in item
4
of Theorem
B
.

Theorem 4.3
.

The set
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}=\spt\nu_{\star}\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

is locally around
\mathscr{H}^{1}
-almost every point a union of segments with
locally constant densities.
In fact, for every compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}

and any
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
,
there exist a unit vector
\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}}
and positive numbers
\rho_{0}
,
c_{x_{0}}

so that
B(x_{0},\,\rho_{0})\subset\!\subset K
,

(4.21)

\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap B(x_{0},\,\rho_{0})=(x_{0}-\rho_{0}\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}},\,x_{0}+\rho_{0}\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}})

and

(4.22)

\zeta_{\star}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}B(x_{0},\,\rho_{0})=c_{x_{0}}\left(\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(x_{0}-\rho_{0}\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}},\,x_{0}+\rho_{0}\mathbf{e}_{x_{0}})\right).

The number
c_{x_{0}}=\mathfrak{v}_{\star}(x_{0})
is bounded below by the
ratio
\eta_{\star,K}\,/\,2{\rm K}_{\beta}
, where

\eta_{\star,K}
is given by (
3.9
)
and
{\rm K}_{\beta}
is the number in (
3.99
).

Proof.

Let
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
be any compact set and
let
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}=\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap K
.
Recall that, because of item
4
of
Theorem
3.3
,
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
is also characterised
as the set on which
\mathfrak{e}_{\star}\geq\eta_{\star,K}
, where

\eta_{\star,K}
is given by (
3.9
).

Step 1

(General local structure)
.

From item
2
of Theorem
3.3
and from
Proposition
3.19
, we know that,
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
, the density
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
is bounded
both from above and from below, by a strictly positive number. More specifically,
the lower bound is given by
\eta_{\star,K}/2K_{\beta}
,
by (
3.6
) and (
3.99
).
We are therefore in a position to
apply
[
1
, Theorem in Section 3]
with

V=(\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K},\,\mathfrak{v}_{\star})
.
Calling
S_{V,K}
the set of points of
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}

around which the density
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
is not constant,
we have
\|V\|=\zeta_{\star}(S_{V,K})=0

by
[
1
, Remark below the Theorem in Section 3]
,
so that we have as well
\mathscr{H}^{1}(S_{V,K})=0
,
and that

\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\setminus S_{V}
is, in fact, a union of
segments open relative to
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
,
on each of which
\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
is constant, as claimed.
Since
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}
can be look at as the countable union
of the sets
\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}
over an(y) ascending sequence of compact sets

K_{n}\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
,
it follows that

\mathscr{H}^{1}(S_{V})=0
, where
S_{V}:=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S_{V,K}

does not depend on the chosen sequence
\{K_{n}\}
.

Step 2

(Refined local structure)
.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\star,K}\setminus\mathfrak{E}_{\star}
.
Reasoning word-for-word as in
[
6
, Section 10]
,
one may improve on Step
1
to show
that (
4.21
) and (
4.22
) hold.
Being the argument totally analogous to that in
[
6
]
,
with no but notational changes, we address the reader
to
[
6
]
for full details.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

By applying the change of variables
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon}

described in
[
9
, Section 3.1]
(which
amounts to project
\mathbf{M}_{\varepsilon}
on the eigenframe
of
\mathbf{Q}_{\varepsilon}
) and then blowing-up, the system (
9
) becomes

(4.23)

-\Delta\mathbf{u}+\nabla_{\mathbf{u}}h(\mathbf{u})=0,

where
\mathbf{u}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is exactly the same
potential as defined in
[
9
, Section 3.1]
. In particular, the equation
for the component
u_{2}
reads

(4.24)

-\Delta u_{2}+\left(\left|\mathbf{u}\right|^{2}-1+\sqrt{2}\beta\right)u_{2}=0.

Multiplying (
4.24
) by
u_{2}
and assuming that

\sqrt{2}\beta\geq 1
, it is readily seen that
\left|u_{2}\right|^{2}
must be a subharmonic function. Therefore, bounded solutions of (
4.23
) with
\sqrt{2}\beta\geq 1
must have constant
u_{2}
, and in fact

u_{2}\equiv 0

(because 0 is the only constant solving (
4.24
)).
Consequently, every bounded entire solution of (
4.23
)
is, in fact, one-dimensional. Recalling the well-known integrality
result in
[
16
]
, this suggests, at least formally
and at least if
\sqrt{2}\beta\geq 1
, that the limiting varifold

\mathbb{V}_{\star}
is
integral
, i.e., the density function

\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
is integer-valued. However,
we will not further
pursue this issue in this paper, deferring rigorous
analysis of it to future work.

We have now all the ingredients to complete the proof of Theorem
B
.

Proof of Theorem
B
.

The statement
\zeta_{\star}=\mathfrak{v}_{\star}\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathfrak{S}_{\star}

is already contained in Proposition
3.18
.
Combining
Theorem
3.3
and Proposition
3.19
,
we obtain the boundedness from below of the density

\mathfrak{v}_{\star}
of
\zeta_{\star}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{\star}\cap K
,
for any compact set
K\subset\Omega\setminus\spt\mu_{\star}
, with a constant

c_{K}
depending only on
K
,
\beta
, and the energy bound
\mathcal{E}_{0}

in Proposition
2.2
.
The boundedness from above, again with a constant

C_{K}
depending on
K
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
,
follows from (
3.95
)
and Proposition
2.2
.
The conclusion of
Theorem
B
now follows from
Theorem
4.2
and Theorem
4.3
.
∎
